We find that these density profiles, for both RTPs and Brownian particles, exhibit a nontrivial space-time structure. We also study stationary current fluctuations on an interval connected to reservoirs. Finally, we analyze the MFT equations perturbatively in the interaction. In both the infinite-line and reservoir geometries, we obtain explicit first-order corrections to the current generating functions for short-range interacting RTPs and Brownian particles.

### Paper [156]
Title: Mechano-chemical control of enzymatic activity in crowded environments
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03239
Authors: Michalis Chatzittofi, Luca Cocconi, Jakob Metson, Ramin Golestanian
Abstract: Macromolecular crowding has long been known to alter biochemical equilibria and enzyme kinetics, with reported catalytic activities that may increase, decrease, or vary non-monotonically with crowder concentration. Classical Michaelis--Menten kinetics does not by itself identify which of these responses should occur for a conformationally active enzyme. Here, we develop a minimal non-equilibrium mechano-chemical theory for a fueled enzyme embedded in a bath of passive crowders and uncover a fluctuation-induced effective force dipole acting on the enzyme subunits, akin to a generalized form of dispersion or depletion interaction. Since the preferred conformation of the model enzyme varies through the catalytic cycle, the fluctuation-induced mechanical load affects the relevant kinetic activation barriers and is sufficient to produce both monotonic inhibition and non-monotonic modification of turnover. The framework provides a thermodynamically consistent route for linking crowded environments to catalytic currents, suggesting how mechanical design principles could be leveraged to control enzymatic pathways.

### Paper [157]
Title: Establishing N\'eel order in the square-lattice antiferromagnetic Heisenberg model: a bootstrap proof
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03241
Authors: Nisarg Chadha, Taige Wang, Pavel A. Nosov, Michael G. Scheer, Eslam Khalaf
Abstract: Rigorously establishing spontaneous symmetry breaking (SSB) in the thermodynamic limit is generally challenging. This is exemplified by the two-dimensional (2D) $S = 1/2$ antiferromagnetic Heisenberg model where the existence of N\'eel order is not rigorously established despite overwhelming evidence from a variety of methods. We show that a bootstrap approach can be used to rigorously establish SSB and use it to prove the existence of N\'eel order in the 2D $S = 1/2$ XXZ model by deriving a strictly positive lower bound on the N\'eel order parameter for a set of discrete values of the anisotropy parameter $\Delta$ in the range $[0,2]$ including the isotropic (Heisenberg) point $\Delta = 1$. Our approach employs a generalization of the Kennedy--Lieb--Shastry inequalities, which use an infrared bound to reduce the existence of long-range order to bounds on certain finite-distance correlators. We compute these bounds for all sufficiently large systems with periodic boundary conditions using the bootstrap. An important ingredient in our proof is a novel method for obtaining exactly certified variational upper bounds on the energy density in the thermodynamic limit from matrix product states. Our approach can be readily generalized to other local lattice models for which infrared susceptibility bounds can be obtained.

### Paper [158]
Title: Magnetic field as a tuning knob for edge mode interaction in integer quantum Hall interferometers
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03284
Authors: Ning Ma, Tev\v{z} Lotri\v{c}, Kenji Watanabe, Takashi Taniguchi, Steven H. Simon, Mitali Banerjee
Abstract: The quantum Hall interferometer has been shown to be a powerful tool for probing both the nature of the chiral modes present at the edges of these systems and the interactions between them. Strong inter-edge mode interactions can lead the system into a regime where the period of the interferometer signal corresponds to an insertion of a half-quantum flux h/2e, rather than the standard flux quantum h/e. Using a highly tunable bilayer graphene device, we are able to measure the prevalence of both the h/e-period and the doubled-frequency regimes as a function of magnetic field, providing insight into the microscopic nature of the doubling mechanism. Furthermore, we are able to push our device to a regime where double-frequency oscillations coexist with a superimposed period-h/e pattern associated with the inner chiral modes farther from the edge. We argue that this regime has a natural interpretation in a picture of the frequency doubling being caused by strong capacitive couplings between the edge modes.

### Paper [159]
Title: Membrane particle wrapping across methods and shapes
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03310
Authors: Pietro Sillano, Haochun Sun, B. Yolda\c{s} Cinemre, Timon Idema
Abstract: Understanding the passive wrapping of adhesive particles by lipid membranes is a crucial step in untangling more complex endocytotic processes. However, despite the variety of computational approaches used to study passive endocytosis, a direct comparison of results across different modeling methods has never been performed. In this work, we present a comparative analysis of three distinct frameworks: numerical solutions of the axisymmetric membrane shape equation, a particle-based molecular dynamics model (MesoMem), and an energy-based triangulated surface model (FreeDTS). First, we validated our numerical solver for spherical particles against established literature and extended the implementation to include closed vesicle membranes and non-spherical particles. Next, we studied the same system with our two simulation methods, and found that the phase plots obtained from our numerical solution agree with those from our simulations. We then extended our study to anisotropic particles. By tracking engulfment pathways in MesoMem and FreeDTS, we observed a dynamic rotational transition where non-spherical particles initially bind in a horizontal ('submarine') orientation and subsequently rotate to a vertical ('rocket') orientation to complete wrapping - a behavior impossible to capture with standard axisymmetric models. Lastly, we considered the wrapping of soft vesicles with FreeDTS. Our simulations of a deformable, fixed-connectivity particle show that stronger adhesion promotes wrapping, whereas increasing membrane tension suppresses it. Complete engulfment is accompanied by a substantial reduction in particle volume, suggesting that particle compressibility provides an additional pathway for wrapping.

### Paper [160]
Title: Electron--phonon cooling noise in monolayer and bilayer graphene
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03316
Authors: Muhammad Waqas Haseeb, Edvin G. Idrisov
Abstract: We study the electron--phonon heat-current fluctuations in monolayer and Bernal bilayer graphene coupled to longitudinal acoustic phonons. To do this, we construct a symmetric heat-current operator and evaluate the non-equilibrium current--current correlator at finite frequency for unequal electron and phonon temperatures. The heat-current vertices then involve the probe energy combined with twice the phonon energy, and the nonequilibrium spectral density must be calculated directly rather than inferred from a dynamic thermal fluctuation--dissipation relation. Further, we obtain full analytical expressions in the Bloch--Gr\"uneisen (BG) and Equipartition (EP) regimes, derive their low-frequency limits, and compare them with a complete doped-intraband on-shell calculations for monolayer graphene. The numerical results connect the two temperature regimes and show how finite-frequency noise changes out of equilibrium. For representative parameters, the corrections become percent-level in the upper-gigahertz to sub-terahertz range. It is worth mentioning that in equilibrium, the results satisfy the zero-frequency thermal fluctuation--dissipation relation(FDT) and reproduce the well established cooling-power and thermal-conductance laws.

### Paper [161]
Title: Piezogyration and elastogyration in ternary chalcopyrites from first principles
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03365
Authors: Asier Zabalo, Fernando G\'omez-Ortiz, Aldo H. Romero, Eric Bousquet
Abstract: Natural optical activity arises from the spatial dispersion of the optical response and is described by the rank-two gyration pseudotensor. Its dependence on mechanical stress and strain, quantified by the piezogyration and elastogyration tensors, respectively, has been investigated experimentally for decades. However, a systematic first-principles description of these effects remains largely unexplored. Here we present a first-principles methodology to compute the full piezogyration and elastogyration tensors, and apply it to a set of representative ternary chalcopyrite crystals. The gyration of the unstrained crystal turns out to be a poor predictor of the magnitude of the elastogyration response, which we find to be a property inherited from their parent zinc-blende structures. The elastic compliance tensor further shapes the piezogyration response, with particularly pronounced effects in AgGaS$_2$, a well-known achiral yet optically active crystal. Finally, we explore strain-induced chirality through the trace of the gyration tensor, the only pseudoscalar contribution to the natural optical activity that is linear in the tensor components and encodes information about chirality.

### Paper [162]
Title: Acoustic damping limits superfluid helium as a thermal bath for mechanical resonators
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03405
Authors: Yunhu Huang, Mehri Ebrahimi, Alireza Rashedi, John P. Davis
Abstract: Thermalization is a persistent problem for cryogenic mechanical resonators. The weak supports used to obtain high mechanical quality factors are also poor thermal links, while the optical or microwave fields used for readout and control can heat the resonator above the cryostat temperature. Superfluid helium is therefore an appealing exchange medium: it has excellent low-temperature thermal transport and an inviscid superfluid component, suggesting cooling of mechanical devices without the damping associated with an ordinary fluid. Here we test this idea by immersing a \qty{250}{\micro\meter}-diameter yttrium iron garnet sphere in liquid \textsuperscript{4}He and reading out a \(\sim\qty{12.47}{\mega\hertz}\) mechanical mode using cavity magnomechanics. The mechanical linewidth increases from \qty{6.1}{\hertz} in vacuum to \qty{9.1}{\kilo\hertz} after filling the cavity with liquid helium in the normal state, and reaches \qty{17.5}{\kilo\hertz} deep in the superfluid at \qty{84}{\milli\kelvin}. Thus the mechanical quality factor is reduced by more than three orders of magnitude, and crossing into the superfluid does not recover low dissipation. At the same time, the magnon linewidth remains strongly dependent on microwave power at the lowest temperatures, showing that the sphere is not perfectly anchored to the cryostat despite the large mechanical damping. The result follows from acoustic radiation: the surface motion of the sphere efficiently launches gapless longitudinal sound into the liquid, and a radiation-impedance estimate gives a linewidth of order \qty{25}{\kilo\hertz}. Superfluid helium is therefore not a low-loss thermal bath for mechanical modes that efficiently radiate compression waves. Shear motion, on the other hand, may provide a route around this limitation.

### Paper [163]
Title: Amplitude-conserving frequency demultiplexer based on a spin wave delay line
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03423
Authors: Ga\"el Yann Thiancourt, Thibaut Devolder
Abstract: Propagating spin waves have emerged as promising candidates for signal processing and information transmission. We present a theoretical investigation of the generation, propagation and collection of spin-wave wave packets in micrometer-wide magnetic conduits, highlighting their potential for analogue frequency demultiplexing in space and time. We clarify the roles of the excitation duration and of the spin wave dispersion relation, as well as the influence of the spin-wave linewidth in setting the spatio-temporal evolution of propagating spin-wave wavepackets excited by Gaussian field pulses. We issue rules for the spatio-temporal separation of wavepackets that carry different frequencies. We finally propose an amplitude-conserving frequency demultiplexer based on a spin wave delay line of 60x5 $\mu$m$^2$ footprint.

### Paper [164]
Title: Local and itinerant orbital contributions to the gyromagnetic response of paramagnetic metals
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03426
Authors: Garima Ahuja, Mirco Sastges, Dongwook Go, Shobhana Narasimhan, Stefan Bl\"ugel, Yuriy Mokrousov
Abstract: Orbitronics exploits orbital angular momentum to generate and manipulate angular-momentum currents for controlling magnetisation, offering new approaches to magnetic memory and logic. A quantitative description of this process requires establishing how orbital angular momentum contributes to magnetic moments and, consequently, to the gyromagnetic response that governs magnetisation dynamics. Here, we present a first-principles framework for calculating the effective gyromagnetic tensor (g') of paramagnetic metals, consistently incorporating local and itinerant orbital contributions, spin-orbit coupling, and both state- and occupation-change effects. Applying the framework to V and Pt, we find that orbital paramagnetic responses are comparable to or larger than spin contributions and substantially modify g' from the free-electron value. The atom-centered approximation can underestimate the orbital response by approximately a factor of two, while neglecting occupation changes produces significant errors in the calculated g'. Our calculated g' values for V and Pt agree with experiment, while their pronounced dependence on band filling reveals a route to engineering angular-momentum responses through electronic-structure control. The results establish the modern theory of orbital magnetism as an essential ingredient for describing gyromagnetic response and provide a framework for identifying materials with enhanced orbital functionalities.

### Paper [165]
Title: Polarity-Programmed Topological Gating of Knotting in Spatially Patterned Active Polymers
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03474
Authors: Shiqi Zhou
Abstract: Spatially patterned tangential activity provides a route to program polymer topology through both geometry and propulsion direction. We use Langevin-dynamics simulations of a flexible, self-avoiding linear polymer to determine how active-domain placement and independently assigned propulsion polarities control nonequilibrium knotting. Reversing the polarities of two terminal active blocks switches the steady state from a nearly always-knotted regime to effective knot suppression, changing the knotting probability by more than three orders of magnitude despite identical active-block positions and activity magnitude. Across symmetric terminal two-block architectures, we further identify an approximate zero-one-two free-end-alignment rule: zero, one, and two free-end-aligned active blocks correspond, respectively, to suppressed, intermediate, and high-knotting states. A two-state kinetic decomposition shows that the conditional knot-formation rate changes only by about threefold under polarity reversal, whereas the mean knot lifetime changes by several orders of magnitude, demonstrating that knot survival and passive-region storage dominate the polarity-controlled switch. Block placement provides an additional geometrical gate: moving an active domain from the chain interior to a terminal region strongly enhances both knot formation and survival. Knot-center residence, knotted-arc distributions, and episode-resolved kymographs reveal formation near free-end-aligned active blocks, directed contour migration, tightening, and long-lived retention in passive regions. Together, these results support a source-conveyor-reservoir-sink mechanism in which signed polarity and spatial activity patterning control whether knots are generated, transported, stored, or rapidly lost, providing a route to programmable topological states in nonequilibrium active polymers.

### Paper [166]
Title: Theory of the Ferroaxial Charge Density Wave in 1T-TiSe$_2$
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03478
Authors: Ilaria Maccari, Rafael M. Fernandes, Fernando de Juan, Maria N. Gastiasoro
Abstract: Recent experiments using strain to probe the charge-density wave (CDW) of 1T-TiSe$_2$ have uncovered additional transitions and broken symmetries inside the CDW state. The elastocaloric effect reveals a second transition below $T_\mathrm{CDW}$, which elastoresistivity shows to be associated with ferroaxial order, breaking all vertical mirror symmetries while preserving inversion. In addition, at even lower temperatures, a diverging nematic susceptibility has been reported through elastoresistivity. In this work, we argue that this rich phenomenology cannot be explained by the standard $L_1^-$ CDW order parameter, and instead requires the existence of a secondary CDW order parameter. Based on symmetry, microscopic, and diffraction considerations, we propose this subleading CDW to have $L_2^-$ symmetry. By analyzing the Landau theory of coupled $L_1^-$ and $L_2^-$ orders in the presence of strain, we reproduce the phase boundaries observed in the elastocaloric measurements and the emergence of ferroaxial order below the CDW transition. Moreover, the model supports a third threefold-rotational symmetry breaking transition at lower temperatures, consistent with the reported divergence of the nematic susceptibility.

### Paper [167]
Title: Cage-enforced i-wave altermagnetism in boron-carbon clathrates
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03492
Authors: Meiyan Wang, Li Zhu
Abstract: The spin splitting of an altermagnet arises from a rotation that relates its two magnetic sublattices. In known cases it is fixed by the crystal structure. Here we show that guest-host frameworks separate the two roles. In the boron-carbon clathrates $M$B$_3$C$_3$ the ordered cage removes the translation between the two guest sites and enforces cubic $i$-wave altermagnetism, with six symmetry-enforced nodal planes, for any magnetic guest in the compensated state; strain retunes the class to $d$- or $g$-wave. We demonstrate the platform with GdB$_3$C$_3$: its compensated antiferromagnetic ground state is a narrow-gap semiconductor with spin splitting of 0.19 eV at the conduction-band edge, and its weakly pinned N\'eel vector switches the helicity-resolved optical response on and off according to the magnetic point group. Thermodynamic stability at high pressure and dynamical stability at ambient pressure indicate that it can be made by the route established for this family.

### Paper [168]
Title: Polariton tweezers
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03506
Authors: Nika Gribova, Evgeny Sedov, Alexey Kavokin
Abstract: The non-invasive, deterministic manipulation of charge carriers remains a fundamental challenge in solid-state quantum technologies. Here, we demonstrate the concept of dynamic polariton tweezers capable of trapping and transporting electrons across a two-dimensional electron system via the long-range electrostatic potential of dipolar (d-) exciton-polaritons. By driving a semiconductor microcavity with a specifically engineered, non-collinear three-beam optical pump with TE-polarization, we synthesize a macroscopic polariton lattice with hexagonal symmetry. The spatially periodic field of this d-polariton condensate acts as a reconfigurable trapping landscape for the electrons. We show that introducing targeted frequency detunings between the continuous-wave pumps strictly breaks the spatiotemporal symmetry of the system, converting the stationary macroscopic interference pattern into a dynamically moving conveyor belt. By precisely customizing these frequency detunings, the entire lattice - along with the electrostatically trapped electrons - can be rigidly driven in any arbitrary in-plane direction. This precise phase-matching mechanism bypasses the need for a net kinetic momentum transfer, establishing a robust, optically controlled platform for the long-range routing of charged particles and topological defects in driven-dissipative quantum fluids.

### Paper [169]
Title: The effects of an alternating bias on a single orbital spin impurity
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03507
Authors: Matyas Nachtigall, Jose Reina-G\'alvez, Christoph Wolf, Nicol\'as Lorente
Abstract: Electron spin resonance with the scanning tunneling microscope (ESR-STM) enables coherent manipulation and detection of individual atomic spins with atomic-scale spatial resolution, yet the microscopic mechanism by which the radiofrequency (RF) bias drives the spin remains under debate. We study this problem using a Redfield quantum-master-equation framework in which the oscillating bias enters explicitly through the electronic reservoirs rather than through an effective time-dependent tunneling amplitude. In the semiclassical limit, the RF voltage periodically sweeps the system through its static transport landscape, so that the transport regime depends jointly on the DC bias and RF amplitude. When the RF trajectory approaches or crosses a charge transition, the damping-like torque (DLT) associated with spin-polarized charge transfer is strongly enhanced. By contrast, the field-like torque (FLT) induced by the exchange field remains finite deeper in the Coulomb-blockade (CB) regime because it originates from virtual charge fluctuations. The RF trajectory also averages strongly bias-dependent static quantities over a finite voltage range, smoothing features such as the Lamb shift near the charge thresholds. These results establish the RF amplitude as a control parameter not only for the strength of the ESR drive, but also for the transport regime and the ratio between FLT and DLT.

### Paper [170]
Title: An Intrinsically Gapless Topological Phase with Dipole Symmetry
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03538
Authors: Amogh Anakru, Linhao Li, Zhen Bi
Abstract: We show that the boundary fractionalization familiar from gapped topological insulators can enforce bulk gaplessness in a dipole-conserving system. In a one-dimensional fermion model with charge conservation, dipole conservation, and particle-hole symmetry, we realize an intrinsically gapless symmetry-protected topological (igSPT) phase combining a dipolar Luttinger liquid with half-quantized boundary charges. This boundary structure obstructs a unique gapped bulk preserving these symmetries and lattice translation. Remarkably, the two charge-neutral edge configurations remain exactly degenerate even at finite size due to dipole conservation and particle-hole symmetry. The boundary fractionalization also manifests as shifted conformal towers in the dipole-resolved entanglement spectrum, reflecting distinct conformal boundary conditions of the Luttinger liquid. We support our analysis of the igSPT by studying exactly-solvable limits and performing DMRG computations, establishing its stability for a wide range of tuning parameters.

### Paper [171]
Title: Multiple windows of eigenstate-selective delocalization in a non-Hermitian quasiperiodic lattice
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03562
Authors: Souvik Roy, Santanu K. Maiti
Abstract: We investigate localization in a non-Hermitian quasiperiodic lattice with a real quasiperiodic onsite potential of strength $W$ and a staggered imaginary quasiperiodic modulation of strength $\lambda$. Using the inverse participation ratio (IPR), normalized participation ratio (NPR), fractal dimension $D_2$, transfer-matrix Lyapunov exponent, and wave-packet dynamics, we find a strongly non-monotonic and state-dependent response. At fixed $\lambda$, varying $W$ yields two distinct delocalization windows, whereas varying $\lambda$ at fixed $W$ produces a single re-entrant delocalization window. These regimes arise within selected eigenstate sectors, where localized states coexist with extended or critical states, leading to mobility-edge-like behavior. The corresponding suppression of the Lyapunov exponent and enhancement of the dynamical RMS spreading are consistent with the IPR, NPR, and $D_2$ results. Finite-size scaling confirms the persistence of the re-entrant features, while the complex-energy spectrum resolves their state-selective structure. The resulting phase boundaries delineate distinct localization regimes in the $(W,\lambda)$ plane. Our results show that the interplay between quasiperiodicity and a staggered non-Hermitian modulation sharing the same quasiperiodic spatial structure can generate re-entrant, state-dependent localization, with implications for localization and dynamics in non-Hermitian quasiperiodic lattices.

### Paper [172]
Title: Internal friction and frequency-dependent conductivity in the bivariate site energy model for the mixed alkali effect in glasses
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03591
Authors: Justus Leiber, Sven Lohmann, Quinn Emilia Fischer, Philipp Maass
Abstract: The mixed alkali effect is fundamental to understanding ion transport in glasses. It manifests itself in strong changes of various ionic transport properties when one type of ion is successively replaced by another type. Recently, we have developed a new theory of thermally activated hopping in bivariate random site energy landscapes that takes into account site occupation statistics according to generalized Fermi distributions and mixture kinetics in accordance with linear irreversible thermodynamics. The theory was successfully applied to predict the behavior of partial conductivities and their activation energies at low frequencies corresponding to the dc limit. Here we show that the theory is capable to capture the salient features of the mixed alkali effect observed in ac ion conductivities and in the peculiar behavior of the internal friction.

### Paper [173]
Title: Inhomogeneous Synthetic Fluxes in Optical Lattices
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03616
Authors: Soujanya Datta, Kush Saha, Krishanu Roychowdhury
Abstract: Synthetic gauge fields in optical lattices are usually engineered to realize a uniform magnetic flux. Here, we propose a scheme to generate spatially inhomogeneous fluxes and demonstrate how such fluxes reshape the single-particle spectrum. Within Wannier--Stark-assisted tunneling, onsite modulation produces a plaquette flux that depends on the orientation of the static tilt, whereas laser-assisted tunneling (LAT) does not. Using LAT, two coherent Raman processes with different momentum transfers imprint a dimerized Peierls phase that alternates the flux between neighboring plaquettes of a square lattice while keeping the mean flux fixed. A bond-selective Raman phase extends the scheme to the kagome lattice, where a parameter $\xi$ redistributes the flux between triangles and hexagons at fixed flux per unit cell. The Hofstadter--Rammal spectrum of the bipartite square lattice retains chiral symmetry for all $\xi$, whereas the kagome lattice lacks this symmetry, and its flux periodicity and reflection symmetries survive only at special values of $\xi$. As $\xi$ is reduced, the anomalous Landau levels of the kagome singular flat band are compressed toward the flat-band energy and evolve into a degenerate zero-energy manifold at $\xi=0$ with compactly localized states. The dynamical structure factor, accessible through Bragg scattering, resolves this evolution as a Rydberg-like series of peaks whose spectral span tracks the width of the anomalous-level tower and vanishes as $\xi\to0$. We discuss the frequency resolution and temperature required for its observation.

### Paper [174]
Title: Ultrafast X-ray-induced dephasing of valence electrons
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03635
Authors: J. Vonka (PSI Center for Photon Science, Paul Scherrer Institute, Villigen PSI, Switzerland), B. Pedrini (PSI Center for Photon Science, Paul Scherrer Institute, Villigen PSI, Switzerland), J. Ravnik (PSI Center for Photon Science, Paul Scherrer Institute, Villigen PSI, Switzerland), A. Steppke (PSI Center for Photon Science, Paul Scherrer Institute, Villigen PSI, Switzerland, PSI Center for Neutron and Muon Sciences, Paul Scherrer Institute, Villigen PSI, Switzerland), E. Prat (PSI Center for Accelerator Science and Engineering, Paul Scherrer Institute, Villigen PSI, Switzerland), S. Reiche (PSI Center for Accelerator Science and Engineering, Paul Scherrer Institute, Villigen PSI, Switzerland), D. G. Mazzone (PSI Center for Neutron and Muon Sciences, Paul Scherrer Institute, Villigen PSI, Switzerland), P. Anand (PSI Center for Photon Science, Paul Scherrer Institute, Villigen PSI, Switzerland, Laboratory for Solid State Physics and Quantum Center, ETH Z\"urich, Z\"urich, Switzerland), S. Allenspach (PSI Center for Neutron and Muon Sciences, Paul Scherrer Institute, Villigen PSI, Switzerland), Y. Ekinci (PSI Center for Photon Science, Paul Scherrer Institute, Villigen PSI, Switzerland), U. Staub (PSI Center for Photon Science, Paul Scherrer Institute, Villigen PSI, Switzerland), S. -W. Huang (PSI Center for Photon Science, Paul Scherrer Institute, Villigen PSI, Switzerland), M. Sander (PSI Center for Photon Science, Paul Scherrer Institute, Villigen PSI, Switzerland), R. Mankowsky (PSI Center for Photon Science, Paul Scherrer Institute, Villigen PSI, Switzerland), H. T. Lemke (PSI Center for Photon Science, Paul Scherrer Institute, Villigen PSI, Switzerland), G. Aeppli (PSI Center for Photon Science, Paul Scherrer Institute, Villigen PSI, Switzerland, Institut de Physique, EPF Lausanne, Lausanne, Switzerland), S. Gerber (PSI Center for Photon Science, Paul Scherrer Institute, Villigen PSI, Switzerland)
Abstract: Diffract-before-destroy schemes for atomic-scale electron mapping assume that ultrashort X-ray pulses outrun radiation damage. Although atomic cores move over tens of femtoseconds, outer electrons respond much faster, compromising their locations. We test this limit in elemental silicon using broadband, energy-chirped X-ray pulses to encode time with few-femtosecond resolution. Comparing valence-sensitive structurally forbidden reflections with simultaneously measured core-dominated Bragg peaks reveals valence-bond dephasing on sub-20-fs timescales even at pulse energies where direct ionization effects are negligible. Our findings demonstrate that crystallography where outer electrons contribute substantially to Bragg peaks requires pulses shorter than today's standard $\sim50\,fs$ standard. They also establish a path to disentangle valence electron and lattice contributions to order and dynamics with hard X-rays, enabling studies across molecular biology, quantum matter and devices.

### Paper [175]
Title: Analytic Bootstrap Solution for Quantum Mechanics on a Circle
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36031
Authors: David Berenstein, Upamanyu Moitra
Abstract: We solve the Schr\"odinger equation for a particle in the circle with a quantum mechanical bootstrap method. The main idea is that in physical situations one needs solutions of the recursion relations for the Fourier coefficients of the measure that vanish exponentially fast. We show that this results in a (generalized) continued fraction representation of the solution. The solution is an analytical function of the couplings and the energy. We also show how one this method directly computes the density of states and checking that the answer is physical is reduced to checking an inequality for a single variable. We also show how to reconstruct the wave function and how to extract the quasimomentum of the particle as a function of the energy. We illustrate our solution in the simplest case of the Mathieu equation, where we show that the method can easily resolve exponentially small bands in the band spectrum.

### Paper [176]
Title: Theory of Convective Diffusion
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40862
Authors: \v{Z}iga Krajnik, Takato Yoshimura, Alvise Bastianello, Enej Ilievski
Abstract: We obtain a hydrodynamic theory of diffusive-scale dynamics in one-dimensional systems with interacting sound modes in the absence of self-interactions. Chaotic systems generically feature three distinct Gaussian relaxation processes: besides normal diffusion, driven by microscopic noise stemming from fast-decaying non-hydrodynamic modes, collisions between sound modes lead to two additional channels: convective diffusion manifesting as an effective multiplicative temporal noise, and an effective additive noise generated by the surrounding hydrodynamic modes. Our theory also includes, as a subclass, integrable systems described by generalized hydrodynamics, representing a pure realization of convective diffusion. Focusing on convective diffusion, we obtain an exact closed-form expression for the higher-point dynamical correlation functions and contrast their structure with those of normal diffusion. Additionally, we show that in convective diffusion each sound mode features a universal non-Gaussian distribution of charge transfer in its comoving frame, thereby establishing anomalous fluctuations as a characteristic feature of diffusive-scale dynamics in integrable systems.

### Paper [177]
Title: A Computational Journey Toward an Optimal Design for Metamaterial Epicardial Passive Sleeves
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00013
Authors: Vahid Naeini, Emilio A. Mendiola, Ahmad Rafsanjani, Fergal B. Coulter, Qian Xiang, Jianyi Zhang, Peter Vanderslice, Vahid Serpooshan, Reza Avazmohammadi
Abstract: Heart failure (HF) following myocardial infarction (MI) is a major clinical challenge with severe complications. Epicardial sleeves and patches are increasingly investigated to improve heart function post-MI, yet their passive mechanical effects remain underexplored. This has resulted in limited insight into how sleeves mechanically interact with the infarct and remote myocardium. This study used 3-D in-silico cardiac models to examine how sleeve shape, material properties, and architecture affect global and regional mechanics. A high-fidelity biventricular model is used to investigate how a continuum cardiac sleeve alters function. Designs that improve regional mechanics successfully limited pathological bulging, modulated fiber strains, and influenced torsional behavior without over-constraining remote tissue, whereas overly restrictive and stiff sleeves penalized healthy myocardium and reduced the intended relief of infarct bulging. These findings highlight the importance of considering regional biomechanical markers when developing sleeve designs. Building on these continuum sleeve insights, a spheroidal left ventricle model demonstrated the proof-of-concept advantage of an "auxetic" metamaterial sleeve, engineered with a negative Poisson ratio. This programmed architecture provided region-specific benefits beyond those of conventional continuum sleeves. Ultimately, this work contributes to an improved understanding of passive sleeve-heart interactions and improves the targeted biomechanical support therapies following MI.

### Paper [178]
Title: The Multi-trace Eigenstate Thermalization Hypothesis
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00197
Authors: Ahmed I. Abdalla, Gabriele Di Ubaldo, Elisa Tabor
Abstract: We introduce a multi-trace eigenstate thermalization hypothesis, an extension of ETH that governs connected correlations of operator matrix elements with arbitrary index structures, including several disjoint index cycles. We derive multi-trace ETH from local rotational invariance, relate its independent data to higher-order free cumulants, and construct a cyclic matrix model that encodes the resulting multi-cycle data. We provide evidence for multi-trace ETH in chaotic quantum systems and compute multi-trace correlations in low-dimensional gravity. In few-body systems, multi-trace contributions arise from correlated periodic orbits, including a linear ramp contribution to the one-point spectral form factor. In two dimensions, we compute interaction-induced multi-trace correlations from a quartically coupled bulk scalar in JT gravity, relating winding matter diagrams to corrections of matter-channel weights and anomalous dimensions. In three dimensions, we show that multi-trace non-Gaussianities arise from both topology-changing saddles and light matter. Using Virasoro TQFT and conformal mean-field theory, we compute examples involving knot and link complements and torus wormholes with matter loops. Across these settings, multi-trace ETH provides a common framework for both connected correlators in chaotic quantum systems and multi-boundary wormhole amplitudes in gravity.

### Paper [179]
Title: Exhaustive Construction of Generalized Parent Hamiltonians for Matrix Product States
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00203
Authors: He-Ran Wang, Lorenzo Gotta, Sanjay Moudgalya
Abstract: Characterizing local Hamiltonians that host interesting many-body states as eigenstates has been an important problem in quantum many-body physics. For matrix product states (MPS), there is a well-known construction of parent Hamiltonians that have the MPS as the frustration-free ground state. However, recent results on quantum many-body scars have shown that such a construction does not exhaust all possible Hamiltonians that have the MPS as an eigenstate. In this work, we develop an exhaustive construction of generalized parent Hamiltonians that have a given translation-invariant MPS as an eigenstate. These go beyond the conventional projector-based construction of frustration-free parent Hamiltonians, where each local Hermitian term has the MPS as an eigenstate, by allowing non-Hermitian telescoping terms that cancel out with periodic boundary conditions (PBC). We derive a local tensor equation that establishes a correspondence between inequivalent classes of generalized parent Hamiltonians, i.e., those that cannot be related using linear combinations with frustration-free Hamiltonians, and the MPS transfer matrix spectrum. We illustrate the connection to intrinsic physical properties, including non-Abelian symmetries and zero correlation length, and further extend this construction to block-injective MPS. Our framework hence provides a systematic bridge between generalized parent Hamiltonians and the local structure of quantum states.

### Paper [180]
Title: Distance to integrability and complexity
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00233
Authors: Sean Casey, Marius de Leeuw, Tristan McLoughlin, Sergiy Zhuk
Abstract: We consider nearest-neighbour interactions on spin-1/2 chains. We find the full set of integrable models of this form and apply a numerical algorithm to determine the "closest" integrable model for a given Hamiltonian. This allows us to define a model-independent distance to integrability. We show that standard measures of chaos interpolate between integrable and chaotic values with increasing values of this distance. However, there is a large variation across models and the distance to integrability provides additional information not directly provided by these diagnostics.

### Paper [181]
Title: Modeling and Analysis of Cooling Induced Isotropic Nematic Transitions in Pharmaceutical Liquid Crystals
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00273
Authors: Pei Liu, Debanjan Banerjee, Daniel Siebel-Cortopassi, Sichen Song, Maria-Carme Calderer
Abstract: This paper studies the phase transition from an isotropic to a nematic liquid crystal driven by controlled cooling, motivated by the manufacturing of next-generation pharmaceutical drugs. Using the non-isothermal Ericksen model for liquid crystals with a variable degree of orientation, we formulate the problem as a stiff, quasilinear system of parabolic partial differential equations. We establish a consistent thermodynamic framework, prove the well-posedness of the system, and conduct a rigorous asymptotic and numerical scaling analysis of the moving phase boundary in a cylindrical cell subjected to bottom-wall convective cooling. Our analysis identifies a critical sample thickness that separates two distinct physical regimes. Below this threshold, the transition follows the cooling dynamics and the material remains quasi-isothermal. Above the thickness threshold, bulk thermal diffusion governs the dynamics, imposing a dominant thermal delay characterized by a concave quadratic propagation front. These results demonstrate that scaling from laboratory characterization to industrial production fundamentally alters the spatial thermal history of the material, providing a predictive mathematical framework to optimize cooling protocols for kinetic arrest.

### Paper [182]
Title: Finesse evolution in a bichromatic cavity under blue and red resonant illumination in vacuum
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00277
Authors: M. S. Caracas Nu\~nez, A. Kadakkavattathu, G. Gatta, L. Duca, N. Mizukami, C. Sias
Abstract: The long-term performance of high-finesse optical cavities depends on the stability of their mirror coatings under vacuum and under continuous exposure to high circulating power. In cavities supporting multiple wavelengths, a central question is whether degradation at one wavelength compromises performance at another. Understanding this interplay is essential for those cavity quantum electrodynamics experiments that need diverse optical fields for cavity stabilization, trapping, and light--matter coupling. Here, we investigate wavelength-dependent degradation and recovery of a bichromatic cavity with high-reflectivity coating at 455 nm and 671 nm in vacuum. Cavity ring-down measurements reveal a decrease in the 455 nm finesse from 7.7*10^3 to 1.9*10^3 after 11 hours of continuous exposure to high circulating power. Simultaneous measurements show no measurable degradation at 671 nm. Continuous operation at 671 nm, instead, produces no measurable degradation at either wavelength under the tested conditions. Comparison of oxygen depletion and Avrami-type loss models indicates kinetics compatible with nucleation and growth, without uniquely identifying the microscopic mechanism. We demonstrate partial recovery of the finesse with illumination at 455 nm in air. Our results indicate a wavelength dependence of laser-induced optical damage in bichromatic cavities and provide an estimate of the blue light intensity that can be safely used in such cavities.

### Paper [183]
Title: Ewald summation of doubly periodic 2- and 3-dimensional Stokes multipoles
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00308
Authors: Teunike A. van de Pol, Zhan Ma, Alexander Morozov, Joakim Stenhammar
Abstract: In spite of the qualitative differences between Stokes flows in 2 and 3 dimensions, most particle-resolved computational studies of microswimmers and other colloidal systems dominated by low-Re hydrodynamic interactions are carried out in triply periodic geometries, while the corresponding continuum descriptions are frequently solved in 2-dimensional doubly periodic domains. Furthermore, most experimental examples of biological and synthetic microswimmers are realised in confined, quasi-2-dimensional geometries with a finite thickness in one of the three dimensions. To enable studying the non-trivial differences between these system geometries, in this paper we systematically derive Ewald representations for periodic Stokes force multipoles in geometries with two periodic directions, treating both three-dimensional Stokes flow with one unbounded coordinate and intrinsically two-dimensional singularities. Starting from the periodic Stokeslet, we obtain explicit expressions for force dipoles and quadrupoles and analyse the non-trivial zero-wavevector contribution arising under partial periodicity. The analytical expressions are independently validated by comparing with results from an explicit lattice Boltzmann solver in each geometry.

### Paper [184]
Title: Recurrence in Sequence Space: How Epistasis Mimics Shared Ancestry
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00360
Authors: Pietro Chiarantoni, Mina Mahboubi, Jason Lamanna, Vincenzo Carnevale
Abstract: Sequences in a protein family do not explore the sequence space freely: folding, packing, and binding couple residues, so the set of viable sequences is irreducibly correlated, a phenomenon referred to as epistasis. We show that such correlations lower the \emph{intrinsic dimension} $d_{\rm IN}$ of sequence space, counteracting the concentration of measure and broadening the distribution of pairwise distances. Consequentially, they make it likely that independently evolving lineages \emph{recur}, approaching one another by chance long after they last shared an ancestor. Using Kac's lemma we show that the logarithmic mean recurrence time grows linearly with the intrinsic dimension, $\log \tau_{\rm rec} \propto {d_{\rm IN}}$ for typical sequences, so strongly constrained (low-dimensional) families recur often and generate sequence similarity that carries no genealogical signal. We establish this first in a solvable Ising-like model with tunable correlation, then in a real protein family using Potts models and Markov chain Monte Carlo, where $d_{\rm IN}$ remains predictive of recurrence even in multi-basins landscapes. Because recurrence is a property of the evolutionary process rather than of any estimator, it bounds the recoverability of deep history for distance-based and likelihood methods alike.

### Paper [185]
Title: Dysfunctional Functionals and their Therapies in Time-Dependent Density Functional Theory
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00377
Authors: Neepa T. Maitra
Abstract: The exchange-correlation functional in Time-Dependent Density Functional Theory (TDDFT) is known to have dependence on past densities and initial states, and yet this memory-dependence is ignored in practical calculations which almost always use an adiabatic approximation. We discuss the dysfunction that arises due to this deficiency and point to therapies that go beyond the adiabatic approximation. Still, adiabatic TDDFT has had success in a wide range of systems for response properties and for fully non-equilibrium dynamics, and why this is so is only partially understood. This, together with the rigorous foundation of the exact theory and its favorable system-size scaling, provides tremendous impetus for continuing to strive towards improved approximations to the exact functionals, and a deeper understanding of the impact of approximations in applications.

### Paper [186]
Title: VESPER: Online Learning of Free Energies with a Multi-Tempered Variational Objective
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00401
Authors: Jay L. Kaplan, Gustavo R. P\'erez-Lemus, Mengjian Hua, Juan J. de Pablo, Eric Vanden-Eijnden
Abstract: Computing molecular free-energy landscapes over collective variables (CVs) is central to understanding molecular mechanisms, but poses a difficult learning problem without training data available a priori. Samples of the marginal CV distribution must be generated by molecular dynamics (MD), whose trajectories can remain trapped in metastable states and rarely cross the free-energy barriers governing conformational changes or chemical reactions. We introduce VESPER, which couples the generation of these samples to neural-network-driven free-energy learning: MD supplies configurations for learning, while the evolving model biases the MD to accelerate exploration and generate further training data. VESPER minimizes a single variational objective that is convex in the free energy and can combine several tempering levels, each flattening the free-energy landscape to promote barrier crossing. Every level has the same physical free energy as its minimizer, allowing data from all levels to train one shared model without reweighting. Learning uses the difference in mean parameter gradients between biased MD configurations and auxiliary walkers that explore the flattened model in CV space. We prove conditional convergence of the coupled overdamped population dynamics without replica exchange under log-Sobolev and Polyak-Lojasiewicz assumptions. VESPER matches or improves on established enhanced-sampling methods on alanine dipeptide and yields self-consistent free-energy reconstructions from three to six CVs on alanine tetrapeptide. For methane dehydrogenation on Ni(511) with a machine-learned interatomic potential, it recovers two-CV Boltzmann marginals and a reactant-to-saddle free-energy difference along a three-CV path, both qualitatively consistent with the lower-dimensional reference. It also samples RMSD-defined folded and unfolded regions of chignolin while biasing seventeen CVs.

### Paper [187]
Title: A Spectral Renormalization-Group View of Learning
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00422
Authors: Charles H. Martin
Abstract: We study how the layers in deep neural networks (NNs) converge, using Wilsonian Renormalization-Group (RG) ideas to organize the spectral picture. During training, these layers often develop heavy-tailed power-law (PL) spectra, and the strongest checkpoints often appear near the PL exponent $\alpha\approx 2$. This pattern raises a simple question: why should this value mark a well-trained layer? The answer is both statistical and physical. Our statistical hypothesis is that $\alpha=2$ marks the boundary between stable if not optimal learning and overfitting via non-self-averaging. The physical answer is that the same value makes the spectral energy gain scale evenly across logarithmic spectral scales, which is the proposed Wilsonian marginal condition. We then apply the ERG trace-log condition, the proposed SETOL theory, which we justify by taking volume-preserving, scale-invariant transformations to remove arbitrary layer scale along the proposed RG flow. The resulting spectral RG flow connects the HTSR and SETOL theories to optimizers such as Muon, and motivates a new optimizer extension, WW-PGD. Together, these results point toward a spectral Renormalization-Group view of learning, in which NNs may approach shared spectral structure in the settings studied here.

### Paper [188]
Title: Stochastic learning flow: moment equations for stochastic gradient descent at finite step size
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00473
Authors: Christian Schmid, James M Murray
Abstract: Analytical theories of learning in deep networks often assume gradient flow, discarding the finite step size and batch noise of stochastic gradient descent (SGD). Starting from the discrete update, we derive continuous-time equations for the mean and covariance of the parameter distribution under a Gaussian closure for a general stochastic learning, to first order in the learning rate $\eta$. Applying these stochastic learning flow (SLF) equations to SGD, we show that the mean descends an effective loss regularized by the covariance and loss curvature, while the covariance, unlike in stochastic differential equation approximations, becomes unstable at curvature $2/\eta$. In SGD ensembles of linear and nonlinear networks, SLF predicts learning curves and mean trajectories more accurately than gradient flow and captures the covariance dynamics, failing where the parameter distribution becomes strongly non-Gaussian. In the full-batch limit, the edge of stability corresponds to a stationary point of the coupled sharpness-covariance dynamics, recovering self-stabilization and central flow, and batch-to-batch curvature fluctuations lower this threshold under SGD.

### Paper [189]
Title: LM6-CastBench: A Geometry-Aware Simulation Dataset for Transient Thermal Prediction in LM6 Sand Casting
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00619
Authors: Suhani Kolhatkar, Anand Khandekar
Abstract: Transient thermal fields during solidification connect casting process conditions and cooling behaviour to the formation of defect-prone regions in aluminum cast ings. Repeatedly solving this thermal problem across geometries and process conditions is computationally expensive, motivating data-driven surrogate models. However, existing machine-learning studies on casting thermal behaviour rely on study-specific simulation databases that do not support reproducible geometry-level benchmarking. We introduce LM6-CastBench, an open simulation dataset for geometry-aware learning of transient thermal fields in LM6 aluminum sand casting, combining three geometry families, ten variants per family, five pouring tempera tures, and three mould temperatures (450 geometry-process cases in total). Each case retains CAD geometry, unstructured mesh connectivity, spatial descriptors, process metadata, and time-resolved nodal temperatures. We benchmark a point wise MLP against a spatially structured U-Net under geometry-level data splits; the U-Net reduces temperature-field errors by a large margin, with the biggest gains on geometries with localized features and abrupt section changes. LM6-CastBench gives researchers a reproducible dataset and benchmark for geometry-aware surro gate modelling of casting thermal behaviour.

### Paper [190]
Title: Autonomous Analysis of Thermoelectric Materials Big Data by Multi-Agentic AI and Construction of a Knowledge Base and Specialized AI
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00627
Authors: Yusuke Hashimoto, Takaaki Tomai
Abstract: Extracting knowledge from large databases of experimental materials data requires repeated analyses and reusable records of their choices and results. We developed Multi-Agentic AI (MAA), a multi-agent system in which AI agents with distinct roles analyze experimental thermoelectric data under predefined instructions and quality checks, saving analysis records traceable to their sources. In four experiments varying theme and cycle counts, we measured what accumulated in these records. In the largest experiment (100 analyses across 10 themes and 10 cycles), concept co-occurrences were concentrated within themes, vocabulary was shared across themes and some relation descriptions were first detected in later cycles. A specialized AI that searches this experiment's knowledge bases and reads their analysis records answered materials questions with cited sources. Retrospective checks, including tracing an answer generated with a preliminary corpus back to the source data, exposed errors: traceability makes errors findable but not absent.

### Paper [191]
Title: Free energy of spherical vector spin glasses
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00710
Authors: Hong-Bin Chen
Abstract: We identify the limit free energy of spherical vector spin glasses by a variational formula. The covariance function need not be convex. Using the global symmetry of the spin configurations, we introduce a family of constraint measures that gives a disintegration of the original spin distribution. We first identify the limit free energy of each constrained model and then apply the Laplace method to recover that of the original model.

### Paper [192]
Title: On the unconstrained Allen-Cahn system and its Cahn-Hilliard limit
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00778
Authors: P. O. Mchedlov-Petrosyan, L. N. Davydov, O. A. Osmayev
Abstract: In [F.P. Duda et al., Meccanica, 2021] the new theory for constituent migration in solids with microstructure was developed. We consider the one-dimensional version of the basic system of this theory with generally non-zero kinetic modulus and the particular diffusivity of the "fast diffusion" type. We obtain exact traveling wave solutions for this system, and analyze several unconstraint and constraint limiting cases. We show that in unconstraint cases of Allen-Cahn type, i.e. for two independent variables, the system demonstrates broader set of possible regimes; in the constraint limit only CH-type process remains.

### Paper [193]
Title: Canonic hemoglobin-oxygen equilibrium: Reassessing the role of cooperativity
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00824
Authors: A. E. Allahverdyan, S. G. Gevorkian, A. Harutyunyan, Y. Sh. Mamasakhlisov, V. Stepanyan
Abstract: Hemoglobin-oxygen equilibrium is normally studied within the grand canonical ensemble, which assumes that each hemoglobin is immersed in a reservoir of unbound oxygen molecules of the plasma. We show that this assumption is incorrect inside the RBC (red blood cell or erythrocyte), where the hemoglobin concentration is larger than the concentration of unbound oxygen in plasma. We suggest a better model, where a single hemoglobin and a few oxygen molecules around it reach a canonical equilibrium at a fixed volume (determined from the RBC structure) and a fixed temperature. The basic models of hemoglobin-oxygen equilibrium -- Pauling's model and Monod-Wyman-Changeux model -- can be reformulated for this canonical situation. They predict cooperative interaction energies, which are significantly lower than predictions of the same models in the grand canonical ensemble. Larger cooperative energies, in particular those predicted by the grand canonical ensemble, lead to instabilities (cascade processes) in oxygen release within the canonical approach. Oxygen-binding fluctuations within this approach are sizably smaller than those in the grand canonical situation. These results suggest that the hopping diffusion of oxygen from one hemoglobin to another may play a role in oxygen diffusion within the RBC.

### Paper [194]
Title: Ultraslow diffusion emerging from local symmetry breaking in quenched disorder
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00831
Authors: Jin Liu, Rainer Klages, Huihai Wang, Kehui Sun
Abstract: Many dynamical systems operate in disordered environments. In this Letter, we investigate how quenched randomness modifies particle diffusion. We show that, in finite-strength potential landscapes, low disorder densities can induce logarithmically ultraslow diffusion. The underlying mechanism originates from local symmetry breaking in particular potential configurations: Barrier-well structures form directional channels, causing particles to undergo hierarchical capture, escape, and recapture processes. A coarse-grained channel theory yields an approximate scaling prediction for the mean-square displacement. Counterintuitively, when the disorder density reaches the fully disordered limit, the system returns to normal diffusion, revealing a nontrivial, nonmonotonic relationship between disorder density and transport type. This mechanism is observed in both nonlinear maps and stochastic systems.

### Paper [195]
Title: What Is the Dynamic Capacitance of a Memristor?
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00849
Authors: Juan Bisquert, Gonzalo Rivera-Sierra
Abstract: Impedance spectroscopy of memristors, nanofluidic channels and halide perovskite devices often shows a low-frequency capacitance that is orders of magnitude larger than any plausible stored charge, or that becomes negative and appears together with an inductive loop. We distinguish two kinds of capacitance that enter the impedance in the same way: a storage capacitance, the derivative of a stored charge, and a dynamic capacitance, produced by the delayed response of a conduction current. Using the conductance-activated quasi-linear memristor (CALM) model, and a general memristor in which an internal state controls the conductance, we show that the dynamic capacitance is the time integral of the transient differential conductance after a small voltage step, measured from its final value. For a single relaxing state it is the product of a relaxing conductance and a relaxation time, with a negative sign. It vanishes at zero bias, and its sign is set by whether the stationary conductance increases or decreases with the magnitude of the voltage. Potentiation gives a negative capacitance and a chemical inductor, and depression gives a positive capacitance that is easily mistaken for stored charge. The analysis gives an explicit formulation of the time-domain criterion for negative capacitance of Jonscher and Ershov. In conical multipore nanofluidic membranes, the dynamic capacitance obtained from impedance and from voltage pulses shows the same sign change at zero bias and reaches values several hundred times larger than the membrane capacitance. The same picture accounts for the capacitive and inductive regimes of halide perovskite memristors.

### Paper [196]
Title: Solvable dynamics model of in-context learning
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00921
Authors: Ruoran Bai, Haiping Huang
Abstract: Pretrained Transformer architectures display a surprising in-context learning ability without additional training. How the learning process guides the network to achieve this intriguing capability remains unsolved. In this study, we focus on a single-layer, single-head linear attention model, where empirical risk minimization is defined for learning. Given a finite amount of training examples, we determine the exact solution of network parameter dynamics, which is linked to the training error and generalization error, characterizing how memorization and generalization emerge from learning and how these transitions are related to curvatures of the empirical risk landscape. Furthermore, we calculate a data-dependent free energy, from which the training and generalization errors in the long-time limit of learning dynamics can be well predicted. Our work adds insights toward understanding the mechanism of in-context learning, especially from a learning dynamics perspective.

### Paper [197]
Title: Ground state and excitations of the \(C_2^{(1)}\) spin chain with non-diagonal boundaries
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01016
Authors: Zhouzheng Ji, Guang-Liang Li, Yi Qiao, Junpeng Cao, Wen-Li Yang
Abstract: Understanding boundary effects in higher-rank integrable quantum spin chains requires connecting exact spectral relations with the root configurations underlying their energies and excitations. With non-diagonal boundaries, determining these configurations remains challenging even when an exact spectral solution is available, hindering the direct extraction of thermodynamic properties. We address this problem for the rational $C_2^{(1)}$ chain by combining DMRG in the tensor-network framework with the quantum inverse scattering method. The numerically determined zeros of the fundamental and fused transfer-matrix eigenvalues reveal three bulk-string distributions, labeled by $x$, $y$, and $w$, together with boundary strings and additional roots. Substituting these configurations into the operator identities relating the two transfer matrices yields the ground-state energy and the surface energy in the thermodynamic limit under non-diagonal boundary conditions. The analytical surface energy agrees with the numerical results up to $O(\tfrac1N)$ corrections. From the regularity conditions of the operator identities, we derive a boundary-string selection rule and numerically identify allowed configurations beyond the ground state. For excitations preserving the boundary-string configuration, we identify three elementary hole excitations, $X$, $Y$, and $W$, in the $x$-, $y$-, and $w$-string distributions, respectively, and derive their energies. Based on the symmetry of the zero roots, we also present a bulk-excitation selection rule governing the allowed combinations of these elementary excitations in physical eigenstates. The framework developed here can therefore be used to systematically analyze boundary effects, bulk excitations, and related physical properties in other high-rank integrable quantum models with non-diagonal boundary conditions.

### Paper [198]
Title: Semiclassical Wave Packet Dynamics For Nonlinear Schr\"odinger Equations with Harmonic Potentials
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01108
Authors: Jared Smith, Tomoki Ohsawa
Abstract: We propose an approximate Hamiltonian dynamics for the Gross--Pitaevskii equation $i\hbar\psi_t = -\frac{\hbar^2}{2m}\Delta \psi + V(x)\psi + \hbar^2 \lambda |\psi|^2\psi$ with harmonic potential $V(x)$ and $x\in\mathbb{R}^d$. We do this by extending the Gaussian ansatz of Perez-Garcia et al.~(Phys.~Rev.~A 56, 1424 (1997)) to the semiclassical wave packets of Hagedorn. We derive the evolution equations for the wave packets from the Hamiltonian/symplectic perspective, rather than the Lagrangian/variational one taken by Perez-Garcia et al. We first show that these two perspectives yield identical evolution equations in the Gaussian case. We then derive the evolution equations for an arbitrary element of the semiclassical wave packets. Finally, we demonstrate the efficacy of the approximate dynamics with numerical experiments.

### Paper [199]
Title: Extended Onsager's variational principle for nonlinear chemical reaction kinetics in active matter
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01131
Authors: Xinpeng Xu, Changhao Li, Zhongqiang Xiong, Bin Zheng, Kento Yasuda, Shigeyuki Komura
Abstract: Onsager's variational principle (OVP) constructs dissipative dynamics from free energy, dissipation, and input power, but deterministic rate laws alone do not identify fluctuations or chemical fuel consumption. We examine these distinctions for nonlinear reaction kinetics in active matter. Comparing Gaussian diffusion and Poisson reaction events clarifies when statistical fluctuations match a physical Rayleighian: the reaction dissipation potential must be consistent with counting statistics, local detailed balance links rate bias to chemical affinity, and kinetic scales remain constitutive inputs. Under these conditions, the normalized Rayleighian excess gives the leading rate-fluctuation cost in the frozen-state, many-event regime. Three models illustrate the consequences. Spatially uniform reactions exhibit protocol-dependent nonreciprocal response compatible with fixed-state OVP; different Rayleighian representations can select the same rate without specifying the same fluctuations. In an active emulsion, a finite fuel pool accounts for free-energy transfer and dissolution of a chemically maintained domain after replenishment stops. In a membrane-cytosol model, distinct reaction-channel realizations yield identical protein-density trajectories but different chemical input power. Under a separate kinetic assumption, an exact total-density equation still requires an initial rate and does not determine chemical work or fluctuations. These results clarify the physical and statistical inputs required for constructing reaction-transport models and interpreting their fluctuations.

### Paper [200]
Title: Density functionals for the Pauli-Fierz Hamiltonian
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01208
Authors: Mih\'aly A. Csirik, Andre Laestadius, Markus Penz, Erik I. Tellgren
Abstract: We develop a density-functional theory for the Pauli-Fierz Hamiltonian of nonrelativistic quantum electrodynamics in the spirit of the convex-analytic formulation of Lieb. We consider finitely many electrons, subject to external scalar and vector potentials, coupled to the full continuum of massless transverse photons with an ultraviolet cutoff. The natural basic variables are the standard electron density and a combined current density, the sum of the paramagnetic current density and a field-induced current density generated by the quantized vector potential. We prove representability results for these density-current pairs and show that the mixed-state constrained-search functional attains its infimum and coincides with the Lieb functional obtained by convex conjugation. Since arbitrarily soft photons purify mixed states at a negligible energy cost without changing their density and combined current density, the pure-state Levy-Lieb functional equals the Lieb functional and is therefore convex, even though its infimum need not be attained. In addition, we establish the zero-coupling limit, which recovers the mixed-state Vignale-Rasolt functional of current-density-functional theory. Finally, we consider the long-wavelength (dipole) limit and show that the limiting functional can be written as a constrained search for the well-known length-gauge dipole Hamiltonian.

### Paper [201]
Title: Cooling-path-selected self-assembly of telecom InAsP quantum dots
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01214
Authors: Yiteng Zhang, Doaa Abdelbarey, Markus Etzkorn, Zenghui Jiang, Ankita Choudhary, Andreas Schaate, Tom Fandrich, Xin Cao, Chenxi Ma, Arijit Chakraborty, Eddy Patrick Rugeramigabo, Michael Zopf, Fei Ding
Abstract: Epitaxial quantum dots are leading solid-state sources of quantum light, but reproducible growth on indium phosphide (InP) remains challenging because dot-formation windows are narrow and emission wavelengths are difficult to predict. Here we report a new epitaxial route for InAsP quantum dots on InP(001), in which the cooling path selects the final morphology without intentional deposition of group-III dot material. High-temperature As exposure forms a shallow near-surface InAsP layer through As-P exchange; the cooling path and continued As supply determine whether three-dimensional islands form. Surface-sensitive spectroscopy shows near-surface As enrichment, while cross-sectional STEM/EELS identifies graded, As-rich InAsP islands. The capped emitters span 1.2-1.6 micrometers, with single-photon emission observed up to 100 K. Across 20 wafers, the surface-effective exchange dose D provides a practical calibration for targeting the median emission wavelength. These results establish cooling as an active epitaxial step for telecom InAsP quantum-dot formation and wavelength control.

### Paper [202]
Title: Programmable Droplets as Active Matter in a Reconfigurable Environment
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01277
Authors: Ivan Shmarov, Abhishek Sharma, Michael Jirasek, Kristine Laws, Leroy Cronin
Abstract: Exploring systems that bridge the gap between non living and living matter requires a reconfigurable environment coupled to synthetic systems capable of autonomous motion, adaptive behaviour and persistent dynamics. Self propelled droplets provide a minimal platform for such life like behaviour, but quantitative measures capturing both persistence and adaptability to enable closed loop reconfiguration remain lacking. Here we introduce an autonomous closed loop robotic platform to characterise and dynamically program the autonomous behaviour of multicomponent oil in water droplets. We explored two chemical composition spaces, with and without external pH perturbations, analysing droplet behaviour in real time and iteratively selecting formulations. To quantify this behaviour, we introduce two descriptors, persistence and diversity. Persistence is quantified by mapping temporal dynamics in a speed shape complexity space, where we identify a persistence domain that distinguishes active from inactive droplets. Diversity captures spontaneous or environmentally induced variation in motion and the propensity of droplets to reorient or adapt under perturbation. We show that persistence and diversity are constrained by a behavioural trade off: high persistence limits diversity, whereas high diversity reduces persistent activity. Our results provide a quantitative framework for navigating and programming life like behaviours in active droplets and show how closed-loop experimentation can uncover organising principles in complex active matter.

### Paper [203]
Title: Graph-Covariant Bottom-Up Scale-Bridging Reconstruction of Ground and Excited States of Extended Systems
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01349
Authors: Bartosz B{\l}asiak, Irene Burghardt
Abstract: We propose a hierarchical scale-bridging construction for building first principles electronic structure models of extended molecular aggregates with disorder, and its practical realization in exciton systems. The general construction, referred here as to the encapsulation, is based on a recursive operator-valued hierarchy cast in a dual graph-theoretic (discrete) and configuration-space (continuous) structure, in which variables associated with a coarser level parameterize operators governing the degrees of freedom at the next finer level in a graph-covariant, geometry-preserving fashion. In an exemplary application of this scheme, aggregate-level ground- and excited-state operators are constructed through a many-body expansion (MBE), with matrix elements evaluated in a basis comprising the aggregate ground state and local Frenkel excitations. The required monomer and dimer building blocks are obtained from DFT and TDDFT calculations, yielding aggregate-level 1-particle transition density matrices as well as Hamiltonian, dipole moment and other operators that can be evaluated at the level of the building blocks. Starting from structural models obtained from experiment or molecular dynamics, this approach generates aggregate-level operators in a bottom-up fashion without fitting parameters to spectroscopic data or performing calculations on larger system cutouts. A numerical demonstration is given for an excitonic manifold in crystalline quaterthiophene. In particular, through its intrinsic configuration-dependence encoded in the encapsulations, it will provide a bridge between static electronic structure calculations for realistic, structurally disordered aggregates and quantum or quantum-classical simulations of exciton-vibrational dynamics.

### Paper [204]
Title: Convergence of many-body truncations in the in-medium similarity renormalization group
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01458
Authors: L. H. Chen, H. Hergert, J. M. Yao
Abstract: The in-medium similarity renormalization group (IMSRG) is a leading \textit{ab initio} method for describing atomic nuclei. Most applications truncate the flowing operators at the normal-ordered two-body level, neglecting induced higher-body terms. Convergence with increasing operator rank remains poorly understood, particularly in strongly correlated systems. We systematically assess the many-body truncation hierarchy in the single-reference and multi-reference IMSRG, retaining normal-ordered operators through the four-body and three-body levels, respectively. Using an exactly solvable pairing-plus-particle-hole model, we quantify successive higher-body corrections over a broad range of interaction strengths, spanning weakly to strongly correlated regimes. The multi-reference formulation exhibits faster and more robust convergence than its single-reference counterpart, with the advantage becoming most pronounced at strong coupling. In this regime, higher-rank irreducible density matrices play an essential role in achieving convergence of the many-body expansion.

### Paper [205]
Title: InformedXRD: Reproducible Benchmarks and Physics-Informed Evaluation for Powder Diffraction Symmetry Classification
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01525
Authors: Edward G Friedman, Elizabeth J. Baggett, Abhishek Shetty, Derrick Chan-Sew, Vanellsa Acha, Harshitha Dwaracherla, P. A. Kienzle, William Ratcliff II
Abstract: Machine learning for powder diffraction (PXRD) symmetry classification is being trained at scale, yet the field lacks a common evaluation standard: papers report results on different subsets of experimental archives, with different target taxonomies, preprocessing, and metrics, and published models are rarely released in a form that permits rescoring. We present InformedXRD, an evaluation framework with three parts: (i) a machine-readable mapping from 230 space groups to 99 extinction groups, the finest classes powder diffraction can identify from systematic absences alone; (ii) a benchmark-construction method whose inclusion rules are specified as code and reproduce from a frozen snapshot, instantiated as RRUFF-473, RRUFF-325, and, as a small example, opXRD-98; (iii) a reporting protocol with prior-only frequency baselines, a label-permutation test, a hierarchy-aware error metric on the condensed translationengleiche subgroup graph, and stratified evaluation. We build on the mapping, RRUFF benchmarks, subgroup graph, and four ViT checkpoints released in our earlier work (Baggett et al., 2026). Using these checkpoints, a 1D residual CNN, and six architectures trained under the XRDBench recipe (Cao et al., 2025), we show that evaluation design changes what conclusions are justified: paired tests point in different directions at Top-1 and Top-5; paired Top-k tests do not separate the three leading XRDBench models, while hierarchy-aware analysis describes error distance and direction across all six; an input-blind frequency predictor matches the models on aggregate Top-k, yet a label-permutation test still detects pattern-specific evidence; and stratified analysis exposes a label-entropy confound that aggregate accuracy hides. All mapping tables, benchmark code, scripts, and checkpoints are released as shared infrastructure for PXRD-ML evaluation.

### Paper [206]
Title: The massive phase of the two-dimensional Heisenberg model is stable under small anisotropy
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01532
Authors: Yan Ru Pei
Abstract: Polyakov predicted that the classical Heisenberg model on $\mathbb{Z}^2$ has exponentially decaying correlations at every temperature, and a proof of this has recently been posted. We show that the massive phase survives small anisotropy. For every inverse temperature $\beta$ there is $\delta(\beta)>0$ such that the $S^2$-valued model with couplings $\sum_iJ^i\sigma_x^i\sigma_y^i$ has exponentially decaying spin two-point functions, uniformly in finite subgraphs with free boundary conditions and in edge strengths up to $\beta$, whenever $|J^i-1|\le\delta(\beta)$ for $i=1,2,3$. For the easy-plane model with couplings $\sigma^x\sigma^x+\sigma^y\sigma^y+\lambda\sigma^z\sigma^z$ this shows that the onset of the Berezinskii-Kosterlitz-Thouless phase, proved by Garban and de Montgolfier for every $\lambda\in(-1,1)$, moves to zero temperature as $\lambda\uparrow1$: in free-boundary limits, correlations decay exponentially for every $\beta\le c |\log(1-\lambda)|^{1/2}$. The physical prediction is of order $|\log(1-\lambda)|$. The same holds for aligned single-ion anisotropy, which includes a confining potential $\varepsilon\sum(\sigma^z)^2$, and for nearly isotropic $O(n)$ models with $n\ge3$. The proof keeps the sign-cluster argument of the isotropic proof, using Ginibre's correlation inequality for anisotropic planar energies, and uses the rotation symmetry only on one finite annulus, where the anisotropy is a bounded perturbation of the Hamiltonian.

### Paper [207]
Title: Bounds and sufficient conditions in quantum Zeno effect
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01679
Authors: Vladimir Parfenyev, Sergey Belan
Abstract: When a quantum system undergoes a series of repeated measurements during its time evolution to check whether it remains in the initial state, the resulting state lifetime can decrease (the quantum Zeno effect) or increase (the quantum inverse Zeno effect) as the inter-measurement period increases. Several transitions between these two regimes may occur within the same system. A carefully chosen measurement period $\tau_\ast$ (or rate $r_\ast$ in the case of a stochastic measurement protocol) brings the state lifetime to its global minimum. Here, we describe the upper and lower bounds on the optimized state lifetime, which are expressed in terms of characteristic time scales encoded in the state survival probability $P(T)$, which is assumed to decrease monotonically to zero at large $T$. In addition, we analyze the bounds on the optimal measurement periods/rates and formulate sufficient conditions for observing the inverse Zeno effect. The analysis is also extended to states with non-monotonic survival functions. The results are illustrated numerically with an example of a system of optically trapped ultra-cold atoms.

### Paper [208]
