Title: An Electrolyte Equation of State Based on openCOSMO-RS: From Aqueous Solutions to Non-Aqueous Systems
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01686
Authors: Leander Rausdzus, Simon M\"uller
Abstract: Predictive COSMO-RS-based electrolyte models such as COSMO-RS-ES describe electrolyte solutions from quantum-chemical information, but treat the liquid as incompressible and give no access to densities or pressure effects. This work presents openCOSMO-RS-ES-Phi, to our knowledge the first open-source implementation of a predictive electrolyte equation of state based on openCOSMO-RS. It combines the COSMO-RS-based equation of state openCOSMO-RS-Phi with the ion interaction energies and the long-range term of COSMO-RS-ES, which is first refitted on the same simplified short range. With this refit, a dielectric decrement improves the mean ionic activity coefficients (MIAC), whereas a Born term counts the solvation of the ions a second time and is therefore left out. The equation of state then adds only volume, dispersion and temperature parameters of the ions, fitted to aqueous MIAC and densities. openCOSMO-RS-ES-Phi reproduces COSMO-RS-ES in dilute aqueous solutions, improves on it in concentrated ones and also yields solution densities. Extrapolating from aqueous systems, it predicts the MIAC in water + cosolvent mixtures as accurately as the fitted COSMO-RS-ES, as well as the MIAC and bubble points of a benchmark database of water-alcohol-salt systems. Salting-out of organic components is not described as accurately, and salts come out too soluble in organic solvents. Overall, the interaction model developed for COSMO-RS-ES carries over to a large extent to an equation of state, which makes openCOSMO-RS-ES-Phi a sound base model for further development, with ion transfer between solvents as a clear target for fitting to non-aqueous data.

### Paper [209]
Title: How Does Chain-of-Thought Supervision Teach Models to Reason? A Theoretical Analysis
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01740
Authors: Kaito Takanami, Cengiz Pehlevan
Abstract: Training with chain-of-thought (CoT) supervision is an effective approach for teaching models multi-step reasoning procedures. At inference time, however, models may need to continue reasoning beyond the finite teacher CoT sequences observed during training, and longer reasoning can instead degrade performance, a phenomenon known as overthinking. How the amount and structure of training-time CoT give rise to such failures remains theoretically poorly understood. To study this problem, we introduce an in-context weight prediction model trained on finite gradient descent trajectories for linear regression. Using high-dimensional asymptotic analysis, we show that the success and failure modes of long-horizon reasoning undergo phase transitions depending on the context length and the total amount of training CoT. We further distinguish two mechanisms of overthinking, error accumulation and error amplification, and show that with short training contexts, increasing the training CoT budget can worsen long-horizon error before eventually stabilizing reasoning. Experiments with fully trained linear and multi-head softmax attention further show that these qualitative behaviors persist. Our results provide a theoretical account linking properties of training-time CoT to inference-time failures and offer guidance for designing teacher CoT that supports stable reasoning.

### Paper [210]
Title: Stochastic Orders of the Exit Pair and the Monotone Response to Resetting
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01756
Authors: Tony Newton, Juan Antonio Vega Coso
Abstract: Resetting a process, that is, restarting it from its initial position at random times, changes the probability that it leaves an interval through one end rather than the other. We show that the direction of this change is governed by how the exit time depends on the exit side, and we express this through classical stochastic orders. For random walks and birth-death chains we determine when resetting more intensively always moves the exit probability in the same direction, and we show that this can fail: we give an explicit chain in which the direction reverses twice over time, and bound the number of such reversals spectrally. We also show how the direction depends on the starting point. In the continuum limit we obtain the corresponding results for diffusions with drift.

### Paper [211]
Title: Influence of the Coulomb Interaction on the Radiation of a Few Body System
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01777
Authors: Zukhriddin Ruziev, Caterina Cocchi, Anvar Khujakulov
Abstract: Understanding energy absorption in laser-plasma interactions is crucial for applications ranging from inertial confinement fusion to particle acceleration. At the most fundamental level, this energy exchange is governed by the motion of individual charged particles in the laser field. However, when multiple particles are present, their mutual Coulomb interaction can modify the dynamics and, consequently, the energy absorption. In this work, we investigate this scenario by solving the classical equations of motion for two coupled charged particles interacting with an intense laser field. Within the plasma approximation, we find that the Coulomb interaction between the electrons does not significantly affect the energy transfer during the laser-matter interaction. Additionally, we explore the role of mass asymmetry by considering the classical electron-proton interaction as a target system. Our results highlight the critical importance of mass: the proton, due to its much slower dynamics, acquires substantially less energy from the field compared to the electron, leading to a pronounced asymmetry in the energy distribution between the two species.

### Paper [212]
Title: Pore-scale mechanisms of capillary imbibition in cement-based materials revealed by direct numerical simulations in a realistic FIB-SEM microstructure
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01901
Authors: Luka Malenica, Zhidong Zhang, Ueli Angst
Abstract: Moisture transport in cement-based materials involves transient multiphase flow through highly irregular pore networks, where evolving air--water interfaces strongly influence liquid invasion and gas displacement. Here, pore-resolved, image-based direct numerical simulations (DNS) are used to investigate spontaneous imbibition in a FIB--SEM-reconstructed cementitious microstructure, resolving air--water interface dynamics at nanometre-to-micrometre spatial scales and nanosecond-to-microsecond timescales. As these dynamics remain inaccessible to direct experimental observation at such scales, the simulations provide, to the best of our knowledge, the first direct view of transient imbibition within the capillary porosity of a realistic cementitious microstructure.
  The three-dimensional simulations reveal highly heterogeneous wetting-front propagation, continuous interface reconfiguration, and progressive air entrapment, showing that local dynamics depend on evolving interface curvature and capillary-pressure fields rather than on characteristic pore size alone. Significant air entrapment develops even within the capillary pores and under perfectly water-wetting conditions, progressively reducing the saturation rate, while the capillary pressure of trapped-air clusters depends strongly on local interface geometry rather than gas volume alone. Controlled two-dimensional analyses show that the balance between capillary, viscous, inertial, and gravitational effects is strongly scale-dependent. Comparison with the three-dimensional simulations highlights the importance of out-of-plane interface curvature and pore-network connectivity. The framework also resolves moisture conditions at the steel--concrete interface, illustrating its potential for corrosion-relevant pore-scale analysis.

### Paper [213]
Title: Controlling the Sign and Magnitude of the Thermoreflectance Coefficient of Gold-MoS$_2$ Metamaterials
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02024
Authors: D. Saleta Reig, B. Liu, K. Xu, J. S. Reparaz, K. -J. Tielrooij
Abstract: Heating a material changes how much light it reflects. This effect, quantified by the thermoreflectance coefficient $C_\mathrm{TR}$, enables temperature sensing, while it is a nuisance for situations where constant reflection is crucial. Whereas temperature detection requires a large $C_\mathrm{TR}$, the latter situation demands it to be as close to zero as possible. Controlling the $C_\mathrm{TR}$ has so far relied on the selection of materials and wavelengths, rather than engineering the coefficient itself. This work demonstrates that the $C_\mathrm{TR}$ of a 50 nm thin gold film on SiO$_2$/Si can be controlled in sign and magnitude by an ultrathin ($<$8 nm) MoS$_2$ film underneath it. Time-resolved thermoreflectance measurements reveal suppression, sign inversion, and revival of the thermoreflectance signal, controlled by three knobs: ($i$) the buried MoS$_2$ thickness, ($ii$) probe wavelength, and ($iii$) ambient temperature. Transfer-matrix simulations using temperature-dependent dielectric functions of gold and MoS$_2$ reproduce all experimental observations, demonstrating an optothermal metamaterial platform with controllable properties. In particular, these (anti-)thermoreflective metamaterial mirrors bring $C_\mathrm{TR}$ under the same interference design rules previously applied to the optical response itself, opening ways to maximize or minimize the temperature dependence of the optical reflectance, tailored to the specific application.

### Paper [214]
Title: An entropic barrier to equilibration in the discrete nonlinear Schr\"odinger equation: Rigorous bounds for the microcanonical Brownian motion
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02025
Authors: Claudio Arezzo, Antonello Scardicchio
Abstract: The relaxation of the discrete nonlinear Schr\"odinger equation is known to slow down dramatically when the energy density $\varepsilon$ exceeds a critical value $\varepsilon_c$, where the system enters a condensed phase. Replacing the Hamiltonian dynamics by Brownian motion on the microcanonical surface of $N$ sites at fixed mass and energy, we prove rigorous upper and lower bounds on the equilibration time $\tau_{\mathrm{eq}}$, which is governed by the first non-zero eigenvalue $\lambda_1$ of the Laplacian on this surface. In the condensed phase the equilibration time is stretched exponential, not exponential, in the system size. The upper bounds on $\lambda_1$ come from trial functions: of one occupation for $\varepsilon<\varepsilon_c$, where the problem reduces to a Sturm-Liouville equation, and of two occupations for $\varepsilon>\varepsilon_c$, where they cross the bottleneck through which the condensate moves between sites. The lower bounds on $\lambda_1$ come from a Bochner-Reilly argument, with a vector field that compensates the concavity of the boundary. As a result, $\tau_{\mathrm{eq}}=O(1)$ for $\varepsilon<\varepsilon_c$, while for $\varepsilon>\varepsilon_c$, asymptotically in $N$, $(\sqrt{2}-1)\sqrt{\varepsilon-\varepsilon_c}\leq\ln\tau_{\mathrm{eq}}/\sqrt{N}\leq\sqrt{\varepsilon-\varepsilon_c}$. We conjecture that the left-hand bound is sharp and the right-hand one is not.

### Paper [215]
Title: Three-dimensional computational rheometry of viscoplastic fluids in vane-in-cup fixtures
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02115
Authors: James Malcolm, Clare McColgan, Emma Mullarkey, Emad Chaparian
Abstract: We develop a computational framework to investigate the flow kinematics of viscoplastic materials, described by the Herschel-Bulkley model, in vane-in-cup rheometers. Numerical simulations are performed using augmented Lagrangian finite element and regularised finite volume methods for two- and three-dimensional configurations, respectively, to characterise the hydrodynamics and yielding behaviour over a broad range of effective parameters. Three types of vanes are investigated: conventional 4- and 6-arm bespoke vanes and a 24-arm fractal vane (Owens et al., J. Rheol., vol. 64(3), 2020, 643-662). Our previous two-dimensional study (Chaparian et al., J. Non-Newtonian Fluid Mech., vol. 307, 2022, 104857) demonstrated the superiority of the fractal vane over conventional N-arm designs. The bespoke vanes produce highly non-uniform yielding, leading to significant deviations from the circular Couette analogy, whereas the 24-arm fractal vane generates nearly axisymmetric yield surfaces that effectively "cloak" the vane geometry. Consequently, the resulting flow closely resembles the azimuthal shear in a conventional "bob & cup" rheometer, providing substantially more accurate rheological measurements. Building on these findings, the present study revisits the torque-to-stress and rotation-to-strain rate conversions used in vane-in-cup rheometry. In particular, we re-examine the circular Couette analogy that underpins both the physical interpretation of the flow and the extraction of flow curves from raw rheometric data. Based on our extensive two- and three-dimensional simulations, we identify limitations of the existing conversion relations and propose improved closure expressions that enable more accurate determination of material's rheological properties. Furthermore, the three-dimensional flow structures and yielding patterns within the vane-in-cup fixtures are characterised.

### Paper [216]
Title: Uniqueness and radial symmetry of magnetic skyrmions
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02141
Authors: Giovanni Di Fratta, Cyrill B. Muratov, Theresa M. Simon, Valeriy V. Slastikov
Abstract: This paper establishes radial symmetry and uniqueness of magnetic
  skyrmions as global minimizers of the simplest local micromagnetic
  energy. The strategy is to show that any radial critical point with
  strictly decreasing out-of-plane component is the unique minimizer
  in the class of general degree-one maps with the prescribed limit at
  infinity. To this end, we identify a class of null Lagrangians that
  is sufficiently flexible to contain a calibration, giving a uniform
  lower bound for the energy that is uniquely saturated on the
  critical point. Our arguments provide a blueprint for proofs of
  symmetry and uniqueness of minimizers in vectorial variational
  problems under topological constraints.

### Paper [217]
Title: Sound-wave interference reveals the symmetry of a quantum transition
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02163
Authors: Sergei Studenikin, Yousef Karimi Yonjali, Alex Bogan, Andy Sachrajda, Shabir Barzanjeh
Abstract: A single sound quantum emitted from two places interferes with itself, like a particle passing through two slits. The fringe position reveals whether the sources emit with the same sign (symmetric) or opposite signs (antisymmetric). Knowing this symmetry is important because qubits in solids lose energy by emitting such sound quanta, phonons. For a qubit shared between two sites, antisymmetric emission cancels at long wavelengths, suppressing energy loss, whereas symmetric emission does not. Lifetime measurements give only the total loss and cannot distinguish them. Such fringes appear in double quantum dots, but their position has not been analysed. Here we measure it for single phonons emitted as one hole tunnels between two gallium arsenide quantum dots. Using a quantitative model of sound focusing in the crystal, we find that the two dots emit antisymmetrically, as expected for a moving charge, with only a small symmetric part. Spin-flip transitions in a magnetic field weaken the fringes without shifting them. For double-dot charge qubits, antisymmetric emission is predicted to reduce phonon losses strongly at the frequencies where qubits operate, and phonon interferometry gives a direct way to measure how qubits release energy and to design longer-lived qubits.

### Paper [218]
Title: Finite-size scaling and cutoff functions of critical avalanches under cubic saturation
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02396
Authors: Ryota Kojima, Kazufumi Hosoda, Hiroyuki Suto, Hitoshi Yamada
Abstract: Power-law avalanche statistics characterize the branching bulk but leave open how finite systems terminate their largest events. Here we determine both the finite-size cutoff and the full crossover function connecting the branching regime to the large-event tail for an exchangeable absorbing event process motivated by Cauchy-stable recurrent dynamics. The Cauchy dynamical mean-field theory yields a linear-cubic low-activity drift, while a repeatable-event lift supplies absorbing branching fluctuations. Their competition at criticality generates a distinct finite-size edge controlled by cubic saturation. For avalanches initiated by a fixed microscopic seed, the rescaled edge measure connects the universal branching power law continuously to an exponential large-area tail. This full crossover admits an analytic spectral representation in terms of the boundary spectrum of a quartic Schr\"odinger operator on the half-line: high modes reconstruct the branching singularity, while the ground state fixes the far-tail decay. Exact finite-size calculations and Monte Carlo simulations confirm both the predicted cutoff behavior and the full spectral crossover.

### Paper [219]
Title: Dual-Space Analysis of Vortex Configurations and Rotational Energy in a 2D Optical Lattice
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02473
Authors: Avra Banerjee
Abstract: We investigate the effects of a stationary two-dimensional periodic optical lattice on a rotating Bose--Einstein condensate confined by an isotropic harmonic trap. Within the mean-field description, the condensate is modeled by the two-dimensional Gross--Pitaevskii equation in the rotating frame and solved numerically using an imaginary-time split-step Crank--Nicolson method to obtain stationary vortex states. We examine how the lattice depth, lattice wave vector, particle number, and angular velocity influence the vortex configurations, real-space density, momentum-space density, and energetics of the condensate. We find that the stationary periodic lattice significantly modifies vortex nucleation and spatial organization: vortices emerge progressively with increasing angular velocity and, for sufficiently strong lattice strength, undergo rearrangement and merging. Importantly, changes in the vortex configuration are directly reflected in the momentum-space density, demonstrating that vortex nucleation and rearrangement produce pronounced modifications of the condensate momentum distribution. We further analyze the energy per particle and rotational energy as functions of the angular velocity and find that the presence of the optical lattice substantially alters the rotational response of the condensate. In particular, the rotational energy is progressively reduced at higher angular velocities as the lattice strength is increased. These results demonstrate how a stationary periodic optical lattice modifies vortex formation, momentum-space structure, and rotational energetics of a rotating trapped Bose--Einstein condensate.

### Paper [220]
Title: Organizational Learning in Materials Discovery Campaigns: Which Databases, Calculations, and Agent Teams Pay Off
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02571
Authors: Kinston Ack\"olf, Taylor D. Sparks
Abstract: More databases can make a materials campaign worse. Through an organizational lens, we trace each source's contribution. One band-gap campaign ranks seven anion families from archived measured gaps, one vote per source. In the first half of its budget, all seven pooled rank worse (rank agreement 0.737; 1 = measured order) than the best pool of three (0.851). As a stability check, choosing the pool on half the runs and testing on the rest kept the gap. Splitting Materials Project afterwards points to its transition-metal oxides and fluorides. Two databases from one pipeline add nothing over one, but pooling gives their shared ordering two votes. New calculations repay their cost early only when two or three campaigns reuse them. Flat language-model agent teams trailed a random-pick baseline (mean 0.876, sd 0.096, 20 searches). An adaptive Al-Mg-Si team, mostly interatomic potentials, was scored on unseen HSE06 calculations. In its first quarter of compute it found 49% of the ten lowest-formation-energy compounds, against at most 40% for fixed teams and 73% for MACE-MP-0 alone. Merging members whose orderings agreed carried most of its gain. This suggests pooling a few sources that fit the measurements, counting each pipeline once and charging for computation.

### Paper [221]
Title: Quantum 6j-symbols arising from a subfactor of finite index and finite depth and the zipper condition for 4-tensors in two-dimensional topological order
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02582
Authors: Yasuyuki Kawahigashi
Abstract: We study 3-tensors satisfying the ``zipper condition,'' which play an important role in tensor network approaches to two-dimensional topological order. In our previous work, we identified the 4-tensors appearing in these approaches with bi-unitary connections in the Jones theory of subfactors in operator algebras. These bi-unitary connections give rise to subfactors of finite index. We prove that, when the associated subfactor has finite depth, the 3-tensors satisfying the zipper condition correspond precisely to the quantum $6j$-symbols, also known as $F$-symbols, of the unitary multi-fusion category arising from the subfactor. This realization of quantum $6j$-symbols differs from their realization through a flat bi-unitary connection arising from the higher relative commutants of a subfactor.

### Paper [222]
Title: Chiral phonon magnetism from localized Raman excitation
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02594
Authors: Yuhan Wang, Chuanshan Tian
Abstract: Optically driven chiral phonons can induce transient magnetism in non-magnetic crystals, yet the microscopic relationship between phonon angular momentum and the resulting magnetic response remains unresolved. Here we distinguish the roles of pseudo-angular momentum and mechanical angular momentum in tetragonal {\alpha}-TeO2. Combining phase-resolved terahertz spectroscopy with magneto-optical Kerr measurements (MOKE), we reconstruct the motion of a doubly degenerate phonon and continuously control its angular momentum. The MOKE response follows the sign and magnitude of phonon angular momentum as well as its dynamics, whereas non-rotating phonons, including those with finite pseudo-angular momentum, produce no detectable magnetic signal. The MOKE rotation corresponds to an effective magnetic field of approximately 14 mT, nearly seven orders of magnitude above the estimate for spatially uniform ionic currents. Moreover, the Kerr response scales linearly with the emitted terahertz field, rather than quadratically as predicted by the uniform-current model. A localized Raman-excitation model involving Te lone-pair states accounts for this scaling and yields a comparable field magnitude with the MOKE data when electronic enhancement is included. These findings identify mechanical lattice rotation as the dominant dynamical variable for the observed magnetic response and suggest that localized Raman coupling contributes to its unexpectedly large magnitude.

### Paper [223]
Title: Derivation of Novel Isotropic Metamaterials at the Hashin Shtrikman Stiffness Limit Enabled by Physics Informed Neural Networks
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02622
Authors: Manish Kumar Singh, Dominic Kang Jueh Lim, Anson Wen Han Cheong, Adams Wai Kin Kong, Chang Quan Lai
Abstract: Current methods of deriving ultrastiff isotropic metamaterials require heuristics or topology optimization, which are highly inefficient and limited by human input. To circumvent this, Physics Informed Neural Networks are applied to analytical equations to systematically solve for plate orientations in closed cell lattices. Following a standardized procedure, the plate orientations were modelled and assembled into geometries resembling quasi random foams for validation via finite element simulations. Closed cell 6-plate geometries were also additively manufactured using LAPIS and experimentally characterized. The results indicate that a minimum of 6 plates is required to constitute an ultrastiff isotropic lattice, 7 plates would return the established plate Cubic Octet structure while 8 to 10 plate geometries did not show significant differences in properties. Despite the introduction of some plate bending due to sub-optimal plate placement strategy, relative stiffness of the PINNs lattices adheres closely to the Hashin Shtrikman upper bound, 90 percent of Hashin Shtrikman upper bound at a relative density of 0.4, and the lattices maintain near isotropic behaviour, achieving a Zener ratio of 0.95 at a relative density of 0.1. Benchmarking results show that specific stiffness of PINNs lattices is higher than the majority of isotropic architectures reported in the literature.

### Paper [224]
Title: Maximal correlation and optimal transformations of probability distributions in tensor-train format: a variational algorithm
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02731
Authors: Kayo Kinjo, Jun Ohkubo
Abstract: The correlation between two subsystems $A$ and $B$ of a many-body system is usually measured through a pair of observables chosen in advance. The Hirschfeld-Gebelein-R\'enyi maximal correlation removes the choice: it is the largest correlation coefficient between any function of the configuration of $A$ and any function of the configuration of $B$, and the two functions that reach it, the optimal transformations, are the observables that carry the correlation. Both follow from a singular value decomposition (SVD) of the joint distribution normalized by its marginals, a matrix whose dimension is the number of configurations. We show that this SVD can be computed variationally for a classical distribution of discrete variables, such as the Boltzmann distribution of a spin model, given in the tensor-train (matrix-product) format, without ever forming the matrix. The optimal transformations are sought as tensor trains; each step updates a single core in closed form, and the inverse of a small matrix contracted from the distribution and the remaining cores performs the division by the marginals core by core. Every iterate is a feasible pair, so the objective is a lower bound on the maximal correlation that increases monotonically, and the cost grows linearly with the number of variables. The algorithm reproduces the exact SVD on small systems, recovers the known nonlinear optimal transformations of synthetic distributions of up to ten angular variables ($10^{15}$ configurations), and, for the bipartite antiferromagnetic model with $2^{32}$ configurations, returns an optimal transformation that is a function of the group magnetization, linear above the transition and sign-like below it: the staggered order parameter is read off from the distribution instead of being postulated.

### Paper [225]
Title: Site-orbit masked twin-branch Transformer for symmetry-constrained crystal generation
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02906
Authors: Junchi Liu, Haoran Si, Luo Yan, Sergei Tretiak, Liujiang Zhou
Abstract: Generative models for crystal structure prediction (CSP) have expanded the search space for novel materials and improved screening efficiency. However, simultaneously generating stable and novel structures remains challenging because of the complex structural and physical constraints governing crystals. To address this challenge, we propose site-orbit masked twin-branch Transformer model (ST2M), a symmetry-guided discrete--continuous diffusion framework incorporating degree-of-freedom masks and conditioned on Wyckoff patterns. ST2M represents crystal structures as sequences of independent occupied Wyckoff orbits and employs a twin-branch Transformer to jointly denoise discrete and continuous variables. In our experiments, ST2M achieves a structural validity of 96.37\%. For known patterns in dataset, 23.86\% of generated structures simultaneously satisfy stability, uniqueness, and novelty criteria, representing a relative improvement of 68.15\% over the best-performing baseline. First-principles calculations identify a total of 21 previously unreported stable structures among selected candidates with low predicted energies. These results demonstrate that ST2M can generate chemically viable crystal candidates under prescribed crystallographic constraints and generalize to unknown Wyckoff patterns, providing a practical route to accelerated materials discovery.

### Paper [226]
Title: Inducing cooperative dynamics in an inhomogeneous many-emitter ensemble with a pulse sequence
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02973
Authors: Vincent P. Iglesias-Cardinale, Shreekanth S. Yuvarajan, Herbert F. Fotso
Abstract: Driving a many-particle quantum system with an appropriately tuned external field can give rise to features in its nonequilibrium dynamics that are absent in the equilibrium counterpart. Cooperative dynamics in a dense ensemble of quantum emitters manifested through superradiance, a burst in the emission intensity as a function of time, is typically suppressed by spectral disparities among emitters in the ensemble. We demonstrate the enhancement of cooperative dynamics in a spectrally inhomogeneous ensemble of quantum emitters when it is driven by a periodic pulse sequence. Using both a semi-analytical mean-field solution and a digital quantum simulation, we examine the coherence of the pulse-driven ensemble and the intensity of the emitted radiation as a function of the spectral disparity in the ensemble, as a function of time, the number of atoms in the system, and as a function of the inter-pulse delay. We identify clear signatures of the enhanced cooperative dynamics for the pulse-driven inhomogeneous ensemble when dephasing in the dynamics of the ensemble is suppressed by a sufficient number of pulses during the individual emitter lifetime. These results demonstrate a post-fabrication tuning knob based on externally applied control fields for the dynamics of the many-emitter ensemble.

### Paper [227]
Title: Emergent stigmergy and pheromone-guided decisions in a foraging collective
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02997
Authors: Manoj N H, S Ganga Prasath
Abstract: Ants in a colony forage and recruit other nest-mates for cooperative transport of food through stigmergy. Traditional approach to capture the foraging dynamics relies on fine-tuning phenomenological rules for pheromone deposition, chemotaxis, evaporation, and behavioral switching after reaching the food source. Here, we show that stigmergy emerges from an infinite-horizon stochastic optimal control problem involving outbound foragers traveling from the nest to the food source and returning agents traveling from the food source to the nest. Pheromone dynamics along a trail emerge from the forward propagating Hamilton--Jacobi--Bellman (HJB) equation, with the Bellman discount rate setting the rate of pheromone evaporation. Using this mapping, we identify the conditions under which trail feedback suppresses exploration or leads to the exploitation of established trails. For ants exploring multiple trails, we also find flux-potential relations, analogous to Ohm's law, with ant flux and inverse of squared value field playing the roles of current and resistance on the network. Our model captures collective behavior observed in the classic double-bridge experiments: trail selection on identical routes through a symmetry-breaking instability, selection of the shorter bridge, and bistability in the trail-length ratio that can leave ants transiently committed to the longer route. Our formulation places collective foraging in the broader framework of stochastic optimal control, while simultaneously providing an unifying picture on their emergent behavior consistent with experimental observations.

### Paper [228]
Title: Noise-resilient collective order through temporal memory in self-propelled particles
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03003
Authors: Mihir Durve, Sauro Succi, Antonio Celani
Abstract: Temporal memory can stabilise collective motion by filtering noisy local observations, but persistent information can also impede adaptation when the collective state changes. We investigate this competition in Vicsek-type systems using two distinct alignment rules: a finite buffer of past observations and a recursive update of an internal directional state. In both models, increasing effective memory shifts the loss of polar order to stronger noise and can restore ordered motion under conditions for which the corresponding memoryless Vicsek model is disordered. The stationary responses of the two models show an approximate correspondence when parameterised by a kernel-based effective memory, consistent with temporal averaging playing a common role in their ordering behaviour rather than from the details of the memory implementation. Their transient dynamics, however, are not determined by effective memory alone. Following a collective perturbation, recovery is fastest at an intermediate memory depth and becomes slower when outdated observations are retained too strongly. Temporal integration therefore produces a trade-off: deeper memory improves noise resilience, whereas excessive persistence reduces responsiveness. These results identify memory depth as a control parameter that separates the optimisation of stationary order from that of dynamical recovery in noisy active systems.

### Paper [229]
Title: Writing with Near-Field: towards Atomic Resolution in Material Processing
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03144
Authors: Haoran Mu, Hsin-Hui Huang, Tomas Katkus, Darius Gailevicius, Saulius Juodkazis
Abstract: Direct laser writing with ultra-short pulses is the most flexible route to two- and three-dimensional (2D/3D) material modification, yet its resolution is judged against the optical diffraction limit. This review argues the opposite: the finest, most reproducible modifications come not from shortening the wavelength but from localising the absorber. Through the threshold effect and a transient epsilon-near-zero (ENZ) state - the dielectric-to-metal (Die-Met) state of the excited solid - the deposited energy is confined to a dipole-like nano-seed whose near-field intensity falls as r^-6 (ideal point dipole). The localisation is thus set by the seed size, not the focal wavelength, and the final feature is shaped by the material response: post-pulse heat flow, cavitation and quenching. We show that polarisation controls the anisotropy of deposition: the quasi-static near-field and interface boundary conditions select the writing direction and the modification orientation, down to a single defect. We survey energy-deposition mechanisms from 100 nm to the single-nanometer scale and link them to working engineering: stitch-free large-area patterning and record aspect-ratio (10^3-10^4) dicing and wafering of transparent crystals. Finally, we conjecture guided volumetric deposition, in which laser-inscribed lossy nano-seeds act as antennas that let cavity microwaves anneal the modified nano-volumes from within, as a 2.45 GHz oven heats food through water-dipole absorption. Throughout, atomic resolution is used in a quantum-confined sense and denotes a direction of travel: 10-20 nm features are demonstrated, while deterministic single-defect and single-bond modification remain goals. The thesis is that ultrafast direct write beats diffraction-limited intuition because the material itself forms a transient nanoscale absorber whose near-field and boundary conditions govern energy deposition.

### Paper [230]
Title: A combined segmented anode gas ionization chamber and time-of-flight detector for heavy ion elastic recoil detection analysis
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03147
Authors: Petter Str\"om, Per Petersson, Marek Rubel, G\"oran Possnert
Abstract: A dedicated detector system for heavy ion elastic recoil detection analysis at the Tandem Laboratory of Uppsala University is presented. Benefits of combining a time-of-flight measurement with a segmented anode gas ionization chamber are demonstrated. The capability of ion species identification is improved with the present system, compared to that obtained when using a single solid state silicon detector for the full ion energy signal. The system enables separation of light elements, up to Neon, based on atomic number while signals from heavy elements such as molybdenum and tungsten are separated based on mass, to a sample depth on the order of one micrometer. The performance of the system is discussed and a selection of material analysis applications is given. Plasma-facing materials from fusion experiments, in particular metal mirrors, are used as a main example for the discussion. Marker experiments using nitrogen-15 or oxygen-18 are specific cases for which the described improved species separation and sensitivity are required. Resilience to radiation damage and significantly improved energy resolution for heavy elements at low energies are additional benefits of the gas ionization chamber over a solid state detector based system.

### Paper [231]
Title: PaddleMaterials: An End-to-End Deep Learning Toolkit for Materials Science
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03154
Authors: Xuwei Liu, Zhimin Zhang, Xiaoguang Hu, Zhongwei Liu, Yuanhui Sun, Jun Huo, Hua Wu, Haifeng Wang
Abstract: Machine learning supports materials design, atomistic simulation and experimental characterization, yet models and workflows remain fragmented. We present PaddleMaterials, an open-source framework integrating heterogeneous materials-learning tasks while preserving task-specific representations and scientific semantics. It unifies data processing, training, evaluation, pretrained-model management and workflows across six task classes: property prediction, structure generation, machine-learning interatomic potentials, electronic-structure fields, spectrum elucidation and microscopy image processing. Task interfaces record physical quantities, units, aggregation conventions and references, making cross-task composition verifiable. A shared compiler backend enables switching from dynamic-graph to compiled execution through one setting, using graph optimization, operator fusion and hardware-targeted code generation without changing model definitions. Across 65 model instances in 11 groups, compiled execution accelerated every instance in eight groups, with group median speedups of 11%-224% and group maximum speedups of 11%-336%. Case studies on LiMnO2 and toluene link structure generation or spectrum elucidation, interatomic-potential relaxation, property prediction and electron-density prediction, using same-geometry density functional theory (DFT) calculations as references. For LiMnO2, predicted formation energies for generated and relaxed structures differed from same-geometry PBE+U references by at most 7 meV per atom, while the computational cost of model steps was less than one-twentieth of a DFT single-point calculation. PaddleMaterials extends unified materials workflows beyond conventional property, potential and structure-generation tasks while supporting verifiable cross-task operations and compiled execution.

### Paper [232]
Title: Universality and Emergent Classicality in Krylov Space
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03188
Authors: Debarghya Chakraborty
Abstract: Krylov complexity grows linearly at late times for many systems. In this regime, we give a general spectral formula for the leading growth coefficients of Krylov complexity and its higher moments, without requiring detailed knowledge of the Lanczos coefficients. For states with a narrow energy distribution compared to the bandwidth, dynamics in the Krylov basis resembles motion along a classical trajectory. We demonstrate our results for the critical XY chain and double-scaled SYK. For the latter, we compute the leading late-time behavior of the chord-number moments through one-loop order.

### Paper [233]
Title: Spin SYK-dot chains: entanglement bound and resource estimates towards a quantum advantage
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03207
Authors: Sayantan Dey, Abel Carreras, Siddhartha Patra
Abstract: Ground states of Sachdev-Ye-Kitaev (SYK)-type models are volume-law entangled, which makes them costly both for tensor networks and for quantum hardware simulations. Thus, it remains unclear where quantum hardware could outperform classical simulation of such models. In this work, we study a spin SYK-dot chain, in which q dense SYK dots of p qubits are joined by single ZZ bonds of strength K. The entanglement across any cut is then bounded by floor(p/2) ln 2 plus the inter-dot entanglement, and the two-qubit depth of one Trotter step is set by p alone. A resource estimate shows that a quantum advantage for the ground state could appear only for dots of about 15 to 21 qubits or more. There, a single Trotter step already requires two-qubit error rates of order 10^{-6}, beyond the reach of noisy intermediate-scale quantum (NISQ) hardware. We test the assumptions of this estimate with an imaginary-time matrix product state (MPS) that applies each dot as one block. It agrees with exact diagonalisation at (p, q) = (6, 2). At (5, 2), a variational circuit with the same dot-plus-link layout comes within 3.6% of the exact energy, and noisy simulations show that its fidelity decays as assumed in the resource estimate. At (8, 5), i.e., N = 40 qubits, the energy converges at bond dimension chi = 768, far below the exact value 2^20, and the inter-dot entanglement stays small and is set mainly by K. Out-of-time-order correlators across the links decay on a time scale that shrinks rapidly with |K|. Thus, the dot size p and the inter-dot coupling K together set the entanglement of the chain, and with it where a quantum advantage could appear.

### Paper [234]
Title: Microscopic Origin of the Equation of Motion for Open Thermodynamic Systems
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03235
Authors: E. Aydiner
Abstract: In my previous work (arXiv:2609.07810) \cite{Aydiner_2026}, I derived a second-order equation of motion for open thermodynamic systems from the First Law of Thermodynamics and established its dynamical equivalence with mechanical oscillators and electrical RLC circuits. In the present study, I independently recover the same mathematical structure of the equation of motion from a mesoscopic model of particle transfer between two reservoirs connected by a channel. Starting from collective momentum balance, particle conservation, and reservoir chemical-potential response, I derive the inertial--dissipative--restoring dynamics and obtain explicit expressions for its effective coefficients in terms of the physical parameters of the system. The resulting equation predicts distinct damping regimes, characteristic relaxation time scales, and resonance under periodic driving. The natural frequency and resonance conditions are explicitly determined by the physical parameters of the particle-transfer system. In the slow-transport limit, the model recovers the conventional Onsager-type linear flux--force relation. The independent emergence of the same dynamical structure from two distinct physical approaches provides theoretical support for the previously proposed thermodynamic equation of motion. By identifying the physical mechanisms underlying its effective coefficients, the present study strengthens the physical foundations of the proposed framework and supports the possibility of describing open thermodynamic systems within a common dynamical framework shared with mechanical and electrical systems.

### Paper [235]
Title: When will androids outnumber humans?
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03242
Authors: Alessio Zaccone, Thomas Tunstall, Jesse H. Ausubel
Abstract: Humanoid robots are increasingly discussed not only as machines but also as a potential technological population. We develop a nonlinear population-dynamical framework to examine their possible diffusion relative to humans, without assuming that large-scale adoption or numerical crossover is inevitable. The human population $H(t)$ is calibrated to the United Nations World Population Prospects 2024 median trajectory. The android population $A(t)$ is represented through the normalized stock $a=A/A_s$. A conventional, human-organized manufacturing and adoption channel is calibrated to a conditional scenario spanning approximately $10^4$ humanoids in 2025 to $10^9$ in 2050 and follows $\dot a=(q_M+r_Ma)\exp(K_Ma^\gamma)$. We separately introduce a hypothetical autonomous channel in which installed androids contribute materially to producing additional androids. Under the minimal canonical TZ closure, the corresponding production--feedback number is $\mathcal{P}(a)=(r_A/\delta_A)\exp(K_Ra)$, where $r_A$ is the gross robot-mediated production coefficient, $\delta_A$ the retirement rate, and $K_R$ the autonomous feedback parameter. This channel is locally self-amplifying if and only if $\mathcal{P}>1$, whereas numerical human--android parity, $A=H$, is a separate cumulative condition. Global uncertainty analysis shows that crossover dates remain strongly scenario-dependent, while the production--feedback threshold is more robust. An extreme early-autonomy stress test can produce parity by 2050, but this represents a conditional dynamical boundary rather than a technological forecast.

### Paper [236]
Title: Dynamics of a two-dimensional microswimmer near a slippery boundary
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03243
Authors: Takahiro Kanazawa, Darren Crowdy
Abstract: Microswimmers near walls and interfaces can switch between qualitatively different modes of motion depending on the slip properties of the neighbouring boundaries. To study this theoretically, a two-dimensional point-singularity model of a microswimmer, which does not self-propel in unbounded fluid, is placed near a flat viscosity discontinuity between two fluids with viscosity ratio $\sigma$, and also near a partial-slip wall with Navier-slip length $\lambda$. Using complex-variable methods, the dynamical systems governing the swimmer motion are derived explicitly in both scenarios. Near the viscosity discontinuity, below a universal critical viscosity ratio $\sigma_c=1/2$, the swimmer is trapped at a fixed height with its stresslet axis normal to the boundary. When $\sigma=\sigma_c$ it glides parallel to the boundary at a speed selected by its orientation, with a terminal speed determined by its initial state. For $\sigma>\sigma_c$ it oscillates periodically in height and orientation while drifting along the boundary. The dynamics near a partial-slip wall depend on the ratio of the slip length to the length scale of the singularity and are similar in exhibiting an analogous transition at a critical slip length. It is found, however, that the dynamics near a viscosity discontinuity for $\sigma$ above $\sigma_c$ are richer than those near the partial-slip boundary, including the existence of a hydrodynamic bound state with zero net displacement over one period. Although the study is limited to two dimensions, these results show how a single boundary parameter organises qualitatively distinct modes of locomotion, in close analogy with slip-induced transitions reported for three-dimensional swimmers.

### Paper [237]
Title: From photons to fractons: Casimir effect in higher-rank electrodynamics and its fractonic branch
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03269
Authors: Nicola Maggiore
Abstract: The Casimir effect probes the modes that can propagate between boundaries. This makes it useful for fracton gauge theories, where restricted mobility follows from the simultaneous conservation of charge and dipole moment and changes the physical sector of the theory. We compare ordinary Maxwell electrodynamics with a Lorentz-covariant higher-rank theory of a symmetric tensor gauge field and with the scalar-charge fractonic branch selected from it by a preferred timelike background. The comparison shows why the distinction matters. In the full covariant theory, the Euclidean slab problem contains three independently propagating Maxwell-like modes and two coupled vector sectors that do not reduce to independent ordinary modes. Their boundary determinants are well defined, but a Lorentzian analysis shows that the vector sector has an unbounded Hamiltonian, so these determinants do not describe the vacuum energy of a stable fractonic phase. On the fractonic branch, the Gauss constraint removes that vector sector and leaves three stable massless modes: two transverse-traceless tensor modes and one scalar trace mode. For ideal reflectors, the physical Casimir energy is therefore 3/2 of the corresponding Maxwell result, for both equal and complementary boundary conditions. The calculation isolates the effect of the fractonic restriction on the radiative spectrum and clarifies which features of the full higher-rank boundary problem survive in the physical fracton sector.

### Paper [238]
Title: Free energy of the Hopfield model
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03306
Authors: Ayman Ali, Jeanne Boursier, Hong-Bin Chen, Fu-Hsuan Ho, Victor Issa, Jean-Christophe Mourrat
Abstract: We identify the limit of the free energy of the Hopfield model with independent symmetric Bernoulli patterns, for every temperature and loading ratio. A Hubbard-Stratonovich transformation reduces the problem to that of identifying the limit free energy of a bipartite spin glass with $\pm 1$ and spherical spins. One key difficulty is that the patterns, which become the coupling coefficients in the bipartite model, are Bernoulli instead of Gaussian, and the limit free energy is sensitive to the law of these coefficients. We show that the model is in fact equivalent to one involving Gaussian patterns and also subject to a finite number of Bernoulli random fields. We then solve this model by adapting the techniques recently introduced to identify the limit free energies of spin glasses with either $\pm 1$ or spherical spins.

### Paper [239]
Title: Functional Dimensional Regularization for the Lee-Yang Model
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03318
Authors: Piero Beretta, Carlos A. S\'anchez-Villalobos, Alessandro Codello
Abstract: We apply functional dimensional regularization (FDR) to the Lee-Yang universality class, a paradigmatic non-unitary theory, and compute its main critical exponents across the dimension range $3\leq d\leq 6$. By combining a second-order derivative expansion with polynomial field expansions, we obtain results that are compatible with resummed $\varepsilon$-expansion as well as with state-of-the-art estimates from Conformal Bootstrap and Fuzzy Sphere techniques. This demonstrates that, in the context of non-unitary theories, FDR retains the efficiency and rapid convergence to accurate critical exponents already observed in the Ising and O(N) cases.

### Paper [240]
Title: Symmetry breaking from conformality loss on holographic R\'enyi defects
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03413
Authors: Siqing Yu
Abstract: The R\'enyi entropies of a ball are computed by a conformal defect, the R\'enyi defect, dual in holography to a hyperbolic black hole. At an index $n_*$ its charged operator merges with its alternate-quantization partner, and the defect loses conformality. Beyond $n_*$ it flows with no source to a conformal defect that breaks the U(1) symmetry; below $n_*$ that defect needs a symmetry-breaking source, so at large $N$, conformality loss makes the breaking spontaneous. The scalar hair beyond $n_*$, expected to break the symmetries of hyperbolic space, thus preserves them outside a thin layer at the entangling surface, as conformal symmetry requires. The region outside the layer sets the universal term of the R\'enyi entropy. We demonstrate the mechanism in the probe Abelian Higgs model in AdS$_5$ at the Bogomolny point, where the saddles are in closed form and numerical methods confirm the flows. On a sphere we prove that a positive source-free flow exists exactly when $n>n_*$ and the sphere is large enough; it is then unique and ends at the symmetry-breaking defect. The mechanism needs a scalar mass in a finite window, and for potentials attractive at small amplitude, the symmetry can break before conformality is lost. For operators of low enough dimension, already the second R\'enyi entropy lies beyond the merger. Classical backreaction does not move the merger at any order; we argue that away from $n_*$ the flows persist at first order.

### Paper [241]
Title: Exceptional Points and Optimized Encoding in Qubit Stabilizer Codes
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03469
Authors: Yves Rottstaedt, Andrei I. Pavlovm Alexander Shnirman, Yuval Gefen, Bernd Rosenow
Abstract: Continuous blind stabilizer measurements turn stabilizer codes into a Liouvillian spectral problem in which Hamiltonian terms compete with dissipative error channels. We show that this competition generically enables Liouvillian exceptional points, whose occurrence at weak error rates is constrained by the code distance. Exceptional points are detrimental in this regime of error correction as they maximize the decay rate of encoded coherences. We then optimize the encoding of the logical qubit using the eigenmodes of the Lindblad operator. This phase-optimized encoding cancels the linear short-time decay of logical qubit coherences. We apply this encoding to the [[5,1,3]] code and demonstrate that it outperforms naive encoding methods near the exceptional point, improves the fidelity of a logical $\pi$ rotation and remains effective in the presence of additional weak error channels.

### Paper [242]
Title: Identifying the Shear Piezoelectric Coefficient using Physics-Informed Neural Networks under Exact Boundary and Initial Constraints
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03485
Authors: Suhas Suresh Bharadwaj
Abstract: The shear piezoelectric coefficient $e_{15}$ governs the shear-mode electromechanical coupling in piezoelectric micromachined ultrasonic transducers (PMUTs) and surface acoustic wave (SAW) devices, but measuring it typically requires destructive resonance testing on dedicated specimens. On simulated data, $e_{15}$ can instead be recovered non-destructively from sparse, noisy subsurface wavefield observations using a constrained physics-informed neural network (PINN). By building exact spatial distance functions and temporal multipliers directly into the network output, the clamped, grounded boundaries and resting initial state hold automatically, leaving the loss with only the interior equations of coupled electro-elastodynamics and the data misfit. Non-dimensional scaling brings all governing coefficients within one decade of O(1), and the setup removes the boundary and initial-condition penalty weights that soft-constraint methods must tune. In a six-way comparison (plane-strain PZT-5H, manufactured wavefield, 30 dB noise), a soft-penalty baseline with adaptive weights converges to an erroneous $e_{15}$ in four of the five runs (51.7% mean error), and fixed penalties reach an error of 0.85% only after a second-order optimization stage. The constrained formulations reach mean $e_{15}$ errors between 0.15% and 0.51% with held-out displacement errors below 1%. Within these variants, the spatial multiplier gives rapid first-order convergence, and the quadratic temporal multiplier lowers the early-time electrostatic residual by a factor of 6.8 versus a linear one. On finite-element data along one subsurface line, the full formulation recovers $e_{15}$ with 1.19% mean error at 30 dB and keeps it between 0.65% and 2.71% across a 40 to 0 dB noise sweep. Finally, training on displacements alone still recovers $e_{15}$ with mean errors of 0.45% on finite-element and 1.64% on manufactured data.

### Paper [243]
Title: Variational Functional Neural Networks (vfNN)
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03542
Authors: Chinmay Katke, C. Nadir Kaplan
Abstract: We present a variational functional neural network (vfNN) algorithm that optimizes physically motivated continuum functionals in integral form (e.g., Lagrangian, Hamiltonian, or free energy) to find the states of a system. That way, vfNN bypasses solving the equations of motion (a.k.a. the Euler-Lagrange equations), which are in the form of differential equations. To this end, our approach is fundamentally different from conventional physics-informed neural networks (PINN) that solve differential equations by minimizing a phenomenological loss function involving higher order derivatives and ad hoc weight factors. In contrast, vfNN merely evaluates lower order derivatives of the generating functional and replaces the weight factors with rigorous physical scales. This also fundamentally changes the loss landscape, and the desired solutions may become unstable. For this emergent nonconvex optimization problem, we develop a quasi-Newton optimization algorithm with simulated annealing that converges to the solutions of the corresponding Euler-Lagrange equations of the functional regardless of their stability properties. Our simulations using vfNN give accurate results for benchmark systems governed by linear or nonlinear ordinary differential equations.

### Paper [244]
Title: The Virasoro Bootstrap: from the $c\sim1$ desert to coupled Potts
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03565
Authors: Stefanos R. Kousvos, Alessandro Piazza, Alessandro Vichi
Abstract: We develop a numerical conformal bootstrap for two-dimensional CFTs with $c>1$ based on Virasoro conformal blocks, computed with Zamolodchikov's recursion and optimal polynomial interpolation. As a first test we isolate an island around three decoupled Ising models. We then consider three 3-state Potts models coupled through their energy operators, a candidate irrational CFT. Mixed correlators isolate the decoupled theory in an island, and different gap assumptions, motivated by perturbation theory, yield a peninsula that contains the coupled fixed point but not the decoupled one. Finally, we explore the standard single-correlator bound on the lowest-dimension scalar primary in the OPE of two identical scalars. We find that for central charges just above $c=1$ the bound collapses over a wide range of external dimensions. This excludes any theory whose lightest scalar is absent from its own OPE and lies in this range, and supports the conjecture that no compact CFT exists in this region. We explain several features of these bounds analytically.

### Paper [245]
Title: Mutual information to all orders from modular flow: a replica-free approach
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03630
Authors: Hewei Frederic Jia
Abstract: We derive an all-orders long-distance expansion of vacuum mutual information between two disjoint balls in generic $d$-dimensional CFTs. The derivation uses a novel radial sewing expansion of the joint vacuum density matrix, together with a modular response formula for relative entropy and the geometric modular flow of a single ball, without analytic continuation in replica number. The expansion coefficients are modular integrals of modular-flowed vacuum correlators. Our formula provides a systematic treatment of arbitrary multi-point sectors, and recovers known results on two-point sector derived from replica methods. The full two-point sector resums into a modular integral of the thermal partition function on $S^{d-1}$, with an effective temperature depending on ball configuration and modular time. We compute the expansion explicitly in CFTs. In two-dimensional Ising CFT, using analytic reductions and multidimensional numerical integration, we obtain the first ten radial orders in the long-distance expansion, which involve sphere correlators with up to twenty spin insertions. The truncated expansion agrees well with lattice data at finite separation, and approximately satisfies crossing symmetry. Analytic calculations also reproduce known expansion coefficients for 2d free compact boson at self-dual radius and its current sector, as well as for free scalars in higher dimensions.

### Paper [246]
Title: Theory of Berry Singularity Markers: Diagnosing Topological Phase Transitions via Lock-In Tomography
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2111.12957
Authors: Panagiotis Kotetes
Abstract: This work brings forward an alternative approach for inferring the topological character of phase transitions in insulators. The method relies on subjecting the system of interest to a set of external fields which consist of two parts, i.e., a weak spatiotemporally slowly-varying component on top of a constant offset. This process maps the Berry singularities dictating the bulk band structure of the insulator to a synthetic space spanned by the control parameters of the external fields. By measuring the response of the system to the weak part of the perturbations, when these are additionally chosen to form spacetime textures, one can construct a quantity that is here-termed Berry singularity marker (BSM). The BSM enables the Berry singularity detection as it becomes nonzero only in the close vicinity of a Berry singularity and is equal to its charge. Moreover, near the Berry singularities, the BSM encodes the quantum metric tensor of the system. An advantage of the present method is that it can be implemented in a lock-in fashion, thus allowing to ``filter out'' disorder effects, therefore opening the door to the resilient diagnosis of topological phase transitions.

### Paper [247]
Title: Storage and Learning phase transitions in the Random-Features Hopfield Model
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2303.16880
Authors: Matteo Negri, Clarissa Lauditi, Gabriele Perugini, Carlo Lucibello, Enrico M. Malatesta
Abstract: The Hopfield model is a paradigmatic model of neural networks that has been analyzed for many decades in the statistical physics, neuroscience, and machine learning communities. Inspired by the manifold hypothesis in machine learning, we propose and investigate a generalization of the standard setting that we name Random-Features Hopfield Model. Here $P$ binary patterns of length $N$ are generated by applying to Gaussian vectors sampled in a latent space of dimension $D$ a random projection followed by a non-linearity. Using the replica method from statistical physics, we derive the phase diagram of the model in the limit $P,N,D\to\infty$ with fixed ratios $\alpha=P/N$ and $\alpha_D=D/N$. Besides the usual retrieval phase, where the patterns can be dynamically recovered from some initial corruption, we uncover a new phase where the features characterizing the projection can be recovered instead. We call this phenomena the learning phase transition, as the features are not explicitly given to the model but rather are inferred from the patterns in an unsupervised fashion.

### Paper [248]
Title: Polarons in atomic gases and two-dimensional semiconductors
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2501.09618
Authors: Pietro Massignan, Richard Schmidt, Grigori E. Astrakharchik, Ata\c{c} \.Imamoglu, Martin Zwierlein, Jan J. Arlt, Georg M. Bruun
Abstract: In this work we provide a comprehensive review of theoretical and experimental studies of the properties of polarons formed by mobile impurities strongly interacting with quantum many-body systems. We present a unified perspective on the universal concepts and theoretical techniques used to characterize polarons in two distinct platforms, ultracold atomic gases and atomically-thin transition metal dichalcogenides, which are linked by many deep parallels. We review polarons in both fermionic and bosonic environments, highlighting their similarities and differences including the intricate interplay between few- and many-body physics. Various kinds of polarons with long-range interactions or in magnetic backgrounds are discussed, and the theoretical and experimental progress towards understanding interactions between polarons is described. We outline how polaron physics, regarded as the low density limit of quantum mixtures, provides fundamental insights regarding the phase diagram of complex condensed matter systems. Furthermore, we describe how polarons may serve as quantum sensors of many-body physics in complex environments. Our work highlights the open problems, identifies new research directions and provides a comprehensive framework for this rapidly evolving research field.

### Paper [249]
Title: Direct signatures of Anderson orthogonality catastrophe in nonequilibrium quantum dots
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2507.03763
Authors: Sarath Sankar, Joshua Folk, Yigal Meir, Eran Sela
Abstract: We propose schemes for unambiguous direct observation of Anderson orthogonality catastrophe (AOC) effects in a quantum dot coupled to a charge detector, enabling experimental estimation of the AOC exponent $\alpha$. We show that certain easy-to-measure observables have a robust dependence on $\alpha$ in the non-equilibrium regimes of source-drain voltage bias or thermal imbalance. Our results are obtained using a rate equation formalism in which the AOC effects on tunnel rates are incorporated in an exact manner, and directly support recent experimental results.

### Paper [250]
Title: Long-range model for electronic pseudospin-nuclear-spin coupling and two-stage ordering in TmVO$_4$
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2507.16619
Authors: Sayan Ghosh, Anirudha Menon, Manoranjan Kumar, Rajiv R. P. Singh
Abstract: We study an infinite-range coupled electronic-pseudospin and nuclear-spin model for ferroquadrupolar and nuclear-spin ordering in TmVO$_4$ in external magnetic and strain fields. This material is an experimental realization of a transverse-field Ising model, where the Ising degree of freedom is quadrupolar and non-magnetic, but a transverse component is magnetic and couples both to external magnetic fields and to the nuclear spins via a hyperfine coupling. At zero external magnetic field, the electronic and nuclear degrees of freedom undergo well-separated two-stage ordering, accompanied by the release of their respective entropies. A transverse magnetic field polarizes the electronic orbital moments and, via the hyperfine coupling, the nuclear spins. The quadrupolar ordering temperature is gradually reduced to zero. However, the nuclear transition is replaced by a crossover at nonzero magnetic field. Quantum fluctuations are enhanced near the phase transitions and lead to peaks in the magnetic susceptibility. The spectral functions reveal a softening of a low-energy mode near the quantum critical point, consistent with the closing of the excitation gap and its reopening in the disordered phase. These features provide direct dynamical signatures of the field-driven quantum critical phenomena.

### Paper [251]
Title: Emergent Gauge Flux in Mixed QED$_3$ with Flavor Chemical Potential: Implications for Magnetized U(1) Dirac Spin Liquids
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2508.08528
Authors: Chuang Chen, Urban F. P. Seifert, Kexin Feng, Oleg A. Starykh, Leon Balents, Zi Yang Meng
Abstract: We design a lattice model of a "mixed" U(1) gauge field coupled to fermions with a flavor chemical potential and solve it with large-scale determinant quantum Monte Carlo simulations. In this mixed construction, dynamical magnetic monopole events are suppressed in the temporal-continuum limit. For zero flavor chemical potential, the model realizes three-dimensional quantum electrodynamics (QED$_3$) which has been argued to describe the ground state and low-energy excitations of the Dirac spin liquid phase of quantum antiferromagnets. At finite flavor chemical potential, which corresponds to a Zeeman field perturbing the Dirac spin liquid, we find a "chiral flux" phase which is characterized by the generation of a finite mean emergent gauge flux and, accordingly, the formation of relativistic Landau levels for the Dirac fermions. We use field-theory arguments to argue that in this state a U(1)$_m$ magnetic symmetry is spontaneously broken, leading to a gapless photon mode. Because of spin-flux attachment this mode would contribute to the low-energy longitudinal spin response. We numerically compute longitudinal and transverse spin structure factors which match our continuum and lattice mean-field theory predictions. In a different region of the phase diagram, strong fluctuations of the emergent gauge field give rise to an antiferromagnetically ordered state with gapped Dirac fermions coexisting with a deconfined gauge field. We also find an interesting intermediate phase where the chiral flux phase and the antiferromagnetic phase coexist. We argue that our results pave the way to testable predictions for magnetized Dirac spin liquids in frustrated quantum antiferromagnets.

### Paper [252]
Title: Unified Learning of Interatomic Potentials and Electronic Hamiltonians, with Applications in 2D Materials
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.16605
Authors: Moon-ki Choi, Daniel Palmer, Shivam Sharma, Harley T. Johnson
Abstract: We introduce UnIPH, an equivariant graph neural network designed to jointly predict both interatomic potentials and tight-binding (TB) Hamiltonians for an atomic structure. UnIPH is trained using density functional theory (DFT) calculations followed by Wannier projection to predict energies and forces as node-level targets and Wannier-projected TB Hamiltonians as edge-level targets. This enables physically consistent modeling of coupled mechanical-electronic responses with near-DFT-level accuracy. After separately training two UnIPH models on DFT data for bilayer graphene and monolayer MoS2, respectively, we find that these models capture key deformation-electronic effects: in twisted bilayer graphene, the trained UnIPH reveals how interlayer spacing, in-plane strain, and out-of-plane corrugation drive isolated flat-band formation, and further shows that modulating substrate interaction strength can generate flat bands even away from the magic angle (approximately 1.1 degrees). For monolayer MoS2, UnIPH accurately reproduces phonon dispersions and strain-dependent band-gap evolution, and further predicts how a non-uniform substrate creates localized strain pockets that spatially modulate the conduction- and valence-band edges.

### Paper [253]
Title: Superconductivity of bilayer two-orbital Hubbard model for La$_{3}$Ni$_{2}$O$_{7}$ under high pressure
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.01801
Authors: Wei-Yang Chen, Cui-Qun Chen, Meng Wang, Shou-Shu Gong, Dao-Xin Yao
Abstract: By combining density functional theory (DFT) and density matrix renormalization group calculations, we investigate the unusual pressure dependence of superconducting transition temperature ($T_c$) in the nickelate superconductor La$_{3}$Ni$_{2}$O$_{7}$. Using the hopping integrals and on-site potentials obtained by fitting the DFT band structures, we map a quantum phase diagram of a bilayer two-orbital Hubbard model with increasing pressure in a ladder geometry, which has an intermediate Hubbard repulsion and a Hund's coupling. Near $3/8$ filling, we find a strong spin density wave order, which at $3/8$ filling shows a real-space spin pattern similar to the spin-charge stripe order along a lattice direction. At $21/64$ filling, we find a superconducting phase with interlayer superconductivity (SC) in both the $d_{z^2}$ and $d_{x^2-y^2}$ orbitals, as well as in-plane SC in the $d_{z^2}$ orbital. Intriguingly, the SC is weakened with increasing pressure and transits to a Luttinger liquid above $80$ GPa, which qualitatively agrees with the experimental observations of decreasing $T_c$ with increasing pressure and a transition to Fermi liquid above $80$ GPa in La$_{3}$Ni$_{2}$O$_{7}$. Through a comparative study, we further show that the ratio of interaction to hopping integral, which reduces moderately with increasing pressure, may play a dominant role in the weakening of SC. Our results of this experimentally relevant model not only find a robust SC through suppressing the competing spin density wave order, but also give new insight into the unusual pressure dependence of SC in La$_{3}$Ni$_{2}$O$_{7}$.

### Paper [254]
Title: Multiterminal Quasiballistic Josephson Effect in Monocrystalline Gold
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.00409
Authors: K. B. Polevoy, G. A. Bobkov, D. S. Kalashnikov, A. G. Shishkin, I. V. Trofimov, A. M. Bobkov, E. A. Sedov, E. A. Krivko, M. A. Tarkhov, I. V. Bobkova, V. S. Stolyarov
Abstract: We report on the realization of a planar, quasiballistic Josephson junction array using a Au micron-sized single crystal. The system exhibits a nonlocal, multiterminal Josephson effect, where the supercurrent between any two superconducting leads is governed by the phase coherence across the entire crystal. Key evidence includes a nonmonotonic dependence of the critical current on junction length and magnetic interference patterns with periods corresponding to the shared normal-metal area. Nonlocal transport measurements further confirm that the supercurrent between two electrodes depends on the phase configuration of all the others. Our results, supported by a developed theoretical model, establish a platform for exploring complex superconducting phenomena in multiterminal ballistic systems.

### Paper [255]
Title: Theory of planar quasiballistic Josephson junctions
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.00411
Authors: G. A. Bobkov, I. V. Bobkova, A. M. Bobkov, K. B. Polevoy, V. S. Stolyarov
Abstract: We develop a theoretical framework for planar quasiballistic Josephson junctions, where multiple superconducting leads are coupled through a large, nearly ballistic normal metal crystal. Our approach, based on quasiclassical Eilenberger equations, accounts for the dominant role of electron reflections from the crystal surfaces or single impurities, a mechanism distinct from both purely ballistic and diffusive limits. We calculate the critical current between superconducting leads for various geometries, examining its dependence on temperature and magnetic field. Crucially, we demonstrate that in multiterminal setups, the junctions are not independent but form a strongly coupled system. The theory successfully explains key experimental observations from a companion work [Polevoy et al., Phys. Rev. Lett. 137, 166001 (2026)], including a non-monotonic dependence of the critical current on the interlayer length, providing a foundation for designing and understanding complex multi-terminal Josephson systems.

### Paper [256]
Title: Finite-Temperature Phase Transition and Logarithmic Quasi-Long-Range Order in the Two-dimensional Long-range Heisenberg Model
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.01956
Authors: Tianning Xiao, Dingyun Yao, Lode Pollet, Zhijie Fan, Youjin Deng
Abstract: We investigate the two-dimensional classical Heisenberg ($N=3$) model with long-range (LR) interactions decaying as $1/r^{2+\sigma}$, focusing on the marginal case $\sigma=2$. Large-scale Monte Carlo simulations up to linear size $L=8192$ reveal a finite-temperature phase transition, in striking contrast to the short-range case where the system is asymptotically free. An adiabatic renormalization-group analysis shows that the LR interaction provides a positive contribution to the stiffness flow and gives rise to a weak continuous transition through non-topological spin-wave fluctuations. We show that the low-temperature phase exhibits a novel logarithmic quasi-long-range order (log-QLRO), with correlations decaying as $C(r)\sim(\ln r)^{-\eta_\ell}$ and vanishing magnetization in the strict thermodynamic limit. High-precision data confirm this picture, including the predicted identity $2\pi\kappa_R\eta_\ell=N-1$, with $\kappa_R$ the renormalized spin stiffness. The decay, with $\eta_\ell\propto T$, is slower than any power law, making the phase practically indistinguishable from true long-range order in realistic systems. Our results establish a novel log-QLRO phase and a continuous transition beyond the Ginzburg-Landau-Wilson paradigm in the two-dimensional Heisenberg model at $\sigma=2$.

### Paper [257]
Title: Symmetry-Protected Topological Structures in Quantum Many-Body Scar Subspaces
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.11216
Authors: Chihiro Matsui, Thomas Quella, Naoto Tsuji
Abstract: We investigate how symmetry-associated topological properties, originally defined for ground states, can be consistently extended to dynamically isolated subspaces of quantum many-body systems. Focusing on quantum many-body scars (QMBS), we show that a scar subspace is invariant under the relevant symmetries, and it can support well-defined topological properties inherited from an underlying symmetry-protected topological (SPT) ground state, even though individual scar states need not themselves be symmetric. We refer to such structures as SPT-like scar subspaces, emphasizing that the topological properties are defined at the level of the symmetry-invariant subspace rather than on a state-by-state basis. As a concrete example, we analyze the spin-$1$ Affleck--Kennedy--Lieb--Tasaki (AKLT) model, where a restricted spectrum-generating algebra (rSGA) generates a tower of QMBS on top of a nontrivial SPT ground state admitting a matrix product state (MPS) representation. We demonstrate that the resulting symmetry-invariant scar subspace inherits well-defined topological characteristics, encoded in bond-space symmetry representations, expectation values of symmetry-twist operators, and long-range string order in the dilute regime. We further show that these features persist in a broader class of models, including deformations and higher-spin generalizations that preserve the relevant symmetries and admit nontrivial SPT ground states with MPS representations. Our results provide a general framework in which symmetry-associated topological properties, while inherited from the ground state, are consistently realized at the level of symmetry-invariant nonthermal subspaces. This perspective clarifies how topological structures can be meaningfully defined beyond individual eigenstates.

### Paper [258]
Title: Thermomagnonic Torques in Insulating Altermagnets
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.14660
Authors: Edward Schwartz, Hamed Vakili, Alexey A. Kovalev
Abstract: We develop a symmetry-controlled theory of anisotropic thermomagnonic torques in insulating altermagnets. Starting from the stochastic LLG equations, we identify a spin-splitter magnonic torque associated with thermally generated, sublattice-odd spin currents and an anisotropic entropic torque dictated by crystal symmetry. These torques produce anisotropic magnetic-texture responses to temperature gradients. In particular, thermally generated spin currents induce domain-wall precession, which reduces domain-wall velocities for selected gradient directions. We also predict an anisotropic skyrmion Hall response, with symmetry-selected directions enabling fast skyrmion motion with strongly suppressed transverse deflection. Our results reveal experimentally testable symmetry fingerprints of insulating altermagnets and extend more broadly to anisotropic magnets with exchange-driven magnon spin splitting.

### Paper [259]
Title: Non-invertible symmetries and mixed anomalies from equations of motion in (3+1)D twisted $BF$ topological quantum field theories
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.01523
Authors: Zhi-Feng Zhang, Yizhou Huang, Qing-Rui Wang, Peng Ye
Abstract: We develop a systematic study on the topological operators that generate invertible and non-invertible higher-form symmetries in $\left(3+1\right)D$ twisted $BF$ topological quantum field theories (TQFTs). We focus on the twisted $BF$ theories with gauge group $G=\prod_i \mathbb{Z}_{N_i}$ and an $a\wedge a\wedge b$ twist, where $a$'s and $b$ are 1-form and 2-form gauge fields, respectively.
