These theories realize non-Abelian $(3+1)$D TQFTs supporting Borromean-Rings braiding and describe three-dimensional non-Abelian topological orders in condensed matter. For $G=(\mathbb{Z}_2)^3$, a microscopic realization is given by the $\mathbb{D}_4$ Kitaev quantum double model. Starting from the equations of motion (EoMs), we construct gauge invariant operators that are supported on closed submanifolds, hence are genuine topological operators. Some of them are associated with nontrivial Hilbert spaces which are the projective representation spaces of specific gauge subgroups, thus being non-invertible. The others are invertible topological operators with group-like fusion rules. The fusion rules of non-invertible topological operators correspond to the decomposition of the product of the projective representation spaces. We provide a field-theoretical algorithm to calculate the fusion rules. Besides, we identify an obstruction to retaining a non-invertible topological operator after gauging an invertible symmetry, and derive the constraints on pairs of non-invertible operators relevant to simultaneous gauging. Our results give a unified and practical approach for identifying and characterizing higher-form symmetries and mixed anomalies, which can be extended to more general TQFTs and topological orders.

### Paper [260]
Title: Emergent Elasticity and Quasiconformal Flow in Active Solids
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.08968
Authors: Nikolas H. Claussen, Fridtjof Brauns, Boris I. Shraiman
Abstract: Elasticity theory rests on a constitutive relation between stress and strain relative to a reference state. Living matter, however, generates forces by motor molecule activity independent of strain, requiring a different approach to active tissue mechanics. We develop a theory of two-dimensional continuum mechanics in which the active force configuration, not a reference shape, is the fundamental input. Motivated by the cell-level Active Tension Network model for epithelia, we encode motor-driven intercellular forces in a Riemannian tension metric, whose mapping into physical space relates macroscopic stress to the microscopic tensions. Assuming force balance, we show that a stress-free reference state and an effective stress-strain relation emerge at the tissue scale, making the system an effectively elastic active solid. Extending from statics to dynamics, we show how such a solid can morph its shape through adiabatic evolution of active tensions. To capture plastic cell rearrangement, we introduce a second metric representing the cell adjacency graph in the continuum; topological rearrangement then appears as a continuous reparametrization of the tension manifold. The key fields characterizing the tissue's local mechanical state - tension anisotropy, macroscopic stress, and cell shape - arise from the discrepancies between the tension, adjacency, and physical metrics, represented mathematically by quasiconformal maps. Our theory explains how local contractility controls tissue shape and yields predictions distinct from existing models: tissues can remain rigid while "flowing", with a speed set by the rate of change of active forces rather than their magnitude, and shear flow can occur even under isotropic stress, since macroscopic stress is distinct from local tension anisotropy.

### Paper [261]
Title: INTERFACE Force Field for Alumina with Validated Bulk Phases and a pH-Resolved Surface Model Database for Aqueous, Electrolyte, and Molecular Interfaces
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.12570
Authors: Cheng Zhu, Krishan Kanhaiya, Samir Darouich, Sean P. Florez, Karnajit Sen, Patrick Keil, Nawel S. Khelfallah, Eduard Schreiner, Ratan K. Mishra, Hendrik Heinz
Abstract: Alumina and aluminum oxyhydroxides underpin numerous chemical engineering technologies in heterogeneous catalysis, corrosion protection, functional coatings, energy storage devices, and biomedical components. Yet molecular models that predictively connect phase structure, pH dependent surface chemistry, electrolyte organization, and adsorption across operating conditions remain limited. Here we introduce the INTERFACE Force Field (IFF) together with a curated, ready-to-use pH-resolved surface model database for an accurate and transferable atomistic description of major alumina phases according to experimental benchmarks available. The framework covers alpha-Al2O3, gamma-Al2O3, boehmite, diaspore, and gibbsite using a single parameter set compatible with CHARMM, AMBER, OPLS-AA, CVFF, and PCFF. Simulations reproduce experimental reference data across structural, thermodynamic, mechanical, and interfacial benchmarks with deviations of few percent relative to experiment, exceeding existing force fields and density-functional approaches. We introduce a transferable treatment of surface ionization and charge regulation across alumina phases over a broad pH range, enabling simulations of realistic solid-electrolyte interfaces without phase-specific reparameterization. Quantitative reliability is demonstrated by reproducing zeta-potential trends and pH-dependent adsorption of an organic corrosion inhibitor at alumina-water interfaces. Predicted adsorption free energies and surface contact times correlate with experiments across an order of magnitude, enabling process-relevant screening and explaining limitations of earlier adsorption models. The approach is 10,000 to 100,000 times faster than DFT-machine learned interatomic potentials (MLIPs) and supports the design of alumina-containing catalysts, corrosion-resistant coatings, energy storage and biomaterials under realistic process conditions.

### Paper [262]
Title: Nonreciprocal perfect Coulomb drag in electron-hole bilayers: coherent exciton superflow as a diode
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.02643
Authors: Jun-Xiao Hui, Qing-Dong Jiang
Abstract: Distinguishing an exciton condensate from an excitonic gas or insulator remains a fundamental challenge, as both phases feature bound electron-hole pairs but differ only by the emergence of macroscopic phase coherence. Here, we theoretically propose that a spin-orbit-coupled bilayer system can host a finite-momentum exciton condensate exhibiting a nonreciprocal perfect Coulomb drag -- the coherent-exciton diode effect. This effect arises from the simultaneous breaking of inversion and time-reversal symmetries in the exciton condensate, resulting in direction-dependent critical counterflow currents. The resulting nonreciprocal perfect Coulomb drag provides a clear and unambiguous transport signature of phase-coherent exciton condensation, offering a powerful and experimentally accessible approach to identify, probe, and control exciton superfluidity in solid-state platforms.

### Paper [263]
Title: Pathway to lowest-energy structures and stress relaxation for the surface triple junction of copper
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.01920
Authors: Ipen Demirel, Yuan Fang, Yan Liu, Haitian Shan, Xiaopu Zhang, Yuchuan Shao, Jianda Shao, John J. Boland
Abstract: The behavior of surface triple junctions (STJ) at emergent grain boundaries on free surfaces is critical to the microstructure evolution, and therefore to the stability of next generation interconnects. Yet, despite this significant importance, its lowest-energy structure and local stress have remained persistently unknown. Here, we fill this critical gap through high-resolution experimental mapping of the local surface deformation at STJs of a symmetrical tilt grain boundaries with [111] rotation axis and misorientation angles 13.17{\deg}, the analysis of the local core-shifted structure and stress relaxation, and ergodic searching metastable structures accordingly with the molecular-statics (MS) calculation. The computation clearly shows the zipped Y-shaped notch as the lowest-energy structures for this group of STJs, the line profile of which is consistent with the experimental observation. This energetic preference was well explained by the stress delocalization mechanism and was tested for a wide range of boundaries with even deeper core-shift depth using machine learning methods based on the data obtained from MS calculation. By revealing the elusive thermodynamics of STJs, our findings advance the research field by redefining the energetic framework for capillary driven structure evolution and providing foundation for understanding kinetically diffusive deformation and for engineering thin-film interconnects and related materials.

### Paper [264]
Title: A Vibrated Compacting Granular System: A DEM Light Scattering Comparison
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.01921
Authors: Linnea Heitmeier, Jan Philipp Gabriel
Abstract: We perform Discrete Element Method (DEM) simulations of granular particles (polystyrene spheres) vibrated inside a cubic container. The study investigates the evolution of the packing fraction with and without rotational friction at different shaking amplitudes. The mean-squared displacement (MSD) is used to analyze the particles' diffusive, subdiffusive, and superdiffusive behavior. By monitoring both the dynamics and density evolution, one can observe the system's glassification. The comparison with experiments shows that the MSDs from the simulations are significantly higher than the MSDs measured by Diffusing Wave Spectroscopy (DWS) \cite{kunzner2025dynamics}. Following our finding that the rotational MSD is of the same order of magnitude as the MSD measured in DWS experiments, we propose that the experimental signal is not dominated by translational motion but rather by rotational particle dynamics. This provides access to a relevant particle property that has previously been difficult to measure directly. Finally, we conclude that the system reaches a dynamically constrained state well before random close packing, with particle displacements already below the Lindemann length.

### Paper [265]
Title: Circular dichroism in second- and third-harmonic generation in the chiral topological semimetal CoSi
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.03983
Authors: Yuya Ominato, Masahito Mochizuki
Abstract: We theoretically investigate circular dichroism (CD) in second- and third-harmonic generation (SHG and THG) in the chiral topological semimetal CoSi. We demonstrate that both SHG and THG exhibit dichroic responses of order unity, while their robustness against spectral broadening is strikingly different. Specifically, while SHG-CD is strongly suppressed by dissipation, THG-CD remains robust over a wide frequency range. We show that this qualitative difference originates from the phase structure of the nonlinear current, where SHG-CD arises from subleading interference processes that are sensitive to dephasing, whereas THG-CD emerges already at the leading nonlinear order and is therefore protected against spectral broadening. As a result, THG-CD provides a robust probe of chirality encoded in nonequilibrium electronic dynamics. We further reveal non-monotonic frequency dependences and pronounced sensitivity of harmonic emission to the polarization state and crystallographic orientation of the driving field. Our results provide a representative example of robust nonlinear chiroptical responses in chiral topological semimetals and highlight the potential of high-harmonic spectroscopy as a probe of phase-resolved electronic dynamics.

### Paper [266]
Title: Rationalizing defect formation energies in metals and semiconductors with semilocal density functionals
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.05385
Authors: Jorge Vega Bazantes, Timo Lebeda, Akilan Ramasamy, Kanun Pokharel, Ruiqi Zhang, John Perdew, Jianwei Sun
Abstract: The study of defects in materials is of utmost importance for technological applications and the design of new materials. In this work, we analyze the performance of density functional approximations on two prototypical sets of defective systems: monovacancies in eight fcc metals, and interstitials in the semiconductor Si-diamond. Specifically, we compute defect formation energies using the local density approximation, the Perdew-Burke-Ernzerhof generalized gradient approximation, the meta-generalized gradient approximations (meta-GGAs) strongly constrained and appropriately normed (SCAN), its regularized version (r2SCAN), the Lebeda-Aschebrock-Kummel (LAK) meta-GGA, and the Heyd-Scuseria-Ernzerhof screened hybrid functional. For metals, the local density approximation shows better performance compared to the other approximations, whereas for silicon, the meta-generalized gradient approximation Lebeda-Aschebrock-Kummel yields outstand- ing accuracy, surpassing the hybrid functional and approaching the results of more computationally demanding Quantum Monte Carlo methods. To rationalize the different performances, we study the semilocal ingredients rs, s and {\alpha} in both the pristine and defective structures. We identify critical regions that indicate the observed trends of the defect formation energies and pave the way for improving density functional approximations.

### Paper [267]
Title: Scar subspaces stabilized by algebraic closure: Beyond equally-spaced spectra and exact solvability
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.11015
Authors: Chihiro Matsui
Abstract: We propose algebraic closure as a stabilizing mechanism for quantum many-body scar subspaces, showing that scar states need not rely on an embedded equally spaced spectrum or analytical solvability. We formulate Lie-algebraically closed invariant subspaces and construct local quantum many-body Hamiltonians that preserve an $\mathfrak{su}(N)$-invariant scar subspace but need not act diagonally within it. Such non-diagonal action mixes the conventional tower states while preserving the multidirectional lattice-like spectral structure, allowing nonthermal dynamics and persistent multifrequency oscillations to survive even when individual scar eigenstates become analytically intractable.

### Paper [268]
Title: Probing bilayer topological order with layer-resolved transport
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.19955
Authors: Hongquan Liu, J. I. A. Li, D. E. Feldman
Abstract: Shot noise has been used to measure fractional charges of anyons. The value of the charge imposes constraints on fractional statistics but does not determine it. This limitation is particularly important in multi-component systems. For example, the zero charge of neutral anyons in bilayer graphene gives no information about their statistics at all. We propose a way to shed light on the statistics of charged and neutral anyons in multi-component systems with layer-resolved or spin- resolved noise. The protocol applies to the fractional quantum spin Hall effect in MoTe$_2$, topological states in multi-layer graphene and bilayer GaAs, and to recently discovered fractional excitons in bilayer graphene. The approach relies on the relation between statistics and the distribution of the anyon charge over the components. Information about statistics can also be extracted from a simpler measurement of the layer-resolved electric current through a narrow constriction in a Hall bar even in the presence of long-range interactions and other non-universal effects.

### Paper [269]
Title: Replica Tensor Train
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.22718
Authors: Miha Srdinsek, Gabriel Gouraud, Xavier Waintal
Abstract: We describe a numerical many-body technique that is based on both tensor networks and quantum Monte Carlo. The variational ansatz is a tensor network that can harvest volume-law entanglement. It is constructed from a tensor train to which one applies a set of non-local operators that force several indices of the tensor train to represent the same physical index, hence its name -- replica tensor train (RTT). From the tensor network toolbox, it inherits the possibility to make linear combinations of these states and apply a certain class of operators. We can therefore find the ground-state of a local Hamiltonian in a purely algebraic way as in standard tensor network algorithms -- i.e. without using gradient descent methods. On the other hand, the volume-law structure forbids calculating physical observables directly. In much the same way as on a quantum computer where one can prepare a state but can only sample it at the end, here we have to use Markov Chain Monte Carlo to compute the observables. We further show that the approach can be extended to build Krylov-subspace ground-state methods within the variational manifold. We illustrate the different algorithms on a two-dimensional spin model with a transverse magnetic field, which can be solved by this approach at low computational cost.

### Paper [270]
Title: Bayesian phase transition for the critical Ising model: Enlarged replica symmetry in the epsilon expansion and in 2D
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.23346
Authors: Kay Joerg Wiese, Alapan Das, Adam Nahum
Abstract: A process that images or measures bond energies in the critical Ising model can be in distinct measurement 'phases', depending on the precision of measurement. We study the transition into the strong-measurement phase using replica field theory (an epsilon expansion around six dimensions), a symmetry analysis, and numerical simulations in two dimensions. The results show that certain inference problems possess unexpected structure associated with an enlarged symmetry of the replica description. The symmetry imposes constraints on correlation functions, and is an analog of the Nishimori phenomenon in the Ising spin glass, but in a distinct replica limit. It determines the exact critical exponent for an Edwards-Anderson-like correlator in the measured Ising model, both in 2D and near the upper critical dimension. The symmetry is present microscopically for certain measurement protocols, and it emerges in the infrared for generic protocols at the critical measurement strength. We also examine the epsilon expansion for models with power-law interactions and/or long-range measurement. Finally, we describe an analogous replica symmetry enlargement for measurement in Wegner's lattice gauge theory.

### Paper [271]
Title: Nonintegral Flux Trapping in Frustrated Josephson Networks of Triplet Superconductors
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.24734
Authors: Grayson R. Frazier, Colton Lelievre, Yi Li
Abstract: In a Josephson junction network, anisotropic coupling between spin triplet pairing correlations can lead to frustrated $d$ vector textures that support spontaneous Josephson currents and nonintegral flux trapping. Such networks can appear in superconducting polycrystals, as well as single-crystal superconductors. In analogy to classical spin systems, in which the presence of geometric frustration and anisotropic superexchange can lead to nontrivial spin textures, Josephson networks with anisotropic Josephson couplings cannot simultaneously optimize their $\mathrm{U}(1)$ superconducting phase differences and relative $d$ vector orientations. The internal pairing structure of Cooper pairs twists as they tunnel across the Josephson junction, and the $d$ vector texture enters as an emergent geometric phase which can spontaneously trap fractional flux. For real-valued Josephson couplings between unitary $d$ vectors, the emergent geometric phase reduces to a $\mathbb{Z}_2$ structure, allowing for spontaneous $\pi$-flux trapping above a critical antisymmetric coupling, distinct from usual half-quantum vortices. Further, we demonstrate a continuously tunable nonintegral flux for complex Josephson couplings. The results of this work reveal new routes to engineer frustrated Josephson networks from the interplay of magnetic textures and spin triplet superconducting pairing order.

### Paper [272]
Title: Constructing Bulk Topological Orders via Layered Gauging
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.27363
Authors: Shang Liu
Abstract: Understanding quantum phases and phase transitions in the presence of symmetries is a central objective of quantum many-body physics. A powerful modern paradigm for investigating this problem is topological holography, which relates symmetries in $k$ dimensions to "bulk" topological orders in $(k+1)$ dimensions. While conceptually profound, most existing bulk construction methods rely on sophisticated mathematical formalisms and can be difficult to apply to certain symmetry types. In this work, we propose a physically intuitive and versatile method, termed the layered gauging construction, to systematically generate $(k+1)$-dimensional (liquid or fracton) topological orders from $k$-dimensional generalized symmetries. Roughly speaking, the prescription is to stack many layers of $k$-dimensional quantum systems with certain symmetries into a $(k+1)$-dimensional pile, and then sequentially gauge a diagonal symmetry acting on each nearest-neighbor pair of layers. The detailed procedure depends on the specific symmetry types. We have successfully implemented the method in a number of examples in different spatial dimensions, with symmetries that are conventional, higher-form, subsystem, anomalous, nonabelian, or noninvertible. We hence conjecture the method to be very general. For example, from the subsystem symmetry of the $2d$ plaquette Ising model, we derive the X-cube model and also an anisotropic fracton topological order. Additionally, starting from an anomalous $Z_2$ symmetry in $1d$, we construct a new square lattice model realizing the double semion topological order. From a quantum-error-correction perspective, our layered gauging construction may be viewed as a generalization of the hypergraph-product construction beyond the standard Calderbank-Shor-Steane setting.

### Paper [273]
Title: Computing eigenpairs of quantum many-body systems with Polfed.jl
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.10191
Authors: Rok Pintar, Konrad Pawlik, Rafa{\l} \'Swi\k{e}tek, Miroslav Hopjan, Jan \v{S}untajs, Jakub Zakrzewski, Piotr Sierant, Lev Vidmar
Abstract: We present Polfed$.$jl, an open-source Julia package implementing the Polynomially Filtered Exact Diagonalization (POLFED) algorithm for computing mid-spectrum eigenvalues and eigenvectors (shortly, eigenpairs) of quantum many-body Hamiltonians. Access to such eigenpairs is essential for studying non-equilibrium many-body physics, but is hindered by the exponential growth of Hilbert-space dimension. POLFED addresses this challenge through a polynomial spectral transformation evaluated on the fly within a Lanczos iteration, preserving Hamiltonian sparsity and substantially reducing memory costs compared to other diagonalization methods. The package supports flexible energy targeting, automatic optimization of the spectral mapping for structured Hamiltonians, and GPU acceleration, which is particularly effective since the dominant computational cost reduces to repeated sparse matrix-vector multiplications. Benchmarks on disordered spin-chain and fermionic models demonstrate access to larger system sizes than alternative approaches, and CPU--GPU comparisons confirm significant speedups. In particular, we also provide code for constructing the quantum sun model Hamiltonian, a toy model of a many-body ergodicity-breaking transition. While our focus is on many-body Hamiltonians, Polfed$.$jl may be applied to any large sparse matrix.

### Paper [274]
Title: Spatial statistics for screening molecular structures
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.17147
Authors: Pranoy Ray, Surya R. Kalidindi
Abstract: The dominant paradigm in computational materials discovery relies on heavily parameterized deep architectures, including message-passing graph networks and equivariant models, that require millions of DFT-labeled training structures and produce non-convex latent representations that complicate continuous optimization for inverse design. These architectures are impractical in data-scarce regimes, which is the typical case in molecular screening, and exhibit well-documented limitations in capturing chemically disordered configurations and chiral geometries. This review presents feature engineering based on spatial statistics as a physically rigorous and immediately deployable alternative. Molecular structures are encoded as voxelized scalar fields, and two-point auto- and cross-correlations are evaluated deterministically via Fast Fourier Transforms, explicitly transferring the burden of spatial pattern recognition from the learning algorithm to a closed-form, physics-informed operation. Principal component analysis of the resulting correlation maps yields low-dimensional, strictly convex representations that support lean neural networks (<100k trainable parameters) and non-parametric surrogate models, achieving sub-2% prediction error with as few as 10 training samples. Demonstrated across periodic crystals, chemically disordered high-entropy alloys, and non-periodic organic molecules, this framework enables Bayesian active learning and zero-shot extrapolation on commodity hardware, which current large-scale architectures cannot replicate at equivalent data budgets.

### Paper [275]
Title: Band Structure and topology of a periodically deformed Kitaev honeycomb model
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.20181
Authors: Abdullah AlJishi, Ali AlSwaid, Moayad Ekhwan, Hocine Bahlouli, Raditya Weda Bomantara, Michael Vogl
Abstract: Motivated by the growing interest in spin liquids and topological phases, as well as the rise of deformation engineering, we study the combined effects of deformation and magnetic fields on the honeycomb Kitaev model. The Kitaev model, as one of the prototypical and exactly solvable spin liquid-hosting models, serves as a simple platform that demonstrates the rich physics one can expect at the intersection of deformation physics and quantum spin liquids. Our work builds on a simplified solution to the undeformed base model that we present. This simplified solution allows for a straightforward extension of our analysis to the deformed case. After incorporating periodic deformations into the Kitaev model (chosen for its similarity to moir\'e physics), we investigate the effects of a hexagonally symmetric deformation on the band structure. We find that deformation leads to a smaller Brillouin zone with new band gaps at the edges, indicating the potential for topological transitions. Finally, we introduce a magnetic field to break time-reversal symmetry and thereby allow for non-trivial topology. We find that, under specific parameter conditions, the magnetic field leads to multiple band-gap closings and openings. An investigation into topological properties reveals nontrivial Chern numbers and a plethora of topological transitions. The resulting nontrivial band topology may motivate future investigations of thermal Hall or related transverse responses, which are not calculated here. We also propose a bulk-measurement approach to the Chern numbers and a possible path to their physical realization. Most importantly, our results demonstrate the rich phenomenology that can arise from the interplay between deformation and spin-liquid physics.

### Paper [276]
Title: First-Principles Study of Fe Adsorption and Its Effects on the Mechanical and Electrical Properties of Monolayer and Bilayer Biphenylene Networks
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.21918
Authors: Xiao-Ke Zhang, Zheng-Zhe Lin
Abstract: Biphenylene network (BPN), a two-dimensional carbon allotrope, has attracted interest for its potential applications. Here, we systematically investigate Fe adsorption on monolayer and bilayer BPN using first-principles calculations, focusing on structural stability, mechanical properties, and electronic transport. On monolayer BPN, the average Fe adsorption energy generally becomes more negative with increasing Fe coverage, accompanied by nonmonotonic variations and increasingly pronounced Fe aggregation, eventually favoring three-dimensional growth. In bilayer BPN, the interlayer four-membered ring center is the most favorable adsorption site, with a single-Fe adsorption energy of -4.3 eV. Both pristine monolayer and bilayer BPN exhibit high in-plane Young's and shear moduli. Fe adsorption induces nonmonotonic changes in the in-plane stiffness of monolayer BPN, without systematic enhancement. In contrast, interlayer Fe incorporation dramatically enhances the out-of-plane stiffness of bilayer BPN. The effective out-of-plane elastic constant C33 increases from 24.59 GPa in pristine bilayer BPN to 515.63 GPa at an Fe/C ratio of 25%. Furthermore, the transport calculations reveal pronounced in-plane conductivity anisotropy, with substantially different conductivity values normalized by the relaxation time ({\sigma}/{\tau}) along the two crystallographic directions.

### Paper [277]
Title: Multipolar exchange in a many-body homonuclear mixture of atoms in different internal states
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.22375
Authors: M. Bulakhov, A. S. Peletminskii, Yu. V. Slyusarenko
Abstract: We develop a general method for constructing the many-body Hamiltonian of pairwise interactions describing homonuclear mixtures of atoms occupying states with different total angular momenta or other quantum numbers. This Hamiltonian explicitly accounts for isotropic interactions governed by low-energy s-wave scattering. The advantage of the irreducible spherical tensor operator formalism is demonstrated: these operators give the Hamiltonian an explicit physical structure, account for all scattering channels, and include multipolar exchange interactions. The latter correspond to the exchange of both angular-momentum projections and the total angular momentum. Particular realizations of the general Hamiltonian, widely used in the physics of ultracold gases, are also analyzed. The resulting Hamiltonian provides a universal framework for investigating a broad range of quantum many-body phenomena in bosonic and fermionic atomic gases.

### Paper [278]
Title: Application and Performance Assessment of Annealing Methods for Electrostatic-Energy-Based Configuration Search in Mixed Crystals
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.24557
Authors: Tack Saquai, Kenta Hongo, Ryo Maezono, Tom Ichibha
Abstract: In first-principles design of solid solutions and disordered materials, exhaustive evaluation of all possible substitutional configurations is often impractical because the number of site occupations increases exponentially. Here, we develop a framework for pre-screening mixed-crystal configurations using annealing methods, where the Ewald electrostatic energy is used as the objective function. Substitutional occupations are represented by binary variables, allowing the Ewald energy to be mapped onto an Ising-type Hamiltonian and the search for low-energy configurations to be formulated as a combinatorial optimization problem.
  We implement this formulation using simulated annealing (SA) and quantum annealing (QA), and benchmark their performance against exhaustive search. For the small-scale system CaYAlO$_4$, SA achieved a speed-up of about 30 times, while QA achieved a speed-up of more than 100 times; both methods identified all lowest-energy configurations. For the medium-scale system $\beta$-KSbF$_4$ and the large-scale Ba-doped SiAlON system, SA achieved speed-ups of about 200--300 times while stably identifying the lowest-energy structures. In contrast, QA showed limited speed-up for the medium-scale $\beta$-KSbF$_4$ system and missed some low-energy configurations, likely due to chain breaks.
  These results indicate that, among the approaches examined here, SA showed comparatively robust performance for Ewald-energy-based configuration search in mixed crystals. The proposed formulation can be implemented using publicly available libraries and provides a practical route for accelerating candidate-structure generation before first-principles calculations.

### Paper [279]
Title: Magnon bandstructure and topology in a periodically deformed Kagome lattice with DM interaction
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.00696
Authors: Mohammed Abdullah Hammadi, Mohammed Salman Alsadah, Husam Abdulmajeed Noorwli, Hocine Bahlouli, Michael Vogl
Abstract: We study the band structure and topology of magnons for a Heisenberg model with Dzyaloshinskii-Moriya (DM) interaction on a deformed Kagome lattice. For simplicity, we focus on a periodically deformed lattice with hexagonal symmetry and an enlarged unit cell. This enlarged unit cell gives rise to a more complex band structure than in the undeformed case. Analyzing band topology, there is a distinction between the topologically trivial case with anti-ferromagnetic coupling and the topologically non-trivial case with ferromagnetic coupling. In the anti-ferromagnetic case, a spin-space symmetry, also present in the classical ground state, enforces these topologically trivial states. In the ferromagnetic case, this symmetry is spontaneously broken by the classical ground state. Consequently, the band structure also hosts numerous topological features. Specifically, we observe many topological transitions and bands with Chern numbers ranging from $+2$ to $-2$, which makes the system richer than in the undeformed case. This emergent topological structure demonstrates that deformed magnets can host new and exciting physics.

### Paper [280]
Title: Same worst case, different risk: how transient response shapes failure probability
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.00789
Authors: Surachate Limkumnerd
Abstract: The smallest disturbance that can push a dynamical system into failure is a standard measure of stability, but it does not determine how likely failure is under randomly directed disturbances of a given size. The geometry of the transient response connects the two. In the linearized dynamics, each failure boundary and each time after the disturbance define a response vector along the most damaging disturbance. The convex hull of these vectors, the response hull, determines the linear failure-probability curve through its support function. The longest vector sets the worst-case threshold. The mean and spread of the support function set where the curve rises and how steeply, but the mean does not rank systems by risk in general. Under mild regularity conditions, an exact linear-response relation shows how a parameter change shifts the disturbance size at which a given fraction fails: through the mean susceptibility of the directions on that threshold. For weakly damped oscillators, the susceptibility to damping is approximately proportional to the time of a direction's dominant response. Damping thus acts mainly on late failure routes rather than on the early one that sets the worst case, changing the failure probability substantially while barely moving the worst-case threshold. At fixed power imbalance, lowering the inertia instead nearly rescales the hull, moving worst case and risk together. In benchmark power-grid models, the linear predictions agree closely with nonlinear simulations. Strong nonlinear coupling, invisible to linear response, can break this picture; we show how it enters at second order.

### Paper [281]
Title: Breakdown of Fluctuational Electrodynamics in the Extreme Near Field
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05845
Authors: Philippe Ben-Abdallah
Abstract: Fluctuational electrodynamics relies on the assumption that thermal fluctuations in distinct bodies are statistically independent. It is shown that this approximation breaks down in the extreme near-field regime, where hybridization of surface phonon-polaritons across nanometric vacuum gaps generates finite fluctuating-current cross correlations between opposite interfaces. Using a microscopic coupled-oscillator model combined with a Green-tensor formulation of the Poynting vector, the resulting correlation-induced correction to radiative heat transfer is derived. For polar materials, these correlations become significant when the hybridization energy approaches the intrinsic damping rate and can substantially modify conventional fluctuational-electrodynamics predictions at subnanometric separations.

### Paper [282]
Title: Controllable Quantum Spin Hall Phases in Bi$_2$Te$_3$-Family van der Waals Heterobilayers
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.20541
Authors: Emmanuel V. C. Lopes, Pedro H. Sophia, Felipe Crasto de Lima, Adalberto Fazzio
Abstract: The tunability and control of topological edge/surface states are crucial for the development of new device applications. In this work, by combining first-principles calculations and Wannier-based tight-binding methods, we show the emergence of quantum spin Hall phases in van der Waals heterostructures formed by stacking two trivial quintuple layers from the Bi$_2$Te$_3$ family. We demonstrate the tunability of the edge states under interlayer strain and external electric field effects, suggesting the possibility of switching topological edge states on/off by external control. Additionally, the quantum spin Hall edge channels remain robust against interlayer twist, highlighting their stability against external perturbations. Our results provide a new way to create and manipulate two-dimensional topological phases in systems based on Bi$_2$Te$_3$ family, which can be valuable for practical applications, such as topological field effect transistors and spintronic devices.

### Paper [283]
Title: Helical Domain-Wall-Ring Networks Reshape Superconducting Correlations
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.23599
Authors: Yi-Chun Hung, Arun Bansil
Abstract: Extended domain-wall networks that emerge in moir\'e materials provide a distinct platform for quasi-one-dimensional electronic states. However, the interaction-driven orders in confined networks remain largely unexplored. Here, we discuss superconducting (SC) correlations in interacting helical domain-wall-ring networks in the closed topological domains formed within the moir\'e patterns of an underlying twisted bilayer honeycomb lattice. We first analyze the system within the framework of an infinite-size theory and show that inter-ring SC-pair tunneling is renormalization-group relevant in an appropriate interaction regime, favoring enhanced SC correlations through inter-ring phase locking. To address finite-size effects resulting from the ring-network geometry, we present a self-consistent variational approach. Our analysis shows that even in the regime where the infinite-size theory predicts strongly coupled pair tunneling, the induced phase-locking scale remains strongly suppressed. In contrast, the SC scaling dimension is governed mainly by density-density interactions and ring geometry, decreasing modestly with decreasing twist angle while remaining insensitive to the pair-tunneling strength. This discrepancy demonstrates that ring networks cannot generally be understood as simple finite-size truncations of their infinite-size counterparts but can exhibit qualitatively distinct collective behavior. Our study highlights how the interplay of confinement effects and ring-network geometry can reshape SC correlations.

### Paper [284]
Title: Quantum phase fluctuations quench chiral superconductivity in twisted overdoped cuprate bilayers
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00630
Authors: Yin Shi, Mengxian Zhao, Fei Yang, Miao Liu, Sheng Meng
Abstract: Twisted cuprate bilayers have been proposed to host chiral superconductivity at temperatures comparable to their large pairing scale, yet experimental signatures of broken time-reversal symmetry remain unclear. We show that quantum phase fluctuations strongly suppress chiral order in twisted overdoped cuprate bilayers despite robust intralayer pairing. At a $45^\circ$ twist, we analytically demonstrate that the chiral transition temperature obeys $T_\chi\propto t^4$ when the single-electron interlayer tunneling amplitude $t$ is weak. This collapse originates from competition between the small Josephson energy scale supporting chirality and the temperature-dependent collective zero-point energy. Chirality consequently survives only in a narrow angular window at ultralow temperatures, with relative-phase plasma softening above the chiral transition. A distinct transport signature arising from the quantum phase fluctuations is a pronounced low-temperature enhancement of the critical current near a magic angle. These findings separate pairing and chiral ordering scales and offer a possible explanation for the difficulty of observing robust chiral superconductivity in twisted cuprates.

### Paper [285]
Title: Modification of Damon-Eshbach magnetostatic mode spectra in ferromagnet/paramagnet bilayer
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00964
Authors: M. A. Kuznetsov
Abstract: Using the magnetostatic approximation, we calculate the spectra of bulk and surface spin waves in an in-plane magnetized ferromagnet/paramagnet bilayer. Due to the dipolar coupling between the layers, the paramagnet becomes polarized, which in turn modifies the spectrum of the Damon-Eshbach magnetostatic modes. We assume that the paramagnet is characterized by a magnetic susceptibility, $\chi \propto 1/(T-T_C)$, which reaches large values when the system temperature $T$ is close to the Curie temperature $T_C$. We find the conditions under which surface spin waves become unidirectional, i.e., capable of carrying energy in only one direction, and determine the magnitude of their frequency nonreciprocity. We demonstrate the possibility of switching the unidirectional wave regime on and off by varying the external magnetic field or temperature, making the ferromagnet/paramagnet system an attractive platform for tunable magnonic logic devices.

### Paper [286]
Title: Unconventional Mixed-Parity Magnetism in Rare-Earth Tetraborides
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.02117
Authors: Dong-Choon Ryu, Jae-Ho Han, Bongjae Kim, Chang-Jong Kang
Abstract: Altermagnetism has advanced the study of compensated magnets by revealing non-relativistic spin splitting, traditionally classified into strictly even- or odd-parity spin textures. Here, we establish component-resolved mixed-parity spin splitting in a fully three-dimensional compensated magnet and identify its microscopic origin. Using first-principles calculations, tight-binding and $\mathbf{k} \cdot \mathbf{p}$ models, along with spin-group symmetry analysis, we demonstrate that the non-coplanar ground state of $\mathrm{TbB}_4$ enforces odd-parity $p$- and $f$-wave-like textures for the in-plane spin components while the out-of-plane component retains an even-parity $d$-wave altermagnetic character. Crucially, the $p$-wave texture is driven not by relativistic spin-orbit coupling but by a staggered emergent flux arising from the scalar spin chirality of the non-coplanar order. This mixed-parity structure dictates distinct transport fingerprints, including bulk non-relativistic Edelstein and spin Hall responses that respond characteristically to reversal of the chirality domains. Spin-orbit coupling additionally enables a Berry curvature dipole. These results establish the rare-earth tetraborides as a robust platform for engineering complex spin-charge conversion phenomena.

### Paper [287]
Title: Graph-Based Kirchhoff Modeling of Non-Ohmic Electron Transport in Self-Assembled Nanonecklace Networks
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.03698
Authors: Obed Issakah, Md Khirul Alam Titu, Srivathsan Badrinarayanan, Ravi F. Saraf, Janghoon Ock
Abstract: Gold nanonecklace networks exhibit non-Ohmic current--voltage I-V characteristics with a sharp conduction threshold, making them promising for sensing and transistor-like devices. However, their conduction has been understood largely empirically, because experiments cannot see how conduction evolves inside the network. To address this, we develop a graph-based Kirchhoff modeling framework that resolves these internal processes, tracking the potential and current of every junction. Although every active element conducts linearly, the model reproduces the non-Ohmic response $I \propto (V-V_T)^\zeta$. It also captures the experimentally observed effects of junction properties, network density, and voids, which lends confidence to its predicted internal states. These states show that the non-Ohmic response emerges from voltage-driven percolation. Conduction is governed by how junctions are connected rather than by how many are active, with the current scaling approximately as \(N^{2.3}\) while the number of active junctions grows only linearly. Contrary to the expectation that junction heterogeneity strengthens the nonlinear response, homogeneous junctions in a well-connected network produce the most abrupt and reproducible turn-on. Thus, each structural parameter acts as a distinct control knob. The mean junction characteristics set the threshold, junction uniformity sets the switching sharpness, and network density and voids set the connectivity and current. These relationships provide design rules for tailoring nanonecklace networks to a desired conduction behavior through synthesis and fabrication.

### Paper [288]
Title: Compatibility of Martensitic Microstructures in Polycrystals
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12572
Authors: John M. Ball, Myrto Galanopoulou
Abstract: The paper studies martensitic microstructures in polycrystals, focussing on their compatibility across grain boundaries. After a reduction to the case of a planar grain boundary, the case when the grain boundary separates two constant gradients of zero energy is considered. It is shown that for cubic-to tetragonal transformations such a configuration can occur when the relative grain rotation is not in the cubic group. Then the case when the grain boundary separates two simple laminates of zero energy is considered, it being shown using a computer-assisted symbolic calculation that in the cubic-to-tetragonal case compatibility is only possible for a closed set of measure zero in the manifold of grain boundary normals and relative grain rotations, and that a similar slightly weaker result holds for cubic-to-orthorhombic transformations. The results suggest why higher-order laminates are often observed for such transformations.
  The Taylor set of deformation gradients is defined and studied, this set having the property that any deformation whose gradient belongs to it corresponds to a zero-energy microstructure for the polycrystal independent of grain geometry and grain rotations. New upper bounds for the Taylor set are proved for cubic-to-tetragonal and cubic-to-orthorhombic transformations, generalizing those of Bhattacharya and Kohn using the geometrically linearized theory. We give a simple proof of a related result of Peigney characterizing the positive diagonal matrices in the quasiconvex hull of the energy wells for cubic-to-tetragonal transformations.

### Paper [289]
Title: Dipolar mixtures in checker-board optical bilayers
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13980
Authors: Rukmani Bai, Luis Santos
Abstract: Ultra-cold dipolar mixtures in component-dependent optical potentials constitute an interesting platform for the study of the interplay between intra- and inter-component anisotropic long-range interactions. We study the particular case of binary dipolar mixtures placed in separated bilayers which are displaced in an anti-magic wavelength configuration. Using a combination of second-order perturbation theory and cluster-Gutzwiller calculations, we unveil a rich landscape of possible crystalline phases for the two components, showing that, interestingly, inter-site hopping may result, via super-exchange, in solid-into-solid transitions between different crystalline phases. These crystalline phases and the corresponding transitions can be experimentally realized using e.g. lanthanide mixtures in optical lattices.

### Paper [290]
Title: Spinless Charged Excitation at the Interface between a Conventional Topological Insulator and a Topological Mott Insulator
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14221
Authors: Cesar A. Gallegos, Andrew J. Millis, Steven R. White
Abstract: We investigate the interface separating two topologically distinct insulating phases of matter using extensive density-matrix renormalization group calculations to study the triangular-lattice Hofstadter-Hubbard model with a spatially varying interaction strength, chosen to realize both integer quantum Hall and chiral spin liquid states in different spatial regions. We find that the integer quantum Hall-chiral spin liquid interface hosts a spinless charged excitation that is bound to the interface. This mode at the interface is identified through charge and spin pumping, and by direct calculations of low-lying excited states. We also characterize bulk excitations in both phases, finding evidence for fractionalization in the chiral spin liquid and for spin-triplet exciton formation in the integer quantum Hall phase.

### Paper [291]
Title: Particle-scale structure of granular suspensions
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.18090
Authors: Santos Bravo Yuste, Antonio M. Puertas
Abstract: Granular suspensions are intrinsically nonequilibrium systems in which dissipative grain-grain collisions coexist with solvent-induced forcing. We study the particle-scale structure of a granular suspension modeled by smooth inelastic hard spheres immersed in a thermal bath and compare Langevin-dynamics simulation results for the radial distribution function $g(r)$ and the static structure factor $S(q)$ with predictions of an equilibrium-inspired rational function approximation (RFA). The equilibrium hard-sphere RFA is supplied with nonequilibrium input for the contact value and a reduced isothermal-compressibility-like quantity, yielding analytical expressions for $g(r)$ in Laplace space and for $S(q)$. We find that the RFA gives a very good description of the short- and intermediate-range structure of the suspension over a broad range of densities, drag coefficients, and inelasticities. It reproduces $g(r)$ substantially better than the Percus-Yevick approximation in inelastic states, especially near contact, and gives a good account of $S(q)$ except at the smallest wave numbers. There, simulations show a drag-dependent enhancement over the RFA prediction, indicating additional long-wavelength nonequilibrium correlations beyond the present equilibrium-like description. These results show that an equilibrium-based hard-sphere approach provides an accurate description of the particle-scale structure of the present Langevin model with inelastic hard spheres (except in the smallest-$q$ region), and suggest that similar equilibrium-inspired approaches may also be useful for related nonequilibrium hard-sphere suspension models, including multicomponent systems.

### Paper [292]
Title: Differentiable Inverse Design of Two-Body Interactions for the Moore-Read State
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.23695
Authors: Yi Yang, Xin Wan, Zi-Xiang Hu
Abstract: Designing interactions that stabilize target quantum states is a central challenge in strongly correlated physics. For fractional quantum Hall states, identifying suitable two-body interactions remains difficult even when a target wave function is known. Here we implement a differentiable inverse-design approach that combines automatic differentiation with exact diagonalization to optimize two-body interactions, using the Moore--Read Pfaffian state as a benchmark. The optimized ground states achieve overlaps exceeding 99\% with the target for systems containing up to 12 electrons. Excitation and entanglement spectra provide complementary finite-size diagnostics. A common exponential pseudopotential profile retains an overlap of 91.1\% at 14 electrons without further optimization, supporting its transferability beyond the optimization sizes. These results demonstrate the approach on a non-Abelian quantum Hall benchmark and motivate its application to other numerically accessible trial states for which suitable approximate parent Hamiltonians remain unknown.

### Paper [293]
Title: Entanglement asymmetry in the gapped XYZ spin-$\frac12$ chain
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25625
Authors: Felipe Taha Sant'Ana
Abstract: The entanglement asymmetry measures how strongly a symmetry is broken inside a subsystem. Analytic results at equilibrium have so far covered free theories and, perturbatively, the critical XXZ chain. We compute the R\'enyi entanglement asymmetries of a large interval in the gapped, $U(1)$-breaking phase of the interacting XYZ chain. The calculation combines three ingredients. A charged-moment identity, which we prove for fermionic Gaussian and for injective matrix-product ground states, ties the asymmetry to the static susceptibility of the broken charge. A non-conservation sum rule then evaluates the susceptibility from sine-Gordon form factors, its two-kink and one-breather channels providing a lower bound on the universal amplitude. The Baxter--Johnson--Krinsky--McCoy solution supplies the kink mass for different couplings. Infinite-system density-matrix renormalization group simulations built on these masses reproduce the master formula.

### Paper [294]
Title: Theory of phonon-magnon hybridization and angular momentum in CrI$_3$ and CrBr$_3$
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.26986
Authors: Maxime Mignolet, Miquel Royo, Massimiliano Stengel, Matthieu J. Verstraete
Abstract: In magnetic materials angular momentum can be mediated by different carriers, including electrons, magnons, and phonons. The magnons can interact with circularly polarized phonons which are close in energy, provided specific symmetry conditions are met. If the interaction is strong enough the phonons and magnons shift in frequency and start to mix to form hybrid magneto-elastic quasi-particles. In this paper, we develop a constrained Hamiltonian framework which incorporates hybrid stiffness matrices and Berry curvatures. We quantify the degree of hybridization between phonons and magnons (up to 8% in CrI$_3$ and 25% in CrBr$_3$) using a decomposition of the total energy, which is a generalization of the norm decomposition for atomic contributions to phonons used in the literature. We also explore how the total angular momentum is conserved but shared between the phononic and magnonic subsystems upon hybridization.

### Paper [295]
Title: Fluctuation electrodynamics of quantum capacitance in electron bilayers
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.27406
Authors: Dmitry Zverevich, Alberto F. Morpurgo, Alex Levchenko
Abstract: Capacitance provides a thermodynamic probe of interacting electrons. It characterizes the energy cost of transferring charge between conductors, which, in low-dimensional systems, is governed by exchange and correlation effects in addition to electrostatic contributions. We develop a theory of the quantum capacitance of electron double layers, semiconductor quantum wells as well as monolayer and bilayer graphene devices, focusing on the contribution generated by interlayer correlations. Within a functional-integral formulation we show that the separation-dependent part of the ground-state energy is, at the level of ring diagrams, exactly the nonretarded Lifshitz expression for the van der Waals energy of two conducting sheets, with reflection amplitudes built from the layer polarizabilities. The interlayer correction to the inverse capacitance is the second density derivative of this energy: a Casimir compressibility. The zero-point fluctuations that generate Casimir forces between mirrors are here the coupled plasmons of the bilayer, and their contribution to the capacitance is obtained in closed form; its sign shows that interlayer correlations oppose charging at high density. A Gell-Mann-Brueckner analysis gives the high-density limit in terms of universal functions. For graphene we find that monolayers behave like the electron gas, while bilayer graphene is anomalous: interband screening suppresses the correction by orders of magnitude and, within the two-band model, can even reverse its sign in the experimentally relevant range of separations. We delineate the limits of the theory, identifying the dilute Wigner-crystal regime and electron-hole double layers near exciton condensation as regimes where the capacitance becomes a probe of interlayer pairing.

### Paper [296]
Title: Bulk-Boundary Correspondence in Semi-Infinite Chains from Sublattice Zeros
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.01368
Authors: Ilya Iakoub, Nicolas Levasseur, Kylian Lionnet, Richard MacKenzie
Abstract: We provide an alternative derivation of the bulk-boundary correspondence for semi-infinite chains. To describe edge states, we analytically continue the usual Bloch Hamiltonian to complex wave vectors k. To start, we note that the zeros of a Bloch wavefunction are related to edge states, at least in systems with nearest-neighbour hoppings. We show that an analytically continued Bloch Hamiltonian with chiral symmetry has exceptional points, where two different states coalesce. These special points are related to the adiabatic protection of edge states. We derive a winding number that counts the exceptional states which, together with the boundary conditions, give a lower bound on the number of edge states protected by chiral symmetry. This bound is saturated in the models we consider.

### Paper [297]
Title: Transport coefficients in hard-sphere fluids: thermodynamic versus kinetic descriptions
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.06143
Authors: Miguel Hoyuelos
Abstract: A thermodynamic theory for transport coefficients in hard-sphere fluids is developed from a general expression for the Onsager matrix. The theory predicts the ratio $\sigma/\sigma_{\rm id}$, where $\sigma$ is a transport coefficient and $\sigma_{\rm id}$ denotes its dilute-gas value. This ratio depends exclusively on equilibrium thermodynamic properties and can therefore be computed directly from the equation of state. Compact expressions are obtained for the thermal conductivity, viscosity, and self-diffusion coefficient. These expressions agree closely with simulation data at low and intermediate densities and remain useful across the fluid range, although deviations increase near freezing, particularly for viscosity. The self-diffusion prediction substantially improves upon Enskog theory. These results support a thermodynamic description of transport coefficients relative to their dilute-gas values.

### Paper [298]
Title: Thermal Hall tomography of superconducting topology in rhombohedral graphene
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12586
Authors: Kumar Ghosh
Abstract: Chiral superconductors should carry Majorana modes along their edges, yet three decades of work on candidate materials have not measured the integer that counts them. Rhombohedral graphene offers a new route, because its superconductivity coexists with magnetic domains that can be imaged and rewritten. Here we show how heat transport can read this integer and test it against the domain pattern. Warping and finite pair momentum can close the excitation gap while the band Chern number stays fixed. An exact two-band thermal kernel shows how quantization is then lost and bounds the plateau error when the gap survives. Real-space calculations with disorder show that the integer survives disorder several times larger than the gap, because a mobility gap remains. Bogoliubov pockets instead turn the bulk into a heat conductor, and disorder does not restore quantization. Time-reversed domains should show opposite plateaux, and with ideal contacts a wall between them should carry twice either plateau. In microscopic calculations, a written wall carries this number of Majorana channels even when its core is metallic. Writing, moving and erasing such walls would test superconducting topology, its link to magnetism, and protected heat flow in one device.

### Paper [299]
Title: Benchmarking the flow of epithelial cell monolayer with self-aligning deformable active membranes
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13720
Authors: Marcos Pasa, Carine P. Beatrici, Fran\c{c}ois Graner, Leonardo G. Brunnet, Emanuel F. Teixeira
Abstract: Collective cell migration emerges from the interplay between motility, deformability and mechanical interactions, yet incorporating these ingredients into computationally efficient tissue models remains challenging. Here, we benchmark self-aligning deformable active membranes in a confined-flow geometry that mimics epithelial monolayer migration around a circular obstacle. In this model, cells are represented as deformable, adhesive membranes whose self-propulsion direction relaxes towards their velocity. By systematically varying the self-alignment timescale, cell-cell adhesion and inlet forcing, we characterize the resulting flows through collective alignment, relative density, neighbor rearrangements and spatial velocity fields. The model captures a broad spectrum of tissue behaviors, ranging from disordered, liquid-like flows to highly aligned, solid-like states. Compared with a related multiparticle model, self-aligning active membranes achieve stronger collective alignment, exhibit a more systematic density response and access states closer to both limits of the solid-liquid spectrum. We further show that increasing the target shape index promotes cell elongation and accelerates tissue flow, directly linking cell-scale deformability to tissue-scale transport. Finally, we compare simulated velocity profiles with experimental measurements from in vitro migrating MDCK epithelial cell monolayers and find qualitative agreement across multiple horizontal and vertical transects around the obstacle. These results establish self-aligning deformable active membranes as a versatile framework for connecting cell mechanics, shape adaptation and self-alignment to collective tissue migration in confined geometries.

### Paper [300]
Title: Automated Analysis to Reveal Grain Boundary Phase Microstructures
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21540
Authors: R. Daniel Moore, Ian S. Winter, Robert E. Rudd, Fadi Abdeljawad, Timofey Frolov
Abstract: We develop a method for analyzing grain boundary (GB) microstructures that identifies distinct interfacial phases and the dislocation line defects separating them. Similar to bulk materials, GBs can adopt multiple distinct interfacial phases and undergo first-order phase transitions. When these phases coexist, their spatial arrangement and phase junctions constitute a GB microstructure, characterized by variations in excess properties and line defects with associated dislocation content. Despite this intrinsic heterogeneity, our ability to quantitatively characterize GB microstructures remains limited, as it requires identification of individual GB phases, phase-resolved excess properties, and the Burgers content of phase junctions, capabilities not available in existing automated methods. Here, we present an automated tool that performs interfacial microstructure mapping for planar coincidence site lattice GBs. The method identifies the spatial distribution of GB phases, quantifies phase-specific excess properties, and estimates the Burgers content of GB phase junctions. We demonstrate the approach using three representative cases: (i) quantification of mass transport during diffusion-limited GB phase transformations; (ii) identification of structurally indistinguishable phases formed by vacancy and interstitial loops; and (iii) characterization of GB microstructures containing phase nuclei. More broadly, this framework enables quantitative studies of GB evolution processes, including spinodal decomposition and coarsening with direct implications for GB deformation, creep, and migration.

### Paper [301]
Title: Quantifying Ultrafast Orbital Pumping and Nanometre-Scale Orbital Diffusion in Magnetic Heterostructures
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.29165
Authors: Tongyang Guan, Jiahao Liu, Yuxiao Mo, Liangliang Zhu, Yizheng Wu, Zhensheng Tao
Abstract: Quantifying ultrafast orbital pumping and transport remains challenging because spin and orbital currents contribute coherently to terahertz emission. Here, we combine spintronic terahertz emission spectroscopy with wedge-sample thickness control to disentangle these contributions in Fe, Co and Ni heterostructures with Ta, W and Nb. Their opposite spin Hall and orbital Hall polarities enable quantitative determination of relative pumping strengths and propagation lengths. We find that orbital pumping increases from Fe to Ni, with inferred orbital-to-spin current ratios reaching several tens of percent in Ni, exceeding current microscopic predictions. Remarkably, Nb exhibits an orbital diffusion length of ~1.5 nm, substantially longer than in Ta and W and beyond the sub-nanometre scale predicted by recent orbital-relaxation calculations. These findings provide quantitative experimental benchmarks for ultrafast orbital pumping and transport.

### Paper [302]
Title: Irreversibility of many-body harmonic oscillators characterized by generalized Husimi's adiabaticity parameter
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07039
Authors: Kyosuke Watanabe, Yuki Izumida
Abstract: In a classical paper [K. Husimi, Prog. Theor. Phys. 9, 381 (1953)], Husimi showed that, for a single harmonic oscillator with a time-dependent angular frequency and initial conditions sampled from an equilibrium distribution, the averaged energy cannot decrease after a cyclic operation. This irreversibility is quantified by Husimi's adiabaticity parameter constituted with adiabatic invariants. In this work, we generalize Husimi's framework to a many-body system of one-dimensional harmonic oscillators coupled on an arbitrary connected network, with all spring constants sharing a common time dependence. For a system attached to a fixed wall, the dynamics can be decomposed into independent normal modes by transforming to mass-weighted coordinates and diagonalizing the resulting positive definite matrix, with each mode characterized by its own Husimi's adiabaticity parameter. By defining the generalized Husimi's adiabaticity parameter as their arithmetic mean, we derive the exact time evolution of the averaged energy and establish its non-decrease under cyclic operations. Numerical simulations confirm these results for both a uniform nearest-neighbor chain and a heterogeneous network.

### Paper [303]
Title: Quantifying translational and bond-orientational order metrics in hyperuniform and nonhyperuniform many-particle systems
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10359
Authors: Anirban Mukherjee, Salvatore Torquato
Abstract: Quantifying the degree of order/disorder in many-particle systems remains an outstanding problem in physics, materials science, and mathematics. To this end, we consider the translational order metric $\tau_T$, defined as the squared $L^2$ norm of the total correlation function $h(\mathbf{r})$, and introduce its bond-orientational analogue $\tau_O$, defined from the weighted total correlation function $h_{\mathbf{f}}(\mathbf{r})$ using local orientational weights (S. Torquato et al., Phys. Rev. X 16, 011042 (2026)). The pair $(\tau_T,\tau_O)$ places both forms of order on a common two-point statistical footing. We compute both metrics for two nonhyperuniform sphere-packing models in 2D and 3D as functions of packing fraction $\phi$: 1) equilibrium hard particles and 2) nonequilibrium random sequential addition (RSA) packings. For the nonhyperuniform systems, bond-orientational order remains subdominant along the equilibrium-fluid and RSA configurations; however, its magnitude relative to the translational metric increases near the upper end of the equilibrium-fluid branches, much more strongly in 2D than in 3D, and the two metrics become comparable along the sampled crystal branches. At common packing fractions, equilibrium fluids and RSA packings trace distinct $(\tau_T,\tau_O)$ trajectories, revealing preparation-dependent differences in structural order. As a representative hyperuniform family, we study 2D disordered stealthy hyperuniform (SHU) ground states for $0<\chi<1/2$, where $\chi$ is the stealthiness parameter. Within the disordered SHU phase, bond-orientational order remains subdominant, but its magnitude relative to the translational metric increases toward the disorder-to-order threshold. In all three models, $\tau_T$ and $\tau_O$ are positively correlated beyond the Poisson-reference regime: $\tau_O$ increases monotonically with $\tau_T$ across the sampled state points.

### Paper [304]
Title: One-Dimensional Type-IV Collinear Magnetism
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11547
Authors: Xin Chen, Duo Wang, Peng Zhang, Zeying Zhang
Abstract: Two-dimensional type-IV collinear magnets combine nonrelativistic spin degeneracy with spin-orbit-coupling-enabled magnetic responses. Here we establish a one-dimensional counterpart through altermagnetic confinement. A Lieb-based construction connects three-dimensional altermagnets to two-dimensional type-IV magnets and extends the same principle to one-dimensional ribbons. In a symmetric $[110]$ ribbon, a sublattice-exchange operation preserves the longitudinal momentum and enforces nonrelativistic spin degeneracy. {Spin-orbit coupling (SOC) ties spin to the lattice, so the N\'eel orientation determines whether the remaining symmetries protect the doublets or allow them to split.} First-principles-derived Wannier calculations for Ta$_2$TeSeO ribbons reveal spin-polarized propagating channels with an ideal right-moving-mode polarization of approximately $35\%$ at finite conductance for the parent easy-axis orientation $\mathbf N\parallel[100]$. Direct DFT calculations of Ta$_2$Te$_2$O ribbons resolve the contrast between spin-split $[100]$ and spin-degenerate $[001]$ SOC spectra. Assembling the same isolated Ta$_2$Te$_2$O ribbons into a transversely periodic array restores spin splitting without SOC. This conversion parallels the recovery of three-dimensional altermagnetism through suitable periodic stacking of two-dimensional type-IV magnets. One-dimensional type-IV magnetism thus provides a route to spin splitting through the interplay of antiferromagnetic order and SOC, distinct from conventional Rashba splitting driven by structural inversion asymmetry.

### Paper [305]
Title: Sub-kelvin cryogenic wafer probing verified by on-wafer primary Coulomb blockade thermometry
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12596
Authors: Lassi Lehtisyrj\"a, Renan P. Loreto, Juho Luomahaara, Jarno J\"arvinen, Tuure Rantanen, Juha Vikstedt, Janne S. Lehtinen, Matti Remes, Timo Salminen, Juuso Helander, Aki Junes, Pasi Aaltonen, Vesa Henttonen, Mika Prunnila
Abstract: Scalable cryogenic wafer level testing is emerging as an essential tool for the development of quantum devices and cryogenic microelectronics, but the main motivation, the actual operational on-wafer temperature, remains largely unverified. In this work, utilizing a cryogenic wafer prober (CWP) enables wafer-scale electrical testing of Coulomb blockade thermometers (CBTs), while the CBTs simultaneously provide an independent static and dynamic assessment of the prober's thermal performance through primary and in situ calibrated secondary electron thermometry, realizing a reciprocal characterization approach. Using recently introduced scalable normal metal TiW-based CBTs, which require no superconductivity suppressing magnetic fields, we have measured on-wafer electron temperatures of 630-660 mK across a full 150 mm wafer, representing, to our knowledge, record-low electron temperatures measured in a CWP. These exceed the nominal 600 mK chuck setpoint by approximately 40 mK on average, demonstrating the necessity of direct on-wafer thermometry. Self-calibrated secondary CBT thermometry resolves the thermal transients caused by chuck movement and reveals a thermal settling time within 5 minutes after probe touch down. This reciprocal wafer-scale approach simultaneously evaluates the device under test and the thermal performance of the measurement environment, providing a practical methodology for scalable sub-kelvin characterization of quantum devices and cryogenic microelectronics.

### Paper [306]
Title: Spin splitting without symmetry: a nearly compensated ferrimagnet and the origin of altermagnetism
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12931
Authors: Jaekyung Jang, Joo Yull Rhee
Abstract: The spin splitting of collinear altermagnets is usually attributed to the crystal symmetry relating the opposite-spin sublattices --- a rotation or mirror in place of the translation or inversion of a conventional antiferromagnet. We show that this symmetry organizes the splitting but does not produce it; its origin is the anisotropic arrangement of the magnetic orbitals and their ligands, which also fixes the symmetry itself. Two results establish this. A triclinic ($P1$) \ce{Mn} oxide, a fully compensated ferrimagnet whose two inequivalent sublattices are related by no symmetry, is spin split throughout the Brillouin zone; repositioning the same ligands, atom for atom, to restore an inversion centre relating the two metal sites collapses the splitting to a residual two orders of magnitude smaller, so the splitting follows from the arrangement rather than the symmetry. Symmetry, we show formally, can only ever reverse or forbid a splitting that already exists: it cannot create one between sublattices an arrangement has left electronically identical, nor where the underlying orbitals carry no anisotropy to arrange in the first place. Organizing the analysis through an operation-resolved symmetry hierarchy and reading the crystal classes as a single axis, the enforced nodal planes fall from two to one to zero across the orthorhombic, monoclinic, and triclinic classes while the arrangement-generated splitting persists to the base. The altermagnet and the fully compensated ferrimagnet are thus the two ends of one axis --- the same splitting, organized by symmetry at one end and unorganized at the other --- and the search for compensated spin-split magnets is a search over orbital arrangements rather than symmetry labels.

### Paper [307]
Title: Geometry-Driven Suppression of Fermionic Pairing on a Spherical Surface
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18386
Authors: Lorenzo Frigato, Andrea Tononi, Luca Salasnich
Abstract: We investigate the ground-state properties of a two-component attractive Fermi gas confined to a spherical surface. In the strongly attractive regime, the system forms a Bose-Einstein condensate (BEC) of tightly bound dimers, yielding a gap and chemical potential that approach flat-space results with leading-order geometric corrections. As the attraction decreases, the system crosses over into a Bardeen-Cooper-Schrieffer (BCS) regime, the sphere radius $R$ constrains the Cooper pair size and shell effects emerge. For closed shells, corresponding to magic particle numbers set by the single-particle spectrum, pairing is suppressed above the scattering length $a^*=4R$, whose value is universal in the spherical geometry in contrast with box-trapped fermionic gases. Our results highlight a fundamental interplay among geometry, quantum statistics, and interactions, motivating future experiments with shell-trapped ultracold fermions and inspiring geometry-controlled condensed matter devices.

### Paper [308]
Title: Lyapunov-controlled thermalization: an exact real-time example
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20597
Authors: Jonas Loy, Jan C. Louw
Abstract: A verified Kubo-Martin-Schwinger (KMS) relation, after a drive has ceased, is not sufficient evidence to prove equilibrium. We demonstrate this in a large-$N$ large-$q$ Sachdev-Ye-Kitaev (SYK) quench protocol. Although all post-quench fermion correlators are exactly thermal, a second quench back to the initial Hamiltonian reveals hidden memory of the initial state. It is encoded in correlators connecting to times before the first quench. This memory is an extensive nonequilibrium (NEQ) witness with its decay rate being the Lyapunov exponent $\lambda_L$. Thus $\lambda_L$ sets the rate at which the state becomes effectively indistinguishable from a Gibbs state. Despite being a unitary interacting many-body system, the complete NEQ real-time evolution is obtained exactly in the large-$N$, large-$q$ limit, making the setup ideal for analytically studying thermalization.

### Paper [309]
Title: A Cross-Entropy Audit (CEA) for Consciousness Tests Moved from Brain to Large Language Model
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.22300
Authors: Pieter van Rooyen
Abstract: Brain tests of consciousness are often applied to large language models (LLMs). Here consciousness means access: whether information, such as a heard sound, becomes available for report and use. A brain recording is in microvolts and an LLM's internal activations are arbitrary numbers, so the two share no common measurement unit. A brain test of this kind compares competing statistical models of how responses vary over repeated trials. The test's decision names the best-fitting model. The decision is wrong when the test names two states for responses that vary smoothly, or the reverse. No method we reviewed measures how often a transferred test is wrong. We call such a method a transfer audit and propose one, the cross-entropy audit (CEA). First, our audit holds back part of the trials and fits each model to the remaining trials. It then scores how well each fitted model predicts the held-back responses. Comparing two models gives a score difference in nats per trial. That difference does not depend on whether the recording is in microvolts or in activations, so brain and machine are scored on one scale. Second, the audit writes down five choices that such tests make for brain data: the competing models, their fitting, what counts as one independent sample, what the uncertainty interval describes, and the decision rule. Each may need to change on a machine. Third, the audit runs the test on simulated data built to follow one model, so the right decision is known, and counts wrong decisions. In our example, the two models of a two-state test, carried over unchanged to an LLM, found two states in 989 of 12,000 smoothly varying simulated datasets. The audited version of the test found two states in none of them. It detected 599 of 600 two-state datasets. The audited version's uncertainty interval, however, was too narrow at six of the twelve smoothly varying settings and is being repaired.

### Paper [310]
Title: Quantum Simulation of Detuning-Controlled Proximity-Induced Localization and Reentrant Delocalization in a Quasiperiodic Ladder
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.37059
Authors: Mrinal Kanti Giri, Shih Tai-Ming, Pinaki Sengupta, Pochung Chen
Abstract: Proximity effects, in which one subsystem imprints its properties onto another, offer a route to controlling quantum matter. For localization, this raises the question of whether an extended system can be localized by its neighbor, and then delocalized again, in the regime of weak quasiperiodic disorder. Here, we demonstrate this behavior in a coupled Aubry--Andr\'e ladder, where a staggered detuning applied to only one leg localizes the neighboring, undetuned leg through inter-leg hybridization, even before the detuned leg itself localizes. Upon further increasing the detuning, the undetuned leg undergoes re-entrant delocalization, while the detuned leg remains localized. Finite-size scaling supports the persistence of these regimes in the thermodynamic limit. On an IBM Quantum processor, we experimentally observe their dynamical signatures: strongly suppressed wave-packet spreading in the proximity-localized regime and its recovery upon re-entrant delocalization. Our results establish leg-selective detuning as a reversible control knob for localization and transport in coupled quantum systems.

### Paper [311]
Title: Hierarchical Fourier Phase Projection for Local Electronic Observables
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.37814
Authors: Tao Hu, Weiqing Zhou, Zhichang Fu, Yechen Chen, Shengjun Yuan
