Abstract: We develop hierarchical Fourier phase projection (HPP) to evaluate electronic observables without explicitly constructing occupied eigenstates. Nested Fourier probes progressively cancel nearby cross terms, while exponential or algebraic density-matrix decay suppresses distant contributions. Refinement reuses all previous matrix-function responses to evaluate diverse electronic observables. Electron density and nonlocal forces illustrate diagonal extraction and off-diagonal contraction, respectively. A locality-based decay model fitted to changes across completed levels estimates residual errors from the unresolved pairs without additional matrix-function applications. Tests using fixed Kohn-Sham Hamiltonians for semiconductors, metals, and thermally perturbed silica cover different decay ranges and periodic cell geometries. About 90% of the assessed density and nonlocal force predictions lie within a factor of two of the reference errors. The probing requirements for a prescribed local accuracy show weak size dependence. This work thus provides a scalable framework linking electronic locality, observable accuracy, and computational effort in large-scale electronic-structure calculations.

### Paper [312]
Title: Imaging phase winding in topological superconductors with a fork-tip Josephson STM
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38174
Authors: Vladislav Poliakov, Archisman Panigrahi
Abstract: The topological phase winding in a chiral superconductor remains challenging to access by conventional real-space probes. We propose a scheme to probe this winding with a fork-tip Josephson STM near an impurity, where two superconducting tips form an interferometer sensitive to phase differences between two positions. The phase winding remains accessible via the fork-tip critical current even if the relative phase between the fork-tip and sample fluctuates, establishing a route towards a local phase-sensitive STM probe of topological superconductivity. We numerically compute the interference pattern to demonstrate that the magnitude and phase of the order parameter can be reconstructed as a function of angle, providing a route towards resolving the longstanding open experimental problem of directly detecting phase winding in topological superconductors.

### Paper [313]
Title: Anisotropic wavevector-dependent damping of thickness-quantized magnons
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.39612
Authors: Tamara Azevedo, Rostyslav O. Serha, Yannik Kunz, Matthias R. Schweizer, Vitaliy I. Vasyuchka, Mathias Weiler, Andrii V. Chumak, Burkard Hillebrands, Mikhail Kostylev, Alexander A. Serga
Abstract: Magnon damping is a key factor governing spin-wave transport and nonlinear dynamics of multimode magnon systems. However, many descriptions rely on the assumption of an effective mode-independent parameter, which can mask wavelength-dependent relaxation processes that depend on propagation geometry and mode profile. Here, we employ high-resolution parametric-instability spectroscopy to probe thickness-quantized spin waves with wavelengths down to about a hundred nanometers in micrometer-thick yttrium iron garnet films. The instability threshold exhibits a regular sawtooth dependence on the magnetic field, arising from switching between discrete thickness modes. Comparison with dipole--exchange theory reveals anisotropic wavevector-dependent damping that increases with mode number and depends differently on the in-plane and out-of-plane wavevector components.

### Paper [314]
Title: Time-temperature-composition equivalence of the glass transition in digitally mixed 3D-printed polymers
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14986
Authors: Beijun Shen, Sanat K. Kumar, Mary C. Boyce
Abstract: For more than seventy years, time-temperature equivalence (TTE) has enabled a few measurements to describe the linear viscoelasticity of polymers over many decades of frequency. Multimaterial 3D printing now combines elastomeric and glassy resins voxel by voxel into digitally mixed polymers. Composition shifts the glass transition along the frequency axis, as temperature does, but also broadens it and increases the rubbery plateau modulus. A translation, the only operation of TTE, cannot capture these changes. Here we establish a time-temperature-composition equivalence (TTCE) from dynamic mechanical measurements of five compositions of a printed family. A shift of the transition along the reduced-frequency axis, a stretch of its breadth and a normalization between its plateaus collapse the storage modulus onto one master curve. The collapse holds at every measured temperature, frequency and composition, through a single reduced variable. The position and breadth of the transition change linearly with the volume fraction of the glassy resin. The phase angle and the relaxation spectrum superpose with the same parameters, so the family shares one distribution of relaxation times. The resulting closed form gives the storage and loss moduli continuously in composition, temperature and frequency, and quantitatively predicts two further mixtures withheld from the calibration. The same shift, stretch and normalization also collapse published temperature sweeps of a patterned composite family of different resins, pointing to broader applicability. Composition thus joins temperature and frequency as a third variable of one equivalence, and the closed form can guide the design of graded and architected printed structures.

### Paper [315]
Title: Emergent Spinorial Structure in Mesoscopic Irreversible Thermodynamics
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16738
Authors: Giorgio Sonnino
Abstract: Can transport coefficients determine both the coherent evolution of a mesoscopic system and distances between its thermodynamic states? We establish this connection within a canonical operator framework. Thermodynamic coordinates and conjugate forces obey commutation relations governed by a reduced constant ${\kbar}_B$. Requiring coordinate evolution to reproduce self-adjoint flux observables fixes a compatible generator up to an additive multiplication operator, set to zero in the minimal realization. The resulting equation describes coherent mesoscopic amplitudes, with transport fixing its coefficients and the reduced constant setting the evolution scale. An exact first-order factorization gives a geometry through Connes' spectral-distance prescription, including for coordinate-dependent transport. The metric is twice the inverse of the symmetric transport matrix. Coordinate derivatives of the antisymmetric part can change the dynamics without changing distances. Published thermoelectric data illustrate the geometry. A separate quadratic entropy-production observable has adjacent entropy levels separated by the reduced constant under the stated canonical and finite-mode matching assumptions. This conditional prediction offers a possible experimental signature. The evolution is unitary; irreversible relaxation requires additional mechanisms, such as environmental coupling or coarse-graining.

### Paper [316]
Title: Transport Determines Spectral Distance in Mesoscopic Thermodynamics
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16752
Authors: Giorgio Sonnino
Abstract: We derive a distance between thermodynamic states from a positive Hermitian transport tensor within a mesoscopic canonical model. An exact first-order factorization of the evolution generator gives a commutator norm and the metric $G=2g$, where $g$ denotes the symmetric transport tensor. Gradients of the antisymmetric tensor can change the evolution, but they leave this distance unchanged. The construction allows coordinate-dependent coefficients. For constant Onsager coefficients, a Clifford representation gives the same metric. In a two-channel thermoelectric system, the ratio of normalized principal distances is $\sqrt{1+ZT}+\sqrt{ZT}$. Transport coupling thus fixes the anisotropy of this metric. The construction requires neither an independent metric nor a physical spin variable.

### Paper [317]
Title: Trust-Limited Machine-Learning-Accelerated Nudged Elastic Band Calculations for Small-Polaron Hopping in Rutile TiO2
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16850
Authors: Ashish Kumar Singh, Irina V. Lebedeva
Abstract: Nudged elastic band (NEB) calculations of small-polaron hopping are demanding because nuclear motion is coupled to a self-consistent localized electronic state. Near the charge-transfer region, slow self-consistent-field convergence, localization switching, and competing electronic solutions complicate surrogate-assisted optimization. We develop a Gaussian-process-assisted NEB workflow for this regime and demonstrate electron small-polaron hopping in rutile TiO2 using spin-polarized density functional theory with a Hubbard U correction (DFT+U ) with SIESTA. A joint energy-force Gaussian process (GP) with a Mat\'ern covariance model advances the elastic band, while active-atom root-mean-square and maximum-atom displacement limits constrain surrogate motion relative to evaluated configurations. Reaching these limits triggers first-principles sampling without requiring full surrogate convergence. Three independently DFT-verified 96-atom [001] calculations using 11, 13, and 15 images and different surrogate settings and GP parameterizations yield barriers of 0.1645, 0.1637, and 0.1640 eV, respectively, spanning 0.793 meV. In the 15-image calculation, repeated central-image sampling activates a separately calibrated local GP during surrogate optimization. Final full-band DFT verification gives a maximum projected NEB force of 0.0346 eV/{\AA}. A 324-atom calculation yields a lower barrier of 0.1302 eV, indicating supercell sensitivity and demonstrating feasibility at larger GP observation dimension. The workflow combines controlled surrogate extrapolation, targeted charge-transfer-region interpolation, and independent DFT verification to obtain polaron migration pathways, emphasizing reproducible barriers and full-band force convergence.

### Paper [318]
Title: Making Weak Interlayer Excitons Absorb via Purcell Engineering of Critical Coupling
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20307
Authors: Saroj Poudyal, Anabel Atash Kahlon, Michael Klein, Matan Meshulam, Yonatan Gershuni, Thomas Poirier, James H. Edgar, Mukesh Kumar, Seth Ariel Tongay, Itai Epstein
Abstract: Interlayer excitons (IXs) in transition metal dichalcogenide bilayers possess unique dipolar properties, providing a path for exploring correlated many-body phases, electrically tunable dipole-dipole interactions, and long-range exciton transport. However, the large electron-hole separation underlying their dipolar character also results in a significantly reduced oscillator strength, limiting their direct optical interaction with light, and thus the direct probing of their dynamics and many-body interactions. Here, we show that by using Purcell engineering of critical coupling, a weak interlayer transition can absorb as strongly as the dominant intralayer transition, despite its much smaller intrinsic oscillator strength. Using this approach, in an open cavity architecture we demonstrate an order of magnitude IX absorption modulation in bilayer and trilayer MoS2, with doubling of the IX to A-exciton interaction strength weight from ~23% to ~53%. Furthermore, we demonstrate that in a tailored closed cavity architecture, the IX enhancement can reach beyond an order of magnitude, from ~5% to ~83% excitonic absorption, enabling IX absorption similar to intralayer excitons. An analytical description of the interaction is obtained via a closed-form transmission-line model which separates the excitonic and photonic contributions to the optical response, revealing how cavity architecture can be designed towards critical coupling. Our findings establish Purcell engineering of critical coupling as a general route to controlling and enhancing weak excitonic transitions.

### Paper [319]
Title: Moments of Random Permanents and Hafnians: A Replica Approach
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27872
Authors: Paolo Facchi, Angelo Mariano, Giorgio Parisi, Saverio Pascazio, Antonello Scardicchio
Abstract: Using contour-integral representations of the permanent and of the Hafnian, we turn the computation of their moments for random matrices into a replica calculation, analogous to that of the moments of the partition function of a mean-field spin glass. For the $2k$-th moment of $N\times N$ matrices with independent entries, the dominant saddle point is replica symmetric for $k>2$, and we obtain in closed form the exponential growth rate, the prefactor and the first $1/N$ correction, in agreement with exact enumerations. The growth rate depends only on whether the entries are real or complex; higher cumulants affect only the prefactor and the $1/N$ term. At $k=2$ the replica-symmetric solution becomes unstable, separating a power-law bulk of the distribution from a large-deviation tail, which we compute. The results apply to boson sampling with single photons and with squeezed light.

### Paper [320]
Title: Bounded entropy production constrains hierarchical decision-making in homing pigeon flocks
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31126
Authors: Bibaswan Ghosh, Vipul Rai, Harsh Soni, Nitin Kumar
Abstract: Collective decision-making requires individuals to reconcile diverse, often conflicting opinions to reach a common course of action. However, within a group, individuals rarely exert equal influence, leading to non-reciprocal communication patterns manifested as leader-follower relationships and social hierarchies. Here, using homing pigeon flocks as a model system, we develop a trajectory-based framework to infer and quantify hierarchy within navigating living collectives. We find that while small flocks exhibit pronounced hierarchy, increasing flock size progressively weakens it, driving the collective toward more egalitarian decision-making. We provide a physical rationale for this observation using a thermodynamic framework in which non-reciprocal communication between birds carries an information-entropy cost. Our calculations reveal that hierarchy weakening observed in larger flocks is consistent with maintaining a constant per-individual entropy production rate across flock sizes. Together, these results suggest that constraints on information-processing capacity may play an important role in the collective dynamics of living organisms.

### Paper [321]
Title: Structural characterization of the rutile-GeO2/TiO2 (001) heterointerface
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31934
Authors: Imteaz Rahaman, Martha R. McCartney, David J. Smith, Kai Fu
Abstract: Rutile-GeO2 (r-GeO2) has emerged as a promising ultrawide-bandgap (UWBG) semiconductor for future high-power device applications because of its favorable combination of physical and electrical properties. This study has used high-resolution transmission electron microscopy (HR-TEM) to compare heterointerfaces between thin films of GeO2 grown by metal-organic chemical vapor deposition on closely-lattice-matched rutile TiO2 (r-TiO2) substrates at temperatures of 840{\deg} C and 925{\deg} C. Cross-sectional TEM observations reveal interfaces with two entirely different structures: an amorphous GeOx/r-TiO2 heterostructure with an abrupt, highly faceted and non-planar interface, or a fully epitaxial r-GeO2/r-TiO2 heterostructure characterized by a more wavy but often facetted interface. Structural analysis of the latter sample revealed a high density of strain-relieving interfacial dislocations, identified primarily as Lomer-type or 60{\deg}-type, that accommodated the ~4% lattice mismatch between the two materials, while chemical analysis showed very limited interfacial intermixing. Crystallographic analysis showed that the interface faceting was associated with rutile {101} facets, with characteristic facet angles of ~114{\deg}; these observations support thermally driven TiO2 (001) substrate faceting rather than an interface reconstruction resulting from GeO2 epitaxy. Overall, these findings provide useful insights into the mechanisms governing interface formation and strain relaxation in r-GeO2/r-TiO2 heterostructures and suggest that further refinement of growth conditions should enable improved epitaxial growth of r-GeO2 for future ultrawide-bandgap electronic device applications.

### Paper [322]
Title: A Self-Consistent Two-Carrier Drift-Diffusion Framework for p-n Junctions: Extended Diode Equation, Power Dissipation, and Base Transit Time
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32112
Authors: Jianhong Yang, Tinglan Li, Gonghao Sun
Abstract: The classical theory of p-n junctions derives the current-voltage characteristic from minority-carrier diffusion alone: majority carriers are treated as an inert background, the quasi-neutral base as field-free, and the junction voltage as equal to the applied bias. These premises fail together under high bias and high-level injection, where the observed current roll-off is reproduced only by appending an empirical series resistance outside the junction physics. Here we develop a self-consistent two-carrier drift-diffusion framework that introduces no assumption beyond the classical ones. Excess majority carriers are shown to be electrostatically forced transport participants: the ambipolar diffusion equation, closed by exact two-sided boundary conditions, yields a unified excess-carrier profile. The resulting base field separates into an ohmic and a Dember component, and all four current components follow in closed form, their sum being position independent. At device level the framework closes into an extended diode equation that contains the Shockley equation as its low-injection, long-base limit and reproduces the n=2 roll-off intrinsically. The junction voltage emerges as a self-limiting drop confined to the space-charge region and separated from the applied bias by exact base drops. Two device-level corrections are quantified: at high current, 75-90% of the dissipation migrates from the junction into the quasi-neutral base, and field-assisted acceleration shortens the base transit time by up to a factor of six. Three parameter-free predictions follow: a universal ideality-factor crossover that passes through 3/2 when the boundary excess equals the doping, a high-injection onset voltage that scales as 119 mV per decade of doping, and a geometry factor that survives into high injection.

### Paper [323]
Title: Momentum-selective graphene spectroscopy of moir\'e excitons
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33989
Authors: Katsunori Wakabayashi
Abstract: Far-field photoluminescence probes only the light-cone corner of an exciton's momentum content, whereas energy transfer to nearby graphene integrates in-plane momenta up to the inverse spacer thickness $1/z$. For a WSe$_2$/MoSe$_2$ heterobilayer we build the transition polarization $\bm{P}_n(\qv)$ of each localized moir\'e exciton from the registry-dependent vector dipole texture and use each $\bm{P}_n$ for both baths. The two baths rank the states differently: the graphene-rate ratios differ from the optical ones, their ordering inverts with spacer thickness, and the distance exponents at $z=10$\,nm spread by more than two units. These ratios are independent of the transition-dipole prefactor. Within the $C_3$-symmetric leading-harmonic model the chiral $p_+$ and $d_-$ states are also dark at normal incidence yet transfer to graphene at rates comparable to the bright ground state. Their graphene rate depends on the sign of the texture phase, a quantity invisible in far-field emission, which we determine from first principles. In a device this prediction requires a splitting, larger than the symmetry-breaking mixing, that protects the chiral partners. Graphene proximity thus acts as a momentum-selective near-field probe of moir\'e excitons, and we propose its use as a practical spectroscopy.

### Paper [324]
Title: Symmetry-resolved quasilocal entanglement as a key to Mott and pseudogap physics in plaquette CDMFT
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34470
Authors: Gabriele Bellomia, Carlos Mejuto-Zaera, Massimo Capone, Adriano Amaricci
Abstract: In order to identify intrinsic quantum resources in strongly interacting fermionic systems, we introduce a symmetry resolution of the parity-superselected two-site entanglement, as measured by both the fermionic negativity and the relative entropy of entanglement. We discuss how the symmetry resolution can be extended also to the full entanglement in pure and mixed two-site states. We apply our recipe to the Hubbard model, first for a dimer, and then for a plaquette cluster dynamical mean-field theory, an established method tailored to accurately capture short-range correlations. Following the evolution of the model through the interaction- and density-driven Mott transitions at zero temperature, we highlight how the three symmetry-resolved components of the two-site entanglement probe different key aspects of Mott and pseudogap physics.

### Paper [325]
Title: Simplified Parisi Ansatz III: Constructive Cavity Method and Ultrametricity
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34710
Authors: Simone Franchini
Abstract: We present a physical explaination of the Constructive Cavity Method (CCM) for the Sherrington--Kirkpatrick model, which identifies the decomposition of the cavity variables into layers with the random overlap structure of Aizenman--Sims--Starr. We describe the low-temperature excitations through a dilute gas of defects relative to the ground state. The sharing of these defects among the descendants and the renewal of the fluctuations along new branches produce the GREM structure and the ultrametricity of the overlaps. This mechanism is developed first with a finite number of defects and then extended to exponential populations of states. At fixed kernel, the Gaussian covariances of the fresh fields connect the complete cavity variables, including their common components, to the same hierarchy that determines the Gibbs masses. We also correct some ambiguities of earlier formulations.

### Paper [326]
Title: Learning by Confusion Locates a Parametrization-Dependent Fisher Information Maximum
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34769
Authors: George Issa, Richard Scalettar
Abstract: Learning by confusion (LBC) is a machine-learning method for locating phase transitions without assuming a known order parameter. Its characteristic accuracy curve contains a central maximum that is interpreted as the transition point, but what determines the location and finite-size scaling of this maximum has remained unclear. We derive an exact balance condition for the Bayes-optimal confusion peak and show that, for equilibrium systems with a single swept parameter, the peak closely follows the maximum of the corresponding Fisher information. This immediately gives its finite-size scaling and reveals an unexpected dependence on how the data are sampled: the same configurations arranged on grids uniform in $T$, $\ln T$, or $\beta=1/T$ can yield different finite-size pseudocritical trajectories. Tests on the Ising, Blume-Capel, Potts, and Baxter-Wu models confirm the prediction across continuous, tricritical, and first-order transitions. Our results identify the statistical quantity measured by LBC and show that finite-size machine-learning estimates can depend on the parametrization of the training data as well as on the underlying physics.

### Paper [327]
Title: Temperature-dependent magnetic properties of the antiferromagnet Mn$_2$Au
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35153
Authors: Jackson L. Ross, Sarah Jenkins, Ricardo Rama-Eiroa, Paul-Iulian Gavriloaea, Jack Collings, Unai Atxitia, Oksana Chubykalo-Fesenko, Karin Everschor-Sitte, Rub\'en M. Otxoa, Roy W. Chantrell, Richard F. L. Evans
Abstract: Mn$_2$Au is one of the most promising materials for antiferromagnetic spintronics due to its high N\'eel temperature, moderate anisotropy, layered spin structure allowing current induced spin-orbit-torques, and ultrafast spin dynamics. Further understanding and control of the magnetic order in Mn$_2$Au for ultrafast spintronics applications via ultrashort electric/optical means requires accurate understanding of how its magnetic properties depend on temperature. Here we present atomistic spin dynamics simulations of the temperature-dependent macroscopic magnetic properties of Mn$_2$Au single crystals. We introduce a spin Hamiltonian based on previous ab initio calculations which produces temperature-dependent scaling of the anisotropy constants with magnetisation consistent with Zener-Akulov-Callen-Callen power laws. The calculated temperature dependence of exchange stiffness follows a scaling law with magnetisation of $m^{1.83}_e$ , and predicts a temperature-dependent domain wall width that agrees very well with direct simulation. Our model provides an understanding of temperature-dependent magnetic properties of Mn$_2$Au that enables large-scale simulation of the working conditions of spintronic devices.

### Paper [328]
Title: Large superconducting diode effect indicates nonuniform triplet pairing in UTe2
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36332
Authors: Zheyu Wu, Daniel Shaffer, Dmitry V. Chichinadze, Mengmeng Long, Hanyi Chen, Andrej Cabala, Theodore I. Weinberger, Maurice Bal, Steffen Wiedmann, Dave Graf, Vladimir Sechovsk\'y, Michal Vali\v{s}ka, F. Malte Grosche, Alex Levchenko, Alexander G. Eaton
Abstract: The superconducting diode effect (SDE) is characterized by unequal critical current magnitudes for opposite-polarity current flow, owing to broken spatial-inversion and time-reversal symmetries. For a superconducting pair wavefunction to spontaneously break inversion symmetry implies the formation of Cooper pairs with nonzero momentum, yielding a nonuniform superconducting state. For singlet superconductors, the formation of such a (FFLO) state has long been understood to provide an escape from high field Pauli pair-breaking -- how and why such an effect might occur for equal spin pairing is an open question. Here we report the observation of a large SDE in bulk specimens of the three-dimensional heavy-fermion triplet superconductor UTe$_2$, with a peak diode efficiency of 33% at the high magnetic field strength of 33.6 T, representing the highest-field observation of the SDE to date. Crucially, this nonreciprocal transport is strictly confined to a narrow field regime where superconductivity coexists with a field-induced spin-polarized state near a first-order metamagnetic phase boundary. The absence of the SDE away from this slender phase-intersection argues strongly against purely extrinsic vortex pinning asymmetries. Instead, our findings indicate spontaneous inversion symmetry breaking accompanied by finite-momentum Cooper pairing. We model this behavior using a phenomenological Ginzburg-Landau theory, demonstrating that the intertwining of magnetic and triplet superconducting orders can stabilize an odd-parity FFLO state.

### Paper [329]
Title: Nano-Nb Superconducting 3D Josephson Junctions Arrays in Cu-Normal Matrix: Superconducting Phase Coherence and Quantum Fluctuations Properties
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36482
Authors: R. L. de Almeida, J. Albino Aguiar, C. A. C. Passos, S. K. Sharma, J. A. H. Coaquira
Abstract: We investigate the superconducting behavior, phase coherence, and quantum fluctuation properties of three-dimensional arrays of nanometric Nb Josephson junctions embedded in a normal Cu matrix ($\text{Cu-}x\text{ wt}\%\text{Nb}$, $x = 3, 5, 15,$ and $20$), with emphasis on the $5$ and $15$ compositions. Magnetic properties were measured by dc magnetization as a function of temperature and magnetic field using a SQUID magnetometer and by ac susceptibility. Transport properties, including magnetoresistance, $V(I)$ curves, and the critical current $I_c(H,T)$, were measured at fields up to $90\text{ kOe}$. A micro-Hall sensor with ten transverse voltage bridges ($5.0 \times 0.2 \times 0.01\text{ }\mu\text{m}^3$) was used to calibrate the magnetic field actually applied to the sample. Magnetization and transport measurements reveal a double superconducting transition that depends on the applied field and current: $T_c(H)$, with $T_c(0) = 8.2\text{ K}$, is associated with the intragranular response of the Nb grains, whereas the lower $T_c(H)$ is associated with Josephson coupling between grains through the Cu matrix. The response combines that of bulk Nb with signatures of weakly coupled grains, consistent with a collection of superconducting grains embedded in a nonsuperconducting matrix. The characteristic fields $H_{c1} = 400.03\text{ Oe}$ and $H_{c2} = 15\text{ kOe}$ (grains), and $H_{c1j} = 99.93\text{ Oe}$ and $H_{c2j} = 199.25\text{ Oe}$ (intergranular coupling), were determined. The critical current $I_c(H,T)$ exhibits a central maximum at low fields, followed by a long tail and small oscillations with a period of $\Delta H \approx 24\text{ Oe}$, attributed to quantum interference between coupled grains. At higher fields, magnetic flux gradients destroy phase coherence and randomize the phase $\theta$ of the order parameter $\psi = |\psi|e^{i\theta}$, suppressing the oscillations.

### Paper [330]
Title: The Flexoelectric Twist: Persistence of Heavy Fermion Picture in Small-Angle Twisted Bilayer Graphene
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38305
Authors: Qirong Yao, Ka Cheuk Fan, Tianyu Qiao, Hao Shi, Jiangchang Zheng, Kenji Watanabe, Takashi Taniguchi, Xi Dai, Berthold J\"ack
Abstract: Twisted bilayer graphene (TBG) near the first magic angle ($\theta\approx1.05^{\circ}$) hosts topological flat bands that provide a fertile ground for exploring strongly correlated phases. At smaller twist angles ($\theta<1^{\circ}$), lattice relaxation profoundly alters the moir\'e landscape. The extent to which moir\'e lattice reconstruction at different length scales affects the electronic structure through electro-mechanical coupling remains unresolved. Using scanning tunneling microscopy on small-angle TBG devices with different twist angles $\theta\approx0.3-0.7^{\circ}$, we observe a pronounced $dI/dV$ peak at the AA site of the moir\'e lattice on top of a spectrum that is asymmetric about the charge neutrality point; we visualize lattice reconstruction at moir\'e lattice length scales (20-45\,nm); and we detect a dilation of the atomic lattice at the AA sites at Angstrom length scale. We demonstrate that the inclusion of moir\'e lattice reconstruction supports compact localized heavy fermion $f-$states at AA sites whereas the inclusion of atomic lattice dilation generates a strong flexo-electric response that breaks particle-hole symmetry, pushing the $f$-states down in energy. Consequently, these heavy fermion states energetically coexist with itinerant $c$-electrons at the AB/BA sites. Our spatially resolved spectroscopic measurements visualize this coexistence, confirming the persistence of the heavy fermion model in the small-angle regime $\theta<1^{\circ}$. Our findings show that flexo-electric responses can realize qualitatively distinct electronic landscapes in moir\'e materials beyond the single-particle picture. Looking ahead, small-angle TBG could prove an ideal platform to explore the interacting large-$N$ Kondo lattice problem with high tunability of the $f-$occupation and $c-$density through twist-angle and electrostatic back gates, respectively.

### Paper [331]
Title: Fluctuation-dissipation relation in branching particle systems
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39624
Authors: Tom Michoel
Abstract: A fluctuation-dissipation relation (FDR) for branching particle systems is derived that reveals how fluctuations in clonal populations originating from a single ancestor encode both population growth and the dissipative structure of the single-particle dynamics. For particles whose internal states evolve according to a stationary Markov process, the FDR holds in both equilibrium and nonequilibrium stationary states. It yields an upper bound on the spectral gap that is constrained by the magnitude of clonal fluctuations and implies that clonal dynamics is asymptotically dominated by the slowest modes of the single-particle dynamics. This provides a theoretical explanation for the persistence of clonal memory and for experimentally observed clonal cofluctuations between variables with zero covariance in the single-particle stationary state. In nonequilibrium stationary states, the FDR predicts oscillatory clonal fluctuations, suggesting a route to measuring nonequilibrium fluxes in proliferating cell populations without single-cell tracking.

### Paper [332]
Title: Kelvin-Wave Multiplication by Sound Emission
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39909
Authors: Weican Yang
Abstract: Kelvin waves are bending excitations of a quantized vortex. We identify a subsonic decay channel in which one Kelvin quantum emits a phonon and splits into two lower-energy Kelvin quanta, reducing the Kelvin-wave energy while increasing the number of quanta. For sound wavelengths much longer than the Kelvin wavelengths, rotational symmetry fixes the leading shear contribution to the emission amplitude in terms of the released Kelvin energy. This contribution is odd in the phonon momentum along the vortex. Microscopic calculations support the relation. Coherently seeded Gross--Pitaevskii evolution in a reflecting cavity demonstrates phase-controlled Kelvin gain, with increased mean-square vortex displacement and reduced mean-square slope.

### Paper [333]
Title: New horizons for inhomogeneous quenches and Floquet CFT
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2404.07884
Authors: Hanzhi Jiang, M\'ark Mezei
Abstract: A fruitful avenue in investigating out-of-equilibrium quantum many-body systems is to abruptly change their Hamiltonian and study the subsequent evolution of their quantum state. If this is done once, the setup is called a quench, while if it is done periodically, it is called Floquet driving. We consider the solvable setup of a two-dimensional CFT driven by Hamiltonians built out of conformal symmetry generators: in this case, the quantum dynamics can be understood using two-dimensional geometry. We investigate how the dynamics is reflected in the holographic dual three-dimensional spacetime and find new horizons. We argue that bulk operators behind the new horizons are reconstructable by virtue of modular flow.

### Paper [334]
Title: Coexistence of broadband conduction and amplitude-driven topological confinement in synthetic acoustic matter
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2408.16801
Authors: Mathieu Padlewski, Maxime Volery, Romain Fleury, Herv\'e Lissek, Xinxin Guo
Abstract: Nonlinear and topological wave phenomena offer new opportunities for robust wave manipulation. Existing experimental platforms have largely focused on on-site (Kerr-like) nonlinearities within tight-binding frameworks, leaving the regime of programmable nonlinear inter-site coupling in far-field scattering systems essentially unexplored. Here we demonstrate a programmable acoustic metamaterial that exhibits amplitude-driven topological confinement of sound while simultaneously maintaining broadband linear transmission across the same frequency range. The system consists of an open acoustic waveguide coupled to a chain of actively controlled electroacoustic resonators enabling tunable nonlinear and nonlocal interactions. Using a classical wave-scattering model we show that increasing the excitation amplitude drives a robust topological interface mode localized deep within the structure, with the confinement strengthening continuously with drive rather than at a finite threshold. The predictions are verified experimentally using a fully programmable metacrystal composed of individually controlled acoustic resonators, enabling real-time manipulation of coupling parameters. The mode's topological origin is established by symmetry: it survives coupling disorder beyond 50% of nominal but collapses when a delay between adjacent couplings is introduced, effectively breaking the protecting chiral symmetry. These results establish a versatile platform for studying nonlinear topological wave physics and for designing adaptive acoustic metamaterials.

### Paper [335]
Title: The entanglement membrane in 2d CFT: reflected entropy, RG flow, and information velocity
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2411.16542
Authors: Hanzhi Jiang, M\'ark Mezei, Julio Virrueta
Abstract: The time evolution of entanglement entropy in generic chaotic many-body systems has an effective description in terms of a minimal membrane, characterised by a tension function. For 2d CFTs, a degenerate tension function reproduces several results regarding the dynamics of the entropy; this stands in contrast to higher dimensions where the tension is non-degenerate. In this paper we use holography to show that, in order to correctly capture the reflected entropy in 2d CFT, one needs to add an additional degree of freedom to the membrane description. Furthermore, we show that the conventional non-degenerate membrane tension function emerges upon introducing a relevant deformation of the CFT, dual to a planar BTZ black hole with scalar hair and with an interior Kasner universe. Finally, we also study the membrane description for reflected entropy and information velocity arXiv:1908.06993 in higher dimensions.

### Paper [336]
Title: Models of liquid-sample confinement for nanoscale NMR
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2502.13578
Authors: Santiago Oviedo-Casado, Daniel Cohen, Allan Jos\'ue Gonz\'alez-Villalobos, Javier Cerrillo
Abstract: Diffusion is a prominent source of noise affecting nuclear magnetic resonance at the nanometer scale (nano-NMR), preventing high-resolution studies of unpolarized liquid samples and hindering the development of nanofluid devices. Actively managing diffusion noise through sample confinement is likely to unveil alternative noise sources that have so far been disregarded, as they occur in longer timescales and consequently are masked by diffusion. These secondary noise sources could diminish the advantages provided by sample confinement, but they may also yield valuable information about the behavior of the fluid sample and its interactions with the confinement device. In this article, we study in detail two previously unaccounted noise models for confined nano-NMR, namely, surface interactions and porosity, and discuss their implications for typical nano-NMR experiments.

### Paper [337]
Title: Solving Stochastic Inverse Problems in Embedding Space
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2506.11796
Authors: Elias Najarro, Nicolas Bessone, Sebastian Risi
Abstract: Solving an inverse problem consists in finding the unknown parameters of a system from observations. This task is particularly challenging when observations have a strong stochastic component, producing diverse yet equivalent patterns. Self-organizing systems are a notable case: simple local rules can generate complex stochastic patterns. Many natural systems rely on such processes, making self-organization central to understanding natural phenomena. Inverse methods based on direct comparison of observed values struggle in this setting, as such comparisons cannot capture feature similarities between variable outcomes; as a result, the loss fails to meaningfully inform the optimizer. In this work, we show that the difficulty posed by stochasticity in observable patterns can be overcome by reformulating the inverse problem in an embedding space. By mapping stochastic patterns onto an invariant embedding space, where equivalent patterns have similar representations, we can effectively recover unknown causal parameters without the need for handcrafted objective functions or heuristics. We show that embedding models trained on large image datasets produce robust representations that capture these invariances. We evaluate the method on three self-organizing systems: a physical, a biological, and a social one; namely, a reaction-diffusion system, a model of embryonic development, and an agent-based model of social segregation. We show that the method reliably recovers parameters despite stochasticity in the pattern outcomes. We further apply the method to real biological patterns, highlighting its potential as a tool for both theorists and experimentalists to investigate the dynamics underlying complex stochastic pattern formation.

### Paper [338]
Title: Free mutual information and higher-point OTOCs
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.13406
Authors: Shreya Vardhan, Jinzhao Wang
Abstract: We introduce a quantity called the free mutual information (FMI), adapted from concepts in free probability theory, as a new physical measure of quantum chaos. This quantity captures the spreading of a time-evolved operator in the space of all possible operators on the Hilbert space, which is doubly exponential in the number of degrees of freedom. It thus provides a finer notion of operator spreading than the well-understood phenomenon of operator growth in physical space. We derive two central results which apply in any physical system: first, an explicit "Coulomb gas" formula for the FMI of two observables $A(t)$ and $B$ in terms of the eigenvalues of the product operator $A(t)B$; and second, a general relation expressing the FMI as a weighted sum of all higher-point out-of-time-ordered correlators (OTOCs). This second result provides a precise information-theoretic interpretation for the higher-point OTOCs as collectively quantifying operator ergodicity and the approach to freeness. This physical interpretation is particularly useful in light of recent progress in experimentally measuring higher-point OTOCs. We identify universal behaviours of the FMI and higher-point OTOCs across a variety of chaotic systems, including random unitary circuits and chaotic spin chains, which indicate that spreading in the doubly exponential operator space is a generic feature of quantum many-body chaos. At the same time, the non-generic behavior of the FMI in various non-chaotic systems, including certain unitary designs, shows that there are cases where an operator spreads in physical space but remains localized in operator space. The FMI is thus a sharper diagnostic of chaos than the standard 4-point OTOC.

### Paper [339]
Title: BCS superconductivity in the presence of wave dark matter
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.22892
Authors: YeolLin ChoeJo, Yechan Kim, Hye-Sung Lee, Jiheon Lee, Jaeok Yi
Abstract: As particle physics and condensed matter physics continue to expand their boundaries, emerging intersections between the two fields suggest well-motivated frontiers for physics models. In particular, dark matter, which is cosmologically predicted to form the background to our ordinary matter, can affect the physics of materials in a fundamental sense. In this work, we investigate the scenario in which relic ultralight wave dark matter alters the superconductor Hamiltonian via a sinusoidal source term. The Yukawa interaction with the scalar dark matter changes the effective electron mass, inducing periodic perturbations of macroscopic observables such as the superconducting order parameter, whose size could be important in future experiments. We provide here the actual calculations for this scenario on the superconducting resonators, calculating the modulation size depending on the dark matter parameters.

### Paper [340]
Title: Continuous SUN (Stable, Unique, and Novel) Metric for Generative Modeling of Inorganic Crystals
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.12405
Authors: Masahiro Negishi, Hyunsoo Park, Kinga O. Mastej, Aron Walsh
Abstract: To address pressing scientific challenges such as climate change, increasingly sophisticated generative models are being developed to efficiently sample the large chemical space of potential functional materials. The proliferation of these models has necessitated the establishment of rigorous evaluation metrics. While uniqueness (U), novelty (N), and stability (S) of samples serve as standard metrics, their current formulations show several limitations. U and N rely on binary comparisons of crystals, rendering them dependent on heuristic thresholds, incapable of quantifying the degree of similarity, sensitive to atomic coordinate perturbations, and not invariant to sample permutation. Similarly, the binary assessment of S risks a premature exclusion of marginally unstable yet potentially novel candidates. These limitations are addressed by making the aforementioned metrics continuous. Furthermore, we integrate them into a unified metric ``continuous SUN" (cSUN), which offers a smoother score distribution and greater tunability than the conventional binary SUN metric. Experimental results demonstrate that our continuous metrics provide granular insights into sample distributions and secondarily serve as a soft screening score for selecting samples for further analysis. Finally, the use of cSUN as a reward signal in reinforcement learning is explored, showing that its adjustable weighting scheme effectively enhances sample diversity and convergence to a better optima in a Chemeleon2 case study.

### Paper [341]
Title: Unlocking n-alk-1-ynes Conformers: Quantum "Trigger Finger" versus "Stiff Joint" Conformations
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.02470
Authors: Ioan B\^aldea
Abstract: Molecular conformation in n-alk-1-ynes (CnA) is conventionally simplified to an all-planar structure. We report a comprehensive quantum chemical analysis revealing two near-isoenergetic rotamers at the acetylenic terminus: planar (C$_s$) and skewed (C$_1$). The high, symmetric rotational energy barrier ($\approx 150$\,meV) arises from unique steric relief near the $\mathrm{sp}$ center coupled with electronic stabilization of C$_1$. This creates a unique kinetic profile: a Quantum ``Trigger Finger'' ($\alpha$ rotation) that enforces an $\approx 50\%:\,50\%$ $\mathrm{C}_s/\mathrm{C}_1$ ensemble, sharply contrasting with the thermodynamically biased ``Stiff Joint'' ($\delta$ rotation) of the alkyl chain. This structural degeneracy necessitates ensemble averaging for spectroscopic data interpretation, while the slow interconversion permits kinetic trapping and intentional conformer enrichment during synthesis and molecular junction fabrication. Our work redefines the alkyne anchor, providing a blueprint for accurate interpretation of spectroscopic data and achieving conformational control in molecular electronics.

### Paper [342]
Title: Time Evolution of Multi-Party Entanglement Signals
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.16729
Authors: Vijay Balasubramanian, Hanzhi Jiang, Simon F. Ross
Abstract: We study the real-time dynamics of multi-party entanglement signals in chaotic quantum many-body systems including but not necessarily restricted to holographic conformal field theories. We find that scrambling dynamics generates multiparty entanglement with rich structure including: (a) qualitatively different dynamical behaviours for different signals, likely reflecting different dynamics for different kinds of entanglement patterns, (b) discontinuities indicating dynamical phase transitions in the entanglement structure, (c) transient and non-monotonic multiparty entanglement, and (d) periods during which the extensive entanglement of some regions is entirely multipartite. Our main technical tool is the membrane theory of entanglement dynamics.

### Paper [343]
Title: Neural network approximation of regularized density functionals
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.18512
Authors: Andre Laestadius, Mathias Oster
Abstract: We study neural-network approximation of density functionals in a Banach-space setting. Since the exact continuum functionals are discontinuous in the natural density topology, we apply $p$-Moreau-Yosida regularization. We construct finite-rank ReLU networks that preserve convexity and nonnegativity, lie below the regularized functional, and approximate it uniformly on compact sets. For energies obtained by minimization over compact density sets, we account exactly for the energy shift caused by regularization and bound the remaining error by the localization on these compact sets and network approximation errors. We apply this framework to canonical and grand-canonical density-functional theory at fixed particle number and fixed mean particle number, respectively. We obtain uniform energy convergence on norm-compact classes of external potentials, including molecular Coulomb potentials. We illustrate our results through numerical experiments on a simple model system.

### Paper [344]
Title: Neuromodulation-inspired gated associative memory networks: extended memory retrieval and emergent multistability
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.13859
Authors: Daiki Goto, Hector Manuel Lopez Rios, Monika Scholz, Suriyanarayanan Vaikuntanathan
Abstract: Classical autoassociative memory models have been central to understanding emergent computations in recurrent neural circuits across diverse biological contexts. However, they typically neglect neuromodulatory agents that are known to strongly shape memory capacity and stability. Here we introduce a minimal, biophysically motivated associative memory network in which neuromodulator-like signals are modeled by a self-adaptive, activity-dependent gating mechanism. Using many-body simulations and dynamical mean-field theory, we show that such gating fundamentally reorganizes the attractor structure: the network bypasses the classical spin-glass transition, maintaining robust, high-overlap retrieval far beyond the standard critical capacity. Mechanistically, when the gates act as fully open or fully closed switches, the gate stabilizes transient "ghost" remnants of stored patterns even far above the Hopfield limit, converting a single memory fixed point attractor into a cluster of fixed points. The retrieved state is set by the initial overlap rather than quantized to the stored pattern. This graded dependence may be exploited for input-driven computation with persistent activity. These results establish that neuromodulation-like gating alone, without modifying synaptic weights or introducing explicit higher-order couplings, can dramatically expand memory capacity, eliminate catastrophic breakdown, and reshape a discrete attractor landscape into a rich continuum, suggesting a general and biologically accessible route to enhanced computational capabilities in neural circuits and neuromorphic architectures.

### Paper [345]
Title: Madelung hydrodynamics of spin-orbit coupling: action principles, currents, and correlations
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.10698
Authors: Cesare Tronci
Abstract: We exploit the variational and Hamiltonian structures of quantum hydrodynamics with spin to unfold the correlation and torque mechanisms accompanying spin-orbit coupling (SOC) in electronic motion. Using Hamilton's action principle for the Pauli equation, we isolate SOC-induced quantum forces that act on the orbital Madelung--Bohm trajectories and complement the usual force terms known to appear in quantum hydrodynamics with spin. While the latter spin-hydrodynamic forces relate to the quantum geometric tensor (QGT), SOC-induced orbital forces originate from a particular current operator that contributes prominently to the spin current. This distinction between force terms reveals two fundamentally different mechanisms generating quantum spin-orbit correlations. Leveraging the Hamiltonian structure of the hydrodynamic system, we also elucidate spin transport features such as the correlation-induced quantum torques and the current shift in the spin Hall effect. This Hall shift leads to a new definition of the transport spin current thereby addressing an open question in spintronics. Finally, we illustrate the framework via the Madelung--Rashba equations for planar SOC configurations and propose a particle closure for possible numerical implementations.

### Paper [346]
Title: Inferring Stochastic Dynamics Reveals Atypicality and Irreversibility in Social Change
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.17772
Authors: Youngkyoung Bae, Hajime Shimao, Seungwoong Ha, Luna Yang, David Wolpert
Abstract: Human social systems bear striking similarities, yet similar conditions can give rise to markedly different historical trajectories. A central challenge for quantitative social science is therefore to use time-series data to identify features common to the evolution of different social systems without obscuring the variation distinguishing individual histories. Here, we address this challenge by fitting stochastic differential equations (SDEs) to ensembles of observed trajectories through spaces of socially relevant variables. Importantly, the SDE allows the average tendency and variability of change to depend on the current state of the system. This flexibility allows us to use the SDE to analyze myriad aspects of social dynamics. Specifically, the fitted SDEs identify regions of the state space where stochastic variability is greater, allowing a broader range of plausible subsequent changes. The fitted SDE also allows us to quantify the dynamical irreversibility of observed trajectories and how that irreversibility depends on the values of the social variables. In addition, we show how the SDEs can be used to identify statistically atypical transitions in individual trajectories, indicative of exogenous perturbations impacting the associated society. We apply these tools to three domains: modern political economy, quantitative history, and cultural analytics. This reveals state-dependent change in the organization of modern political-economic systems; a broad but stochastic tendency toward increasing social complexity in historical societies; and time-dependent reorganization of lexical dynamics in English. Looking forward, we argue that our framework provides a set of powerful quantitative tools that can be used to investigate a broader range of longitudinal social phenomena.

### Paper [347]
Title: Modular Krylov Complexity as a Boundary Probe of Area Operators and Entanglement Islands
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.02675
Authors: Niloofar Vardian
Abstract: We show that the area operator of a quantum extremal surface can be reconstructed directly from boundary dynamics without reference to bulk geometry. Our approach combines the operator-algebra quantum error-correction (OAQEC) structure of AdS/CFT with modular Krylov complexity. Using Lanczos coefficients of boundary modular dynamics, we extract the spectrum of the modular Hamiltonian restricted to the algebra of the entanglement wedge and isolate its central contribution, which is identified with the area operator. The construction is purely boundary-based and applies to superpositions of semiclassical geometries as well. As a prospective application, we discuss island formation and the Page transition in evaporating black holes using boundary modular evolution, bypassing any bulk extremization. More broadly, our results establish modular Krylov complexity as a probe of emergent spacetime geometry, providing a new route to accessing black hole interiors from boundary quantum dynamics.

### Paper [348]
Title: MSP-LLM: A Structured Large Language Model Framework for Inorganic Material Synthesis Planning
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.07543
Authors: Heewoong Noh, Gyoung S. Na, Namkyeong Lee, Chanyoung Park
Abstract: Material synthesis planning (MSP) remains a fundamental and underexplored bottleneck in AI-driven materials discovery, as it requires not only identifying suitable precursor materials but also designing coherent sequences of synthesis operations to realize a target material. Although several AI-based approaches have been proposed to address isolated subtasks of MSP, a methodology for solving the entire MSP task has yet to be established. We propose MSP-LLM, a structured LLM-based framework that formulates MSP as a two-stage process composed of two constituent subproblems: precursor prediction (PP) and synthesis operation prediction (SOP). Our approach introduces a discrete material class as an intermediate decision variable that organizes both tasks into a chemically consistent decision chain. For SOP, we further incorporate hierarchical precursor types as synthesis-relevant inductive biases and employ precursor constraint factorization that preserves precursor-related information in the autoregressive decoding state. Extensive experiments show that MSP-LLM consistently outperforms existing methods on both PP and SOP, as well as on the MSP task, demonstrating an effective and scalable framework with practical potential for autonomous MSP.

### Paper [349]
Title: Entanglement in quantum spin chains is strictly finite at any temperature
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.13386
Authors: Ainesh Bakshi, Soonwon Choi, Sa\'ul Pilatowsky-Cameo
Abstract: Entanglement is the hallmark of quantum physics, yet its characterization in interacting many-body systems at thermal equilibrium remains one of the most important challenges in quantum statistical physics. We prove that the Gibbs state of any quantum spin chain can be exactly decomposed into a mixture of matrix product states with a bond dimension that is independent of the system size, at any nonzero temperature. As a consequence, the Schmidt number, arguably the most stringent measure of bipartite entanglement, is strictly finite for thermal states, even in the thermodynamic limit. Our decomposition is explicit and is accompanied by an efficient classical algorithm to sample the resulting matrix product states.

### Paper [350]
Title: Unified Probe of Quantum Chaos and Ergodicity from Hamiltonian Learning
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.04486
Authors: Nik O. Gjonbalaj, Christian Kokail, Susanne F. Yelin, Soonwon Choi
Abstract: Developing measures of quantum ergodicity and chaos stands as a foundational task in the study of quantum many-body systems. In this work, we propose metrics for these effects based on Hamiltonian learning that unify multiple advantages of existing metrics. In particular, we show how ergodicity and chaos improve the robustness of Hamiltonian learning to small errors and furthermore demonstrate that this robustness can be used as a metric for such phenomena. We analytically and numerically show that our metrics not only distinguish between integrable and ergodic regimes in various spin chains but also quantify chaos and ergodicity, allowing us to locate regions of parameter space displaying maximal ergodicity and maximal sensitivity to local perturbations. Our approach not only provides conceptual ways to study quantum chaos and ergodicity but also presents viable experimental methods for quantum simulators.

### Paper [351]
Title: A Mathematical Framework for Linear Response Theory for Nonautonomous Systems
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.19509
Authors: Stefano Galatolo, Valerio Lucarini
Abstract: Linear Response theory aims to predict how added forcing alters the statistical properties of a system. This has been predominantly studied for autonomous dynamical systems, yet many systems in the physical, natural, and social sciences are inherently nonautonomous. In such settings, one would like to understand how the system's time-dependent statistical properties change when additional infinitesimal forcings are applied. This question is of clear practical relevance, but from a rigorous mathematical viewpoint it has been addressed only for a few specific classes of systems and perturbations. Here we provide a rigorous linear response theory for a rather general class of deterministic and random nonautonomous systems satisfying a specific set of assumptions that are the counterpart of the standard assumptions used in the autonomous setting. A central ingredient is rapid loss of memory along the nonautonomous evolution.
  Our main strategy is to reformulate the sequential dynamics as a fixed-point problem for a global transfer operator acting on an extended sequence space of measures. This yields explicit and readily implementable causal response formulas for predicting the effect of small perturbations on time-dependent statistical states.
  We illustrate the theory on two representative classes: sequential compositions of expanding maps and sequential compositions of noisy random maps.
  We also establish linear response for reflected Euler-Maruyama discretizations of dissipative nonautonomous stochastic differential equations with nondegenerate additive Gaussian noise, on fixed compact domains. As a physical application, we study the response of a Ghil-Sellers climate energy balance model, discretized in latitude and time to a time-dependent perturbation of the greenhouse parameter.

### Paper [352]
Title: Absolute Experimental Measurement of Intrinsic Helicity in Elementary Plasmonic Nanostructures
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.26058
Authors: Malo B\'ezard, Simon Garrigou, J\'er\'emie B\'eal, Andreas Horrer, Yves Auad, Hugo Louren\c{c}o-Martins, Davy G\'erard, Mathieu Kociak
Abstract: Helicity is a universal quantity describing the internal rotation of an object or a field. Whether it characterizes fundamental properties such as the spin of elementary particles or leads to practical consequences such as the toxicity or harmlessness of chiral molecules, defining and measuring it is essential. However, this remains ambiguous in the case of chiral photonic systems such as plasmonic nanoparticles or photonic metasurfaces, where contrary to common knowledge, the observation of circularly polarized dependent optical properties is not a relevant measure for the helicity. We demonstrate experimentally and theoretically that the helicity can be rigorously defined and measured in a nanoplasmonics system emitting circularly polarized light after excitation in the near-field by a focused electron beam. In the case of a model system composed of two plasmonic dipoles (Born-Kuhn systems), we show that the helicity of photonic modes takes on a very intuitive form and can be simply measured by symmetrizing the geometry of excitation and detection. The method could be extended to a variety of chiral photonic systems, whose locally enhanced properties make them promising for the engineering of local chirality.

### Paper [353]
Title: All-electron Dynamical Bethe-Salpeter Equation for Extended Systems with Atom-centered Orbital Basis
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.08350
Authors: Ruiyi Zhou, Songrui Liu, Jianhang Xu, Yi Yao, Yosuke Kanai
Abstract: Solving Bethe-Salpeter equation (BSE) for the two-particle Green's function is the most widely used approach for taking into account the particle-hole (exciton) interaction in electronic excitation in the context of the many-body theory based on Green's function. In BSE calculations, the static approximation to the screened Coulomb interaction kernel is commonly employed. However, when the excitonic character is significant as typically indicated by a large exciton binding energy, dynamical screening effects become non-negligible, rendering the static approximation questionable. Because of the large computational cost due to the dense Brillouin zone integration necessary for convergence, solving the dynamical BSE for extended systems remains a significant challenge, especially when combined with GW calculation for the calculation of quasi-particle energies. In this work, we formulate the plane-wave based effective dielectric function method [Zhang, et al., Phys. Rev. B 107, 235205 (2023)] for the dynamical BSE calculation using atom-centered orbitals as basis functions. We implement this approach in our recently developed all-electron numerical atom-centered orbital (NAO) implementation of BSE@GW [Zhou, et. al. J. Chem. Theory Comput. 21, 291 (2025)] for extended systems. We validate our all-electron NAO-based implementation of the dynamical BSE method, and we then discuss its realistic application to molecular crystal of naphthalene by performing the dynamical BSE@G0W0 calculation.

### Paper [354]
Title: Local correlations in long-range dual-unitary kicked Hamiltonian chains
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13857
Authors: Vladimir Al. Osipov, Marc Cedric Spyra, Jana Carolina Schumann, Thomas Guhr, Boris Gutkin
Abstract: Many-body Floquet models with exact space--time duality, such as the kicked Ising chain, provide paradigmatic examples of dual-unitary dynamics. The requirement of exact space--time duality is, however, highly restrictive and, in its standard formulation, permits only nearest-neighbor interactions. We demonstrate that the latter constraint can be avoided, still resulting in exactly solvable models. In particular, we construct a broad class of dual-unitary kicked spin chains with long-range interactions. We show that, for an interaction range $r$, local two-point connected correlation functions propagate along the edges of the light cone, $|n|=r|t|$, and can be evaluated analytically for local observables. We illustrate our general statement by analytic calculation of the connected correlation function for a kicked Ising chain with next-to-next-nearest-neighbor interactions.

### Paper [355]
Title: Detecting Historical Turning Points in Italian Media: A Complex Systems Approach to a Diachronic News Corpus
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14348
Authors: Dario Zarcone, Salvatore Miccich\`e, David Sanchez
Abstract: Introduction: The increasing availability of large-scale textual corpora has opened new possibilities for data-driven, quantitative approaches to historical analysis using Natural Language Processing (NLP). However, diachronic corpora with historical relevance from the pre-digital era remain scarce and often incomplete.
  Methods: We present a quantitative approach to historical analysis based on the reconstruction and exploration of a diachronic corpus of around 600,000 articles from the Italian newspaper "La Repubblica", covering all the articles published from the first of January 1985 to the 31st of December 2000 - a period of major political, social, and geopolitical change in Italy and globally. Using NLP techniques, we analyze the text at both lexical and semantic levels; we then apply tools from complex systems and statistical physics to trace shifts in media discourse over time.
  Results: The word usage in the corpus is bursty, with a heavy-tailed distribution of Fano factors that is robust across timescales. Changepoints in word salience and the block structure of semantic similarity allow us to detect key transition periods, such as the transition from the First Republic to the Second Republic in Italy, or major international conflicts like the Gulf War or the Kosovo War, without relying on prior labeling. Periods of war correspond to lower entropy in the semantic space, indicating a more focused discourse.
  Discussion: The results show how combining computational linguistics with ideas from complex systems can offer new quantitative insight into historical changes, opening up new paths for studying the dynamics of media and society through large-scale textual data.

### Paper [356]
Title: Fluctuations of the Sherrington-Kirkpatrick free energy at critical temperature
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.02172
Authors: Hang Du, Brice Huang
Abstract: We consider the Sherrington-Kirkpatrick spin glass model at the critical inverse temperature $\beta = 1$ with zero external field. We prove that the free energy $F_N = F_{N,\beta=1}$ of this model has variance
  \[
  \mathrm{Var}(F_N) = \frac16 \log N + O(1)\,,
  \] confirming a physics prediction of Aspelmeier \cite{aspelmeier2008free}, and that the centered and scaled $F_N$ satisfies a Gaussian CLT. We also identify the critical two-replica overlap scale, proving
  \[
  \mathbb{E} \langle R_{1,2}^2\rangle \asymp N^{-2/3}\,,
  \] as conjectured by Talagrand \cite{talagrand2011mean2}, together with a uniform exponential moment bound for $N^{1/3} |R_{1,2}|$. The key input is a critical reweighted moment method, in the spirit of the ``small subgraph conditioning'' technique from probabilistic combinatorics, but capable of capturing diverging fluctuations. Through this reweighting, we relate the critical SK model to the BBP critical edge, which determines the overlap and fluctuation scales.

### Paper [357]
Title: The large $N$ vector model with angular velocity
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.31151
Authors: Justin R. David, Srijan Kumar
Abstract: We study the free energy of a critical vector model at large $N$ on $S^{1}\times S^{2}$ with an angular velocity $\hat\mu$ without the singlet constraint. We study the model for which the large $N$ dynamics is controlled by the uniform saddle point of the auxiliary field arising in the Hubbard-Stratanovich transformation. The leading high-temperature behaviour is determined analytically both as an expansion about $\hat\mu r=0$ and $\hat\mu^{2}r^{2}=1$ where $r$ is the radius of the sphere. We supplement the analytic results with a numerical analysis that agrees with both the analytical expansions in their respective regimes and smoothly interpolates between them. The leading high-temperature contribution to the free energy develops a pole at $\hat\mu^{2}r^{2}=1$, in agreement with expectations from the thermal effective field theory. Its residue coincides with that of the massless free theory. Sub-leading terms, however, exhibit non-analytic dependence on the angular velocity and distinguish the critical fixed-point result from the free theory answer. The residue at the pole can also be obtained by placing the model on the pp-wave geometry. We show that the residue agrees with that obtained from the direct computation. The free energy of the model connects the non-trivial fixed point of the $O(N)$ model at $\hat\mu r=0$ to its free fixed point at $\hat\mu^2r^2=1$.

### Paper [358]
Title: Odd-viscosity-induced instability in shear flows
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01566
Authors: Yonatan Messica, Igor Gornyi, Dmitri B. Gutman
Abstract: Odd viscosity is a nondissipative component of the viscosity tensor that arises in fluids with broken time-reversal symmetry. We show that, despite being energy-conserving, odd viscosity can qualitatively alter hydrodynamic stability by generating exponentially growing modes that are absent in conventional fluids. For plane Poiseuille flow, we derive the odd-viscous generalization of the Orr--Sommerfeld--Squire equations and find a new instability that first emerges for streamwise-uniform modes and extends to oblique modes. The instability exploits the non-normality of the linearized Navier--Stokes operator: conventional fluids support transiently growing disturbances through the lift-up mechanism, while odd viscosity closes a feedback loop between wall-normal velocity and vorticity, converting these disturbances into self-sustaining exponentially growing modes. This mechanism lowers the linear instability threshold by nearly two orders of magnitude, from $\mathrm{Re}_c \approx 5772$ to $\mathrm{Re}_c \approx 62$. More generally, we show that an energy-conserving coupling can destabilize a linearly stable system by sustaining the growth of otherwise transiently growing disturbances. Such a coupling thus triggers energy growth without directly supplying energy, by enabling continuous energy extraction from the background state. This establishes a mechanism for controlling stability in non-normal systems without direct energy dissipation or injection.

### Paper [359]
Title: Matrix Holography on an Optical Lattice
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05613
Authors: Robin L\"owenberg, Julian Sonner
Abstract: We propose an analog quantum-simulation protocol based on a lattice of atomic ensembles and time-averaged Floquet dynamics to construct the four-body potentials that typically appear in bosonic BFSS-like matrix quantum mechanics. In contrast to gate-based digital implementations requiring deep circuits, our approach generates the target model through a fixed set of control stages per Floquet cycle, keeping the number of consecutive unitaries constant with respect to the matrix size $N$. The principal scaling cost in our construction is given by the frequency range required to control the growing lattice. We show that this cost grows at most as $\mathcal{O}(N^3)$, giving a polynomial scaling route to large$-N$, attractive to experimental implementation. Additionally, we demonstrate that the coupling parameter can be tuned beyond $\mathcal{O}(1)$, enabling the simulation of strong-coupling physics necessary for holographic phases.

### Paper [360]
Title: Effective atom-dimer bands of three-body lattice systems at strong coupling: exact critical mass ratios and a statistics-quasimomentum duality
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.33576
Authors: Mikhail V. Dolgopolov
Abstract: We study three particles on the two-dimensional square lattice with contact interactions of infinite strength, in every sector of total quasimomentum, for three statistics: identical bosons, two identical fermions with a distinguishable third particle, and two identical bosons with a distinguishable third particle. The atom-dimer sector associated with the infinitely degenerate eigenvalue of the pair-contact operator is analysed on the whole Brillouin zone. A Feshbach-Schur reduction gives explicit effective Hamiltonians built from the Dirichlet Laplacian and a rank-four exchange operator, and a spectral transfer theorem with explicit error bounds identifies the atom-dimer threshold of the three-body operator with that of the effective one; the eigenvalue counting function below the threshold is squeezed between those of the effective operator, so that the number of atom-dimer states bound uniformly in the coupling equals the number of bound states of the effective Hamiltonian. The threshold problem reduces to a four-by-four Hermitian matrix whose zero-energy limit is built from the two-point resistances of the square lattice and hence to a quartic polynomial. This yields the complete atom-dimer zones in closed form; the critical hopping ratio as an explicit function on the whole Brillouin zone with two-sided bounds and closed forms at all symmetry points; at most two atom-dimer states per sector with an explicit second threshold; exact onset laws of infinite order at the corner point and linear-over-logarithmic in the pure p-wave channel; and two exact dualities relating a change of statistics to a shift of quasimomentum by half a reciprocal-lattice vector. The reduction of the three-body eigenvalue problem to the lattice Skornyakov-Ter-Martirosyan equation is proved with an explicit isomorphism of eigenspaces.

### Paper [361]
Title: Beyond light cones: State preparation complexity in quantum spin glasses
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02166
Authors: Omar Al-Ghattas, David Gamarnik, Bobak T Kiani
Abstract: We introduce a technique for studying state preparation complexity in the quantum $p$-spin model on $n$ qubits, a disordered quantum spin system with random interactions on every set of $p$ qubits. We reduce the task of proving state preparation lower bounds to bounding the local profile complexity of the class, a quantity derived from the metric entropy of the set of vectors recording each state's expectation values against all weight-$p$ Pauli strings. We show that, for sufficiently large fixed $p$, any class with local profile complexity $O(n^2)$ remains separated from the ground-state energy by $\Omega(\sqrt n)$, a gap on the leading energy scale. When the profile complexity is $o(n^2)$, we prove the stronger conclusion that the class cannot outperform a benchmark class of simpler states at leading order. Product states always provide such a benchmark.
  Our framework strengthens known bounds and covers classes beyond the reach of previous methods. These include examples where circuit lightcones extend across the entire system, preventing an application of standard arguments. We obtain an $\Omega(n^2/\log n)$ gate lower bound for preparing near-ground states and depth--width tradeoffs, allowing arbitrary one- and two-qubit gates and arbitrarily many discardable ancillas. We also derive lower bounds on entanglement depth and matrix product state bond dimension, and energy obstructions for forward and reverse magic at every fixed level of Parham's magic hierarchy (arXiv:2504.19966). For Clifford circuits doped with $T$-gates, we obtain sharper energy comparisons using product stabilizer states as a more restrictive benchmark. The proof combines an adaptation of a nonsymmetric quantum de Finetti theorem (arXiv:1810.12197) with metric-entropy bounds for the Gaussian process indexed by the local profiles of states in the class.

### Paper [362]
Title: Constrained dissipation and the lifetime of quantum many-body scars
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22223
Authors: Weyner Ccuiro, Rosario Fazio, Alessandro Silva
Abstract: We investigate the fate of quantum many-body scars in the PXP model subject to Markovian dissipation. We show that when local jump operators respect the same symmetries of the Hamiltonian the full Liouvillian spectrum displays a tower of states with properties analogous to standard quantum scars: large overlaps with N\'eel states and equally spaced gaps in the imaginary part of the eigenvalues, set by a single frequency. Whether the long-time dynamics is dominated by scars is determined by the real parts of the eigenvalues, which are set by the ratio between the two relaxation rates $\gamma_{\pm}$ describing spin flips down-to-up or up-to-down respectively. For asymmetric dissipation ($\gamma_+ > \gamma_-$) the scarred eigenvalues have the smallest real parts: the resulting enhanced fidelity oscillations are further stabilized by staggered jump operators. In contrast, replacing the full projection operator in the dissipators with a partial one breaks the symmetries of the problem and is seen to destroy dissipative scars altogether.

### Paper [363]
Title: Compositional neural networks for multimodal X-ray and neutron scattering inversion
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29362
Authors: Valentin Munteanu, Alexander Hinderhofer, Dmitry Lapkin, Vladimir Starostin, Frank Schreiber
Abstract: X-ray and neutron scattering are indispensable for structural characterization across condensed-matter physics and materials science. Yet, because scattering experiments generally record intensities without phase information, distinct structures can yield similar scattering patterns, rendering the corresponding inverse problems non-unique. This ambiguity can be reduced by combining complementary measurements, but their integration into machine-learning frameworks remains challenging. Here we introduce a modular framework for probabilistic multi-measurement analysis based on prior-amortized flow matching. Single-observation posterior estimators are trained separately for individual observation types or physical probes and combined only at inference time through a compositional sampler. This late fusion approach allows pretrained single-observation models to be reused without dedicated joint retraining. As a case in point, we consider reflectometry, which provides depth-resolved, nanometer-scale information about thin films and interfaces. Our single-observation demonstrations span X-ray, time-of-flight neutron and polarized neutron reflectometry. In separate co-refinement studies, task-matched estimators enable homogeneous composition across multiple neutron contrasts and heterogeneous multimodal composition of X-ray and neutron measurements. Together, these results establish compositional flow matching as a modular approach to uncertainty-aware inversion and co-refinement, with potential applicability to other scattering problems in which multiple observations constrain a common physical structure while retaining measurement-specific parameters.

### Paper [364]
